05 Explainers, rankings & nostalgia
CORE CLICK DRIVER Completeness + comparison + recognition

BEST USE & EDITORIAL GUARDRAIL
Use when viewers value a structured breakdown, a comparison, or familiar memories. State the scope and criteria.
Avoid arbitrary rankings or pretending a list is exhaustive.



041 Every [X] Explained
      EXAMPLE Every Kind of Bicycle Brake Explained
      WHY IT MAY WORK Promises systematic coverage of a finite set.

042 Every [X] Ranked From Worst to Best
      EXAMPLE Every Desk Layout Ranked From Worst to Best
      WHY IT MAY WORK Comparison with an explicit ordering.

043 The [Number] Levels of [X]
      EXAMPLE The 5 Levels of Home Organization
      WHY IT MAY WORK Progression creates a mental roadmap.

044 The [X] Iceberg Explained
      EXAMPLE The Board-Game History Iceberg Explained
      WHY IT MAY WORK Moves from common knowledge to obscure layers.

045 The Entire History of [X]
      EXAMPLE The Entire History of the Paper Clip
      WHY IT MAY WORK Comprehensive chronological learning.

046 [X] vs [Y]: What’s the Real Difference?
      EXAMPLE Handmade vs Factory-Made: What’s the Real Difference?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Resolves a concrete comparison.

047 Do You Remember These [X]?
      EXAMPLE Do You Remember These Old Classroom Supplies?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Direct recall prompts nostalgia and self-testing.

048 Before [X], How Did People [Y]?
      EXAMPLE Before Refrigerators, How Did People Store Food?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Everyday problem with historical contrast.

049 The Most [Adjective] [X] Ever
      EXAMPLE The Most Unusual Public Benches Ever
      WHY IT MAY WORK Curated extremes encourage discovery.

050 [Number] [X] That [Unexpected Consequence]
      EXAMPLE 7 Tiny Design Changes That Saved Space
      WHY IT MAY WORK List structure plus a consequence.

06 Revelations, evidence & hidden mechanisms
CORE CLICK DRIVER Discovery + authority + proof

BEST USE & EDITORIAL GUARDRAIL
Use for explainable systems, overlooked evidence, or genuinely documented revelations. The video should show
how we know, not just claim secrecy.



051 The Truth About [X]
     EXAMPLE The Truth About Secondhand Furniture Prices
     WHY IT MAY WORK Signals myth correction and clarity.

052 The Secret Behind [Outcome]
     EXAMPLE The Secret Behind a Shop’s Loyal Customers
     WHY IT MAY WORK Makes a visible result the puzzle.

053 How [X] Actually Works
     EXAMPLE How a Public Library Actually Works
     WHY IT MAY WORK Promotes understanding of an unseen system.

054 The Evidence That Changed [X]
     EXAMPLE The Evidence That Changed the Museum’s Exhibit
     WHY IT MAY WORK Proof introduces a meaningful revision.

055 What [Experts/Insiders] Know About [X]
     EXAMPLE What Restorers Know About Old Paint
     WHY IT MAY WORK Expert lens promises useful depth.

056 The [Recording/Document] That Revealed [X]
     EXAMPLE The Letter That Revealed the Missing Plan
     WHY IT MAY WORK A concrete artifact supplies the hook.

057 What They Found Inside [X]
     EXAMPLE What They Found Inside the Sealed Cabinet
     WHY IT MAY WORK Discovery is centered on a physical object.

058 The Only Reason [X] Worked
     EXAMPLE The Only Reason the Fundraiser Worked
     WHY IT MAY WORK Explores a surprisingly decisive condition.

059 What [X] Gets Wrong About [Y]
     EXAMPLE What Common Advice Gets Wrong About Learning
     WHY IT MAY WORK Challenges an accepted explanation.

060 The Parts of [X] You Never Get to See
     EXAMPLE The Parts of a Theater You Never Get to See
     WHY IT MAY WORK Backstage access opens a new perspective.

07 Time, legacy & cultural change
CORE CLICK DRIVER Before/after contrast + memory

BEST USE & EDITORIAL GUARDRAIL
Use when an old habit, place, product, or system has visibly changed. Combine recognition with a real
explanation of what changed, when, and why.



061 Whatever Happened to [X]?
      EXAMPLE Whatever Happened to the Corner Newsstand?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Gentle nostalgia plus an unresolved outcome.

062 Why You Don’t See [X] Anymore
      EXAMPLE Why You Don’t See Coin-Operated Scales Anymore
      WHY IT MAY WORK Everyday disappearance activates recognition.

063 When [X] Ruled [Place/Era]
      EXAMPLE When Roller Rinks Ruled Small-Town Weekends
      WHY IT MAY WORK A high point creates a rise-to-present contrast.

064 The Forgotten [X] That Once [Y]
      EXAMPLE The Forgotten Hall That Once Hosted Every Dance
      WHY IT MAY WORK Rediscovery of a surprisingly important subject.

065 The Last Days of [X]
      EXAMPLE The Last Days of the Old Bus Terminal
      WHY IT MAY WORK A farewell story with emotional gravity.

066 Before [X] Existed, [Y] Was Different
      EXAMPLE Before Online Maps, Road Trips Were Different
      WHY IT MAY WORK Change made tangible through daily routines.

067 How [X] Changed [Y] Forever
      EXAMPLE How the Photocopier Changed Office Work Forever
      WHY IT MAY WORK Clear invention-to-impact relationship.

068 The [X] We Lost Along the Way
      EXAMPLE The Neighborhood Gathering Places We Lost Along the Way
      WHY IT MAY WORK Loss and collective memory make the topic personal.

069 What [X] Looked Like [Number] Years Ago
      EXAMPLE What School Lunch Looked Like 70 Years Ago
      WHY IT MAY WORK Invites direct visual comparison across time.

070 When [X] Was Everywhere
      EXAMPLE When Neon Signs Were Everywhere
      WHY IT MAY WORK A once-familiar world creates recollection.

08 Small causes, big consequences
CORE CLICK DRIVER Scale contrast + the butterfly effect

BEST USE & EDITORIAL GUARDRAIL
Use when an apparently minor event started an important chain reaction. Distinguish a contributing trigger from the

071 How One Small [Decision] Changed Everything
      EXAMPLE How One Small Design Decision Changed Everything
      WHY IT MAY WORK Tiny cause versus large eventual result.

      EXAMPLE The Rejection That Created a New Community Club
      WHY IT MAY WORK A no becomes the origin story.

073 When [X] Said No to [Y]
      EXAMPLE When a Publisher Said No to a New Idea
      WHY IT MAY WORK Refusal provides an instant conflict.

      EXAMPLE The Last-Minute Change That Saved the Event
      WHY IT MAY WORK Deadline pressure and rescue.

075 When a [Mistake] Turned Into [Success]
      EXAMPLE When a Printing Mistake Turned Into a Trend
      WHY IT MAY WORK Accidental success violates expectations.

      EXAMPLE The Volunteer Job Nobody Wanted
      WHY IT MAY WORK Low-status role creates overlooked-hero potential.

      EXAMPLE The Parade Almost Never Happened
      WHY IT MAY WORK A familiar outcome is reframed as fragile.

078 The Moment [X] Realized [Y]
      EXAMPLE The Moment the Team Realized Their Plan Was Wrong

      EXAMPLE It Wasn’t the Weather That Ended the Event — It Was the Budget
      WHY IT MAY WORK Corrects a presumed cause through contrast.

080 [X] Tried to [Goal]. Instead, [Outcome]
      EXAMPLE The Club Tried to Expand. Instead, It Split
      WHY IT MAY WORK Unintended consequence drives the story.

09 Problems, trade-offs & practical insight
CORE CLICK DRIVER Utility + risk reduction + understanding

BEST USE & EDITORIAL GUARDRAIL
Use when the audience wants to learn, choose, save time, or make sense of a trade-off. The promise should be
concrete and useful.



081 How Much Does [X] Really Cost?
      EXAMPLE How Much Does Running a Food Stall Really Cost?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Practical numbers challenge assumptions.

082 Why [X] Is So Expensive
      EXAMPLE Why Custom Furniture Is So Expensive
      WHY IT MAY WORK High everyday friction plus explanation.

083 The Hidden Economics of [X]
      EXAMPLE The Hidden Economics of a Weekend Market
      WHY IT MAY WORK Reveal the incentives behind an activity.

084 The Beginner’s Guide to [X]
      EXAMPLE The Beginner’s Guide to Collecting Postcards
      WHY IT MAY WORK Reduces intimidation with a clear entry point.

085 [Number] Mistakes That Ruin [X]
      EXAMPLE 7 Mistakes That Ruin a First Presentation
      WHY IT MAY WORK Avoidance of foreseeable losses.

086 The Biggest Myth About [X]
      EXAMPLE The Biggest Myth About Learning a New Language
      WHY IT MAY WORK One clear misconception becomes the promise.

087 Why [X] Works (And [Y] Doesn’t)
      EXAMPLE Why Short Meetings Work (And Long Ones Don’t)
      WHY IT MAY WORK Contrast converts insight into a practical choice.

088 What Happens When [X] Is Removed?
      EXAMPLE What Happens When a Neighborhood Loses Its Park?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Explores a system by subtracting one piece.

089 [X] Explained in [Number] Minutes
      EXAMPLE Budgeting Basics Explained in 12 Minutes
      WHY IT MAY WORK Low time commitment for a bounded lesson.

090 What It Takes to [Achieve X]
      EXAMPLE What It Takes to Restore an Old Wooden Table
      WHY IT MAY WORK Behind-the-work difficulty promises respect and detail.

10 Conflict, stakes & alternative outcomes
CORE CLICK DRIVER Conflict + uncertainty + imagined consequence

BEST USE & EDITORIAL GUARDRAIL
Use when the true story contains a strong obstacle, rivalry, narrow escape, or plausible counterfactual.
Separate documented history from speculation.



091 [X] vs [Y]: The Battle That Changed [Z]
      EXAMPLE Two Design Ideas: The Battle That Changed the Product
      WHY IT MAY WORK Opposing forces create a clear narrative.

092 How [X] Survived [Y]
      EXAMPLE How the Bookshop Survived a Difficult Winter
      WHY IT MAY WORK Survival under pressure invites causal explanation.

093 The Warnings [X] Ignored
      EXAMPLE The Warnings the Organizers Ignored
      WHY IT MAY WORK Foreknowledge makes later events feel avoidable.

094 The [Number] Minutes That Changed [X]
      EXAMPLE The 15 Minutes That Changed the Meeting
      WHY IT MAY WORK Compressed timeline raises intensity.

095 What If [X] Had Never Happened?
      EXAMPLE What If the Printing Press Had Never Spread?
      WHY IT MAY WORK Counterfactual helps reveal real-world impact.

096 The Closest [X] Ever Came to [Y]
      EXAMPLE The Closest the Team Ever Came to Quitting
      WHY IT MAY WORK Near-miss structure sustains suspense.

097 [X] Was One Step Away From [Y]
      EXAMPLE The Project Was One Step Away From Cancellation
      WHY IT MAY WORK An uncertain outcome becomes the hook.

098 When [X] Turned Against [Y]
      EXAMPLE When the Community Turned Against the Plan
      WHY IT MAY WORK Alliance-to-opposition creates drama.

099 The Decision [X] Regretted for Years
      EXAMPLE The Decision the Organizer Regretted for Years
      WHY IT MAY WORK Long-term regret gives a consequence.

100 How [X] Became [Its Own Worst Enemy]
      EXAMPLE How a Growing Club Became Its Own Worst Enemy
      WHY IT MAY WORK Internal contradiction instead of outside villain.

Turn a format into a publishable title
Use this repeatable six-step workflow before committing to a title. You are testing an angle, not copying a phrase.


1. Define the viewer and the topic
Write one sentence: “This video is for people who care about ___ and want to understand ___.”


2. Identify the strongest true tension
Look for a verifiable mystery, contradiction, conflict, unexpected outcome, cost, or change. Do not fabricate one.


3. Select 3–5 compatible format families
Choose based on your actual material. A ranking format requires a meaningful comparison set; a scandal format
requires evidence.


4. Draft 8–12 variations
Keep the subject recognizable, but vary the angle, specific stakes, phrasing, and promise. Avoid mere synonym
swaps.


5. Score the best three
Use the six-factor rubric on the next page. Reject any version whose promise the video cannot fulfill.


6. Pair the title with one visual idea
Let the thumbnail add a complementary clue. Then revise for mobile readability and avoid repeating the full title on the
image.

A REUSABLE DRAFTING PROMPT
“My video topic is [TOPIC]. My target viewer is [VIEWER]. Verified facts and useful evidence: [FACTS]. Suggest 10
truthful titles using different formats from the 100-format library. For each, name the click mechanism, tension,
promise, and any claim that requires verification. Reject misleading angles.”
                                                                                       THE 100-FORMAT TITLE PLAYBOOK / 14
                                                                                    CREATOR EDUCATION • TITLE PSYCHOLOGY




A title quality scorecard
Rate every shortlisted title from 0 to 5 on each dimension (30 points maximum). The score is a decision aid, not a
scientific predictor of click-through rate.


DIMENSION QUESTION 0–5 Audience recognition Does the target viewer immediately care about the subject?


____ Clarity Can someone understand the basic premise in one glance? ____ Curiosity gap Does it create one


meaningful unanswered question? ____ Stakes or relevance Is there a consequence, payoff, surprise, or useful


outcome? ____ Distinctiveness Does it differ from nearby videos beyond word substitutions? ____


Proof & delivery Can the script support the claim and fulfill the promise? ____

Suggested interpretation: 26–30 = strong candidate; 21–25 = revise weak dimensions; 0–20 = rethink the angle. Any
unsupported claim is a veto, even when the numeric score is high.


Title + thumbnail: one combined promise
TITLE Why the Old Bookstore Closed
THUMBNAIL Photo: bookstore entrance + “LAST DAY”
PAIRING Title poses the question; thumbnail supplies context and emotion.

TITLE The Mistake That Ended the Festival
THUMBNAIL Photo: empty festival gate + one clear warning sign
PAIRING Title names the mechanism; thumbnail hints at the moment.

TITLE How a Small Town Saved Its Library
THUMBNAIL Photo: library before/after + “SAVED”
PAIRING Title promises a story; thumbnail shows the visual stakes.

Three worked title-building exercises
Exercise 1: A community theater nearly closes
Topic statement: A community theater faced closure but later recovered; assume all details are verified before
publishing.

Mystery What Really Happened to the Community Theater?

Downfall What Went Wrong With the Community Theater?

Contradiction The Theater Was Full — So Why Was It Closing?

Turning point The Decision That Saved the Community Theater

Character How One Volunteer Kept the Theater Alive

Economics The Hidden Costs Behind the Theater’s Crisis




Exercise 2: An old school tradition disappears
Topic statement: A school used to have an annual tradition that later ended; investigate the real reasons.

Disappearance Why the School Tradition Suddenly Disappeared

Nostalgia Do You Remember This School Tradition?

Time When the Annual School Fair Was Everywhere

Investigation The Real Reason the Annual Fair Ended

Legacy The Last Days of the School Fair

Contradiction Everyone Loved the Fair — So Why Did It End?




Exercise 3: A small design fix improves an object
Topic statement: A simple redesign makes a common object more useful; define the actual measurable
improvement.

Genius The Genius of a Tiny Design Change

Practical Why This Design Works (And the Old One Doesn’t)

Contrast A Small Change Made a Big Difference

Behind the scenes How the Improved Design Actually Works

History How One Design Decision Changed Everyday Use

Explainer Every Part of the New Design Explained

                                                                                            CREATOR EDUCATION • TITLE PSYCHOLOGY




12 mistakes that weaken otherwise good titles
01. Copying the surface wording → Transfer the psychology, not an exact competitor headline. 02.

Forcing a “dark secret” onto an ordinary explanation → Pick an angle supported by evidence. 03.

Generic subject wording → Name the object, person, place, incident, or outcome that matters. 04. No real

stakes → Add the consequence, contrast, or payoff that the video covers. 05. Two separate promises →

Keep one dominant question in the title.

06. Repeated channel packaging → Rotate compatible mechanisms; don’t simply replace nouns every upload. 07.

Overusing ALL CAPS and punctuation → Use emphasis sparingly; let the premise carry the click. 08. Overstating

certainty → Use cautious wording for predictions or disputed explanations. 09. Ranking without criteria → State

what “best,” “worst,” or “most” measures.

10. Vague “they” and “this” openings → Keep enough context for a first-time viewer. 11. Misaligned

thumbnail → Add complementary information rather than repeating the entire title. 12. Ignoring the script

promise → Do not title a video around a reveal that never arrives.


EDITORIAL SAFETY CHECKLIST
Before publishing: (1) Is the central claim true? (2) Can the video show or explain the promised answer? (3) Does
“every,” “only,” “first,” “best,” “last,” or “real reason” need qualification? (4) Is this a fair representation of uncertainty?

Printable title development worksheet
Copy this worksheet once for each video idea. Its purpose is to make the first title draft less generic and the final choice
more deliberate.

Target viewer Who should care, and why?


Topic / subject What is the specific person, thing, event, or question?


Verified facts What information can the script safely establish?


Unique angle What is surprising, emotional, useful, or underexplored?


Central argument What single idea will the video prove or make clear?


Central promise What will viewers understand by the end?


Format families Which 3–5 of the 10 families are compatible?


Version A ____________________________________________


Version B ____________________________________________

Winning title ____________________________________________


Thumbnail idea What one image adds tension rather than restating the title?


Final pre-publish questions
□ Would the intended viewer recognize the subject in under two seconds?

□ Does the title promise one clear and interesting outcome?

□ Can the opening 30 seconds reinforce that promise?

□ Can the full video actually deliver it without misleading the audience?

□ Does the thumbnail create a complementary curiosity gap?


       A great topic creates demand. A great format clarifies why it is worth clicking. END
                                                       OF PLAYBOOK




                                                                              THE 100-FORMAT TITLE PLAYBOOK / 18

CHAPTER 11   |    YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
MASTER PROMPT: YouTube Topic Opportunity Validator

YOUR ROLE

Act as a Senior YouTube Niche Researcher, Topic Validation Expert, Competitive Content
Analyst, and YouTube Title Strategist.

Your job is to help a beginner determine whether a selected YouTube topic is worth testing
based on the titles of videos appearing in YouTube search results.

The user will provide ONLY:

1. Their selected topic.
2. A collection of YouTube video titles copied from search results.

Do not ask the user for additional information. Work with the available data.

Your primary objective is NOT to find a perfect topic. Your objective is to identify realistic
content opportunities that a new YouTube creator can test.

Be analytical, practical, skeptical, and evidence-driven.

Do not automatically recommend every topic, but do not reject a topic simply because many
videos already exist.

---

PHASE 1: UNDERSTAND THE TOPIC

First, identify:

- What exactly is the user's selected topic?
- What broader niche does it belong to?
- What type of audience would likely watch this content?
- What viewer problems, interests, questions, or emotions does this topic address?
- Is it suitable for multiple videos or mainly a single video?
- What is the likely primary viewer intent: education, entertainment, curiosity, nostalgia,
problem-solving, fear, controversy, or something else?

Do not invent demographic statistics.

---

PHASE 2: ANALYZE ALL SEARCH RESULT TITLES

Carefully analyze every pasted title.

Do not randomly select a few titles and ignore the remaining ones.
First, clean the data:

- Remove exact duplicates.
- Identify repeated or near-identical titles.
- Separate directly relevant, partially relevant, and irrelevant results.
- Identify different subtopics.
- Identify repeated subjects and angles.

Next, group the relevant videos into content clusters.

For each cluster, identify:

1. The main content theme.
2. Common title structures.
3. Repeated approaches.
4. Psychological triggers used.
5. How crowded the angle appears within the supplied results.
6. Whether creators are exploring different perspectives or repeating the same ideas.

Remember: Search ranking and the number of matching titles do not prove high demand,
strong performance, or low competition.

If views, upload dates, or other performance data were not provided, explicitly mark those
metrics as unverified.

---

PHASE 3: SUPPLY & REPETITION ANALYSIS

Determine whether the provided search results indicate:

A. Diverse Supply

Many videos exist, but they cover different angles, subtopics, or viewer needs.

B. Repetitive Supply

Most videos discuss similar subjects using almost identical angles.

C. Concentrated Supply

A small number of approaches dominate the results.

D. Weak Relevant Supply

Very few supplied titles genuinely address the selected topic.
Explain what these patterns suggest for a new creator.

IMPORTANT:

High supply is NOT an automatic rejection.

Many existing videos may indicate competition, established audience interest, or both.

A crowded topic can still offer opportunities through:

- Different viewer questions.
- More specific subtopics.
- Contradiction.
- Alternative perspectives.
- Better content organization.
- Different title formats.
- Untold or overlooked information.
- Unexpected comparisons.
- Niche bending.
- Format arbitrage.

However, do not claim that any angle is guaranteed to succeed.

---

PHASE 4: IDENTIFY CONTENT GAPS

Find potential opportunities that are underrepresented in the provided titles.

Examine:

1. Subject Gaps

Which relevant subjects or subtopics are missing?

2. Angle Gaps

Which potentially interesting perspectives have not been explored much?

3. Format Gaps

Are creators mostly using one title structure?

Could another format communicate the topic more effectively?

4. Psychological Gaps

Are creators ignoring useful viewer motivations such as:
- Contradiction
- Consequences
- Unanswered questions
- Hidden causes
- Curiosity
- Mistakes and failures
- Transformation
- Emotional relevance
- Unexpected discoveries
- Comparison
- Problem-solving
- Nostalgia

5. Specificity Gaps

Are existing titles too broad?

Could a more specific person, event, situation, question, or problem create a stronger topic?

6. Niche-Bending Opportunities

Could a closely related subject create a fresh entry point without attracting an entirely
different audience?

Never invent a factual historical event, controversy, scientific discovery, or outcome just to
make a title more clickable.

A missing angle in the supplied titles is only an apparent gap, not proof that the idea has
never been covered on YouTube.

---

PHASE 5: TOPIC OPPORTUNITY EVALUATION

Evaluate the topic using these five criteria.

Score each criterion from 0 to 5, using only what can reasonably be inferred from the titles.

1. Search Relevance

How strongly do the results relate to the original topic?

2. Content Variety

Are there enough meaningful subtopics and perspectives?

3. Differentiation Potential
Can a new creator approach the subject differently from the existing titles?

4. Topic Repeatability

Does the subject offer enough related ideas for a continuing content series?

5. Packaging Potential

Can this subject support specific, interesting, and honest YouTube titles?

Calculate a total score out of 25.

Scores must reflect observable title-based signals, not assumed video performance.

Treat these scores as a practical research framework, not an official YouTube metric or a
prediction of views.

Do not score actual demand, CTR, retention, or viral potential without relevant data.

---

PHASE 6: MAKE A DECISION

Choose ONE of the following verdicts.

VERDICT A: RECOMMENDED FOR TESTING

Use when:

- The results show relevant topic coverage.
- Multiple meaningful content opportunities exist.
- There is room for differentiation.
- At least a few distinct, credible ideas can be developed.

This means the topic is suitable for an initial experiment, not guaranteed to succeed.

VERDICT B: CONDITIONAL — CHANGE THE ANGLE

Use when:

- The topic is crowded with repetitive approaches.
- The original angle appears difficult to differentiate.
- Better opportunities exist in a more specific subtopic or alternative format.
- The topic should be refined before production.

VERDICT C: NOT RECOMMENDED
Use when:

- The supplied results give very weak support for the intended subject.
- The topic is too narrow to support meaningful content development.
- The available angles are overwhelmingly repetitive AND you cannot identify credible
differentiation.
- The topic is based on questionable assumptions or lacks enough reliable content
possibilities.
- You cannot generate worthwhile ideas without inventing facts, making misleading promises,
or drastically changing the topic.

If the supplied titles are insufficient, say that evidence is insufficient rather than pretending
the entire niche has been proven unsuccessful.

Important: Never reject a topic simply because it has High Supply.

---

PHASE 7: GENERATE FIVE CONTENT OPPORTUNITIES

If the verdict is RECOMMENDED FOR TESTING or CONDITIONAL, provide exactly FIVE
distinct topic opportunities.

These must be realistic topics that a beginner can potentially produce.

They must NOT be five slight rewrites of the same title.

Use a different strategic angle for each whenever appropriate.

Explore possibilities such as:

1. An underexplored subject or question.
2. A contradiction or unexpected outcome.
3. A specific problem, mistake, or consequence.
4. A different content format or presentation style.
5. A closely related subtopic or niche-bending opportunity.

Do not force these five categories when the research supports better alternatives.

For EACH recommendation, provide:

A. Topic Idea: The subject to cover.

B. Ready-to-Use YouTube Title: A natural, clickable title in English.

C. Psychological Trigger: Why viewers may be interested in clicking.
D. Opportunity Evidence: Which patterns or omissions in the supplied search results support
this suggestion.

E. Differentiation: How the proposed video differs from existing results.

F. Execution Difficulty: Easy / Medium / Hard, with a short explanation based on likely
research and content complexity.

G. Alternative Title: One additional title using a different approach.

Prefer ideas that are practical, specific, and understandable for beginners.

Avoid generic titles, artificial sensationalism, and unsupported claims.

---

PHASE 8: FINAL OUTPUT FORMAT

Use the following structure:

1. TOPIC UNDERSTANDING

- Original topic:
- Broader niche:
- Likely audience interest:
- Topic type:

2. SEARCH RESULT ANALYSIS

- Total titles received:
- Unique titles:
- Directly relevant:
- Partially relevant:
- Irrelevant:
- Main content clusters:
- Most repeated angles:
- Common title formats:

3. SUPPLY ANALYSIS

- Observed supply pattern:
- Repetition level: Low / Medium / High
- Apparent content gaps:
- Main competition concern:
- Opportunities for differentiation:

4. TOPIC OPPORTUNITY SCORE
Criterion| Score
Search Relevance| /5
Content Variety| /5
Differentiation Potential| /5
Topic Repeatability| /5
Packaging Potential| /5
TOTAL| /25

Provide a short explanation of the score.

5. FINAL VERDICT

Select one:

- RECOMMENDED FOR TESTING
- CONDITIONAL — CHANGE THE ANGLE
- NOT RECOMMENDED

Explain your decision in simple Hinglish.

6. FIVE RECOMMENDED VIDEO IDEAS

Only if the topic is recommended or conditional.

For each of the five ideas, give:

- Topic
- Main YouTube title
- Alternative title
- Psychological trigger
- Evidence from supplied results
- Differentiation
- Execution difficulty

Rank these five by practical suitability for a beginner, rather than pretending to know which
will receive the most views.

7. BEST FIRST VIDEO

Choose ONE idea from the five recommendations.

Explain:

- Why it is a sensible first experiment.
- What unique angle to focus on.
- What the video must deliver to satisfy the title.

8. BEGINNER ACTION PLAN
Give three practical next steps the student should follow.

If the topic is NOT RECOMMENDED, replace sections 6–8 with:

- Three clear reasons for rejection.
- What evidence is missing or weak.
- Whether the topic should be narrowed, expanded, or abandoned.
- Three alternative research directions, without presenting them as validated winners.

---

STRICT INSTRUCTIONS

1. Speak in simple Hinglish / Roman Urdu so beginners can understand.
2. Write all proposed YouTube video titles in English.
3. Be critical but constructive.
4. Never recommend a topic merely to please the user.
5. Never reject a topic merely because competition exists.
6. Do not fabricate views, CTR, demand, channel size, or performance.
7. Do not say a title format is "proven to work" unless actual performance evidence has been
supplied.
8. Do not confuse frequent appearances in search with audience demand.
9. Do not copy existing titles word-for-word. Create genuinely differentiated ideas.
10. Every recommended idea should connect to a real research observation.
11. Do not suggest five versions of essentially the same video.
12. Prioritize realistic opportunities over forced creativity.
13. Clearly separate observations, hypotheses, and unverified assumptions.
14. No unnecessary motivational speeches.
15. Do not ask follow-up questions. Make the best possible evaluation from the supplied
titles.
16. Your goal is to help a beginner START with a reasonable, evidence-informed topic, not
spend weeks searching for perfection.

---

STUDENT INPUT

MY SELECTED TOPIC:

[Paste your topic here]

YOUTUBE SEARCH RESULT TITLES:

[Paste all copied YouTube video titles here, one per line]

Now perform the complete analysis and give your final verdict.
ORIGINAL DOCUMENT

CHAPTER 12   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
MASTER PROMPT — YouTube Niche Research Keyword Generator

Act as a senior YouTube niche researcher, content strategist, audience psychologist, and
outlier analyst.

I am researching new opportunities for a faceless YouTube channel.

I do NOT want you to simply give me a list of niches such as:

- History
- Celebrities
- Finance
- Sports
- Technology
- True crime

Instead, I want you to generate SEARCH KEYWORDS, TITLE PHRASES, and
REPEATABLE TITLE PATTERNS that I can type directly into YouTube to DISCOVER niches,
sub-niches, channels, outlier videos, and emerging content formats.

My goal is to discover opportunities through actual YouTube videos rather than relying on
generic niche lists.

MY CHANNEL REQUIREMENTS

I am primarily looking for niches where:

- High-quality faceless videos can be produced.
- Videos can use existing footage, archival clips, interviews, photographs, news footage,
public footage, screenshots, documents, maps, graphics, stock footage, B-roll, or simple
motion graphics.
- The content can support documentary-style storytelling.
- Individual videos can be built around specific people, events, products, companies, places,
incidents, decisions, mysteries, transformations, conflicts, successes, failures, or
discoveries.
- There should ideally be hundreds of potential future topics.
- The niche should not depend heavily on showing the creator's face.
- The niche should have strong title and thumbnail potential.
- Long-form videos should be possible.
- Storytelling, curiosity, conflict, transformation, mystery, nostalgia, or revelation should
naturally exist.
- Prefer evergreen or semi-evergreen subjects rather than content that dies within a few
days.
- Target audience can primarily be from the USA, UK, Canada, Australia, and other
English-speaking markets.

Do NOT restrict yourself to niches I already know.
The entire purpose of this exercise is DISCOVERY.

---

YOUR MAIN TASK

Generate search phrases that I can type directly into YouTube.

These should resemble phrases commonly appearing inside successful YouTube titles.

Examples of the TYPE of phrase I mean:

"What happened to"
"What really happened to"
"Why ___ disappeared"
"Why ___ failed"
"Why ___ refused to"
"The rise and fall of"
"The mistake that destroyed"
"The decision that changed"
"The dark truth about"

These examples are only references.

Do NOT simply give variations of those examples.

Think much broader.

---

STEP 1 — IDENTIFY SEARCH PSYCHOLOGIES

First identify at least 20 different psychological mechanisms that successful
documentary/storytelling titles use.

Examples could include:

- Disappearance
- Unexplained decline
- Failure
- Sudden success
- Betrayal
- Conflict
- Refusal
- Regret
- One bad decision
- One unexpected event
- Career destruction
- Reputation reversal
- Transformation
- Mystery
- Hidden truth
- Forgotten story
- Irony
- Success creating destruction
- Small cause creating massive consequences
- Unfinished story

But find additional mechanisms yourself.

For every psychology, briefly explain:

1. What creates the curiosity.
2. What question appears in the viewer's mind.
3. What kinds of subjects it can work with.

Do not stay generic.

For example, don't simply write:

"Curiosity gap."

Instead explain:

"The viewer knows that the subject was once successful or famous, but now seems absent.
The missing explanation between those two states creates the click."

---

STEP 2 — CREATE YOUTUBE SEARCH PHRASES

Using those psychological mechanisms, generate at least 100 search phrases that I could
literally type into YouTube.

IMPORTANT:

These are SEARCH PHRASES, not completed video titles.

For example:

"why disappeared"

rather than:

"Why Brendan Fraser Disappeared From Hollywood"
I want phrases that expose many different videos, creators, and niches.

Good search phrases could follow structures such as:

why ___
what happened ___
how ___
the ___ that ___
when ___
inside ___
behind ___
truth about ___
real reason ___

But do not limit yourself to these.

---

STEP 3 — GROUP THE KEYWORDS BY PSYCHOLOGY

Organize the 100+ phrases into useful categories.

For example:

Disappearance / Where Are They Now

- what happened to
- whatever happened to
- why disappeared
- why never returned

Failure / Collapse

- why failed
- why collapsed
- how lost everything
- what killed

Conflict / Betrayal

...

One Event Changed Everything

...

Hidden Truth
...

Transformation

...

Nostalgia

...

Mystery

...

Unexpected Consequences

...

Success → Destruction

...

Create as many useful categories as necessary.

I want variety.

Do not give me 30 phrases that mean almost exactly the same thing.

---

STEP 4 — DISCOVERY-FIRST KEYWORDS

Create a separate section called:

HIGH-DISCOVERY SEARCHES

These should be phrases that are especially useful when I have NO niche selected.

They should potentially expose videos across completely different niches.

For example, a phrase such as:

"The mistake that destroyed"

could expose:

- companies
- celebrities
- sports careers
- military campaigns
- engineering projects
- bands
- restaurants
- technology companies

I want at least 30 of these highly transferable search phrases.

For every phrase, tell me 3–6 completely different niches it might uncover.

---

STEP 5 — NICHE-BENDING KEYWORDS

Now generate at least 30 search phrases that are psychologically strong but less obvious.

The purpose is to help me discover content formats that other researchers might overlook.

Think in terms of:

- Contradiction
- Irony
- Unexpected refusal
- Winning but losing
- Success becoming a curse
- A creator hating their own creation
- A person trapped by their biggest achievement
- Something becoming obsolete
- Someone making the correct prediction but being ignored
- Something everyone misunderstood
- A decision that looked smart at first
- Something that worked too well
- Something designed to solve one problem but creating another
- An invention accidentally destroying something
- Someone refusing an enormous opportunity
- A forgotten rival
- A hidden conflict
- An event nobody understood at the time

Turn these psychological ideas into YouTube-searchable phrases.

---

STEP 6 — SUBJECT-AGNOSTIC FORMATS

Give me at least 30 title formulas where I can insert almost any subject.
Examples:

Why [SUBJECT] Refused to [ACTION]

The [THING] That Destroyed [SUBJECT]

How [SUBJECT] Lost [VALUABLE THING]

But create stronger and more diverse formats than these examples.

The formula should ideally work across several niches.

For every formula provide:

PSYCHOLOGY:
Why does it make people curious?

POSSIBLE APPLICATIONS:
Give 3 examples from completely different niches.

Example structure:

FORMAT:
The ___ That Changed ___ Forever

PSYCHOLOGY:
A specific cause is connected to a permanent consequence. The viewer wants to
understand how one thing could have produced such a large change.

APPLICATIONS:
Boxing → The Fight That Changed George Foreman Forever

Business → The Decision That Changed Apple Forever

History → The Battle That Changed Europe Forever

---

STEP 7 — FIND KEYWORDS FOR VISUALLY RICH NICHES

Because I want high-quality faceless production, identify search phrases especially likely to
uncover subjects with lots of available visual material.

Prioritize potential access to:

- Archive video
- Old interviews
- News reports
- Film/TV footage
- Sports footage
- Historical photographs
- Advertisements
- Product footage
- Company footage
- Maps
- Documents
- Newspaper headlines
- Stock footage
- Public appearances
- Speeches
- Existing B-roll

Create at least 30 search phrases specifically useful for discovering visually rich niches.

---

STEP 8 — AVOID LOW-QUALITY NICHE SIGNALS

Flag search phrases that are likely to lead primarily to:

- Generic AI slideshow channels
- Meditation content
- Quotes
- Motivation compilations
- Low-information listicles
- Repetitive celebrity gossip
- Pure breaking-news dependency
- Content requiring daily uploads
- Extremely limited topic pools
- Content where finding legal/useful visual material may be unusually difficult

Do not remove such keywords automatically.

Just label them as lower priority for my goals.

---

STEP 9 — CREATE MY FIRST SEARCH SESSION

Assume I have 60–90 minutes to research YouTube.

Give me the exact first 40 searches you would personally perform.

Order them strategically.

Do NOT group all similar searches together.
I want the searches to maximize niche diversity.

For example, don't give me:

why disappeared
why vanished
why went away
why left
why never came back

one after another.

Instead mix different psychological mechanisms so that the search session exposes
completely different content ecosystems.

For each search give me:

SEARCH:
The phrase to type.

LOOK FOR:
What pattern I should notice.

POTENTIAL SIGNAL:
What would make this search interesting enough to investigate further.

---

STEP 10 — TEACH ME WHAT TO LOOK FOR

After giving me the keywords, explain how I should evaluate the YouTube search results.

I am NOT simply looking for videos with millions of views.

Teach me to identify opportunities such as:

Small Channel + Huge Video

Example:

Channel subscribers: 20K

Normal views: 10K–30K

Outlier video: 800K

This may indicate that the TOPIC or FORMAT is stronger than the channel itself.
Repeated Outliers

If several videos using similar psychology outperform, this matters more than one viral
accident.

Cross-Channel Validation

If multiple unrelated channels are successfully covering similar subjects or using similar
formats, this can validate demand.

Topic Depth

Ask:

Can I make 100 videos here?

Not:

Can I make one good video?

Visual Availability

Ask:

Can a 15–30 minute high-quality faceless documentary be visually supported?

Freshness

Check whether the format is still working on videos uploaded recently rather than relying
entirely on videos from many years ago.

Audience Value

Look for audiences attractive for long-form English-language YouTube.

Add any other important signals an experienced niche researcher should examine.

---

STEP 11 — GIVE ME A NICHE RESEARCH SCORECARD

Create a simple scoring system from 1–10 for every potential niche I discover.

Include:

- Demand
- Outlier frequency
- Competition
- Topic depth
- Visual availability
- Storytelling potential
- Title potential
- Thumbnail potential
- Evergreen potential
- Monetization potential
- Production difficulty
- Research difficulty
- Ability to differentiate
- Availability of recognizable subjects
- Ability to create recurring formats

Then explain what overall score would make you:

TEST IMMEDIATELY

WATCH / RESEARCH MORE

AVOID

---

STEP 12 — MOST IMPORTANT RULES

Follow these rules strictly:

1. Do not start by recommending niches.

2. Start with discovery mechanisms and search phrases.

3. Do not assume that traditional niche labels are the best way to find opportunities.

4. Think in terms of:
  Psychology → Search phrase → Outlier → Pattern → Niche.

5. Prioritize search phrases commonly compatible with documentary-style content.

6. Avoid giving dozens of almost identical keywords.

7. Search phrases should sound like language actually used in YouTube titles.

8. Think across industries.

A search phrase should potentially uncover:

Hollywood
Music
Sports
Business
History
Technology
Science
Automotive
Aviation
Military history
Engineering
Travel/geography
Brands
Products
Television
Internet history
Architecture
Food businesses
Disasters
Exploration
Human stories

9. Don't force these categories. They are examples designed to make you think broadly.

10. Be creative enough that at least 25% of the search phrases are things an average
YouTube niche researcher probably wouldn't think of.

11. Separate:

SEARCH PHRASES

from

VIDEO TITLE FORMULAS.

They are related but they serve different purposes.

12. Prioritize psychological search terms over subject keywords.

For example:

Better:
"the decision that destroyed"

Less useful for this exercise:
"car documentaries"

13. Don't judge opportunities only by absolute views.
Consider:
Views relative to subscribers
Views relative to channel average
Number of similar winners
Recency
Repeatability
Topic inventory

14. My objective is not to copy a video.

My objective is to discover:

AUDIENCE DESIRE
+
CLICK PSYCHOLOGY
+
REPEATABLE FORMAT
+
CONTENT UNIVERSE.

---

FINAL OUTPUT FORMAT

Give me the response in this exact sequence:

A. 20+ Click Psychologies

B. 100+ YouTube Search Keywords

C. Keywords Grouped by Psychology

D. 30 High-Discovery Keywords

E. 30 Niche-Bending Keywords

F. 30 Subject-Agnostic Title Formats

G. 30 Visually Rich Niche Search Keywords

H. Lower-Priority / Risky Search Types

I. My First 40 YouTube Searches

J. How to Analyze Search Results

K. Niche Research Scorecard
L. Top 20 Search Phrases You Would Personally Start With

Do not give shallow answers.

Think like someone whose job is to discover unexplored or underexploited YouTube
opportunities before competitors notice them.

CHAPTER 13   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
Data needed to validate a Niche Bend


         Data                     What to provide                      Why it matters



 1. Core Niche           3–10 channels + 20–50 strong         Shows what the current
 Competitors             videos                               audience already watches



 2. Core Niche           Titles, views, upload age, channel   Shows which angles outperform
 Outliers                average



 3. Proposed             Explain what you want to bend        Defines the experiment
 Adjacent Niche          toward



 4. Adjacent-Niche       20–50 successful videos around       Proves that desire already exists
 Proof                   that adjacent interest



 5. Overlap Evidence     Videos combining both areas          Best proof that audiences may
                                                              overlap



 6. Potential Topics     30–50 possible video ideas           Tests scalability



 7. Packaging            Titles + thumbnails from both        Shows whether the bend can be
 Examples                sides                                packaged clearly


​
​
Act as a senior YouTube niche strategist specializing in faceless channels, audience
psychology, niche positioning, topic selection, packaging, and channel scalability.

I want to determine whether an existing YouTube niche can be successfully BENT toward an
adjacent interest, audience desire, emotion, perspective, or storytelling category.
IMPORTANT:

Do NOT assume my idea is good.

Your job is to actively look for reasons the proposed niche bend might fail.

A niche bend should NOT be approved simply because the two subjects are logically related.

The key question is:

“Would substantially the same YouTube viewer who enjoys the core niche naturally care about
this new angle?”

Separate these three things:

    1.​ Logical topic connection
    2.​ Possible audience overlap
    3.​ Proven YouTube audience overlap

I will provide:

A. CORE NICHE​
B. PROPOSED ADJACENT DIRECTION​
C. CORE NICHE COMPETITOR DATA​
D. CORE NICHE OUTLIERS​
E. ADJACENT-NICHE SUCCESSFUL VIDEOS​
F. CROSSOVER / OVERLAP VIDEOS, if available​
G. POTENTIAL TOPICS​
H. THUMBNAIL / TITLE EXAMPLES, if available




STEP 1 — DEFINE THE CORE NICHE
Identify what the core audience is actually coming for.

Do not define the niche only by subject.

Analyze:

    ●​   Core subject
    ●​   Viewer age / likely demographic
    ●​   Emotional promise
    ●​   Knowledge level
    ●​   Reasons viewers click
    ●​   Reasons viewers continue watching
   ●​   Recurring story types
   ●​   Familiar personalities / events
   ●​   Expected tone
   ●​   Expected pacing
   ●​   Thumbnail conventions
   ●​   Title psychology

Summarize the CORE AUDIENCE DESIRE in one sentence.

Example:

Instead of:

“Boxing fans like boxing.”

Identify something deeper such as:

“Older boxing viewers enjoy legendary fighters, rivalry, nostalgia, personality, dramatic turning
points, and stories that reveal what happened behind famous fights.”




STEP 2 — DEFINE THE PROPOSED BEND
Explain exactly what is changing.

Classify the bend as one or more of:

   ●​   Topic Bend
   ●​   Emotional Bend
   ●​   Audience Bend
   ●​   Perspective Bend
   ●​   Format Bend
   ●​   Era Bend
   ●​   Demographic Bend
   ●​   Storytelling Bend

Then complete this sentence:

“This remains a ______ channel, but it now explores the subject through ______.”

If this statement is confusing, flag the niche positioning.
STEP 3 — AUDIENCE BRIDGE TEST
This is the most important part.

Ask:

Would someone who watches the current niche naturally be interested in the proposed adjacent
angle?

Score Audience Bridge from 1–5:

5 = Extremely natural overlap​
4 = Strong overlap​
3 = Partial overlap​
2 = Weak / requires explanation​
1 = Mostly different audiences

Explain WHY.

Do not award a high score just because the subjects are related academically.

Focus on VIEWING BEHAVIOR.




STEP 4 — CORE NICHE DEMAND ANALYSIS
Analyze supplied competitor data.

Identify:

    ●​   Which subjects repeatedly perform
    ●​   Which emotions repeatedly perform
    ●​   Which storytelling angles outperform
    ●​   Which title promises outperform
    ●​   Which topics fail
    ●​   What the audience appears to value most

Calculate where possible:

Outlier Ratio = Video Views / Channel Typical Views

Classify videos as:

Normal​
Moderate Outlier​
Strong Outlier​
Extreme Outlier

Identify patterns among the strongest outliers.




STEP 5 — ADJACENT DESIRE VALIDATION
Analyze the supplied adjacent-niche data.

Determine:

    ●​   Is this adjacent interest already proven on YouTube?
    ●​   Which emotions drive it?
    ●​   Which subjects perform best?
    ●​   Is the success dependent on huge celebrities?
    ●​   Is it evergreen or trend-dependent?
    ●​   Is the audience compatible with the core niche?

Do NOT yet conclude that the bend works.

First validate that the adjacent desire itself exists.




STEP 6 — CROSSOVER EVIDENCE
Look specifically for evidence where:

CORE NICHE​
+​
ADJACENT ANGLE

already appear together.

Examples:

Boxing + Psychology​
History + Survival​
Music + Career Conflict​
Hollywood + Career Decisions

Analyze any supplied crossover videos.
Ask:

   ●​   Do crossover topics outperform normal videos?
   ●​   Do multiple channels have success with them?
   ●​   Are there repeatable examples?
   ●​   Does the audience respond to the combination or only to one celebrity/event?

Classify crossover evidence:

STRONG​
MODERATE​
WEAK​
NONE

If crossover evidence is absent, clearly state that the niche bend is more experimental.




STEP 7 — VIEWER EXPECTATION TEST
Imagine a subscriber who came for the original niche.

For each proposed type of content ask:

“Would this subscriber understand why this video belongs on this channel?”

Classify each concept:

CORE​
SAFE BEND​
AGGRESSIVE BEND​
TOO FAR

Flag anything that would make the channel feel random.




STEP 8 — 20 TOPIC STRESS TEST
Create or analyze 20 potential videos using the proposed niche bend.

Use a mixture of:

   ●​ Mega-famous subjects
   ●​ Tier-2 recognizable subjects
   ●​ Events
   ●​ Lesser-known but strong stories

Classify each:

A = Excellent natural fit​
B = Strong enough​
C = Possible but forced​
D = Does not belong

If fewer than 12 of 20 are A/B level, flag scalability concerns.




STEP 9 — SAME VIEWER TEST
For each of the strongest topics answer:

Would approximately the same viewer click BOTH:

A. A traditional core-niche video

and

B. This bent-niche video?

Score each topic:

High overlap​
Medium overlap​
Low overlap

Explain the difference.

This test should focus on actual viewer motivation, not topic similarity.




STEP 10 — CONTENT DISTANCE TEST
Measure how far each proposed content type moves away from the core niche.

Create three zones:

GREEN ZONE​
Clearly belongs on the existing channel.
YELLOW ZONE​
Adjacent but should be introduced gradually.

RED ZONE​
Likely attracts a substantially different audience.

Explain the boundaries between these zones.




STEP 11 — TITLE & THUMBNAIL COMPATIBILITY
Evaluate whether the proposed bend can still be packaged in a way familiar to the core
audience.

For the 10 strongest topics provide:

   ●​   Title
   ●​   Core curiosity
   ●​   Thumbnail idea
   ●​   Familiar element from original niche
   ●​   New element introduced by the bend

The packaging should create:

FAMILIARITY + NOVELTY

not:

CONFUSION + NOVELTY

Score Packaging Compatibility /5.




STEP 12 — CELEBRITY DEPENDENCE TEST
Check whether the niche bend only appears strong with huge names.

Test:

Tier 1 — Mega famous​
Tier 2 — Recognizable​
Tier 3 — Niche recognizable
Determine whether the bend itself creates curiosity or whether celebrity fame is carrying the
concept.

If Tier-2 topics collapse, flag scalability risk.




STEP 13 — TOPIC INVENTORY
Estimate the available topic supply.

Classify topics:

Tier A — Killer​
Tier B — Strong​
Tier C — Usable​
Tier D — Forced

Estimate whether the bend can support:

20 videos​
50 videos​
100 videos

Determine whether it is:

A single experiment​
A recurring series​
A channel pillar​
A complete niche position




STEP 14 — CHANNEL IDENTITY TEST
Complete this sentence:

“This channel is for people who want ______.”

Then ask:

Do all proposed videos satisfy that promise?

Also create a one-sentence positioning statement:
“This is a ______ channel that explores ______ through ______.”

If the channel requires several unrelated explanations, flag positioning confusion.




STEP 15 — CANNIBALIZATION / AUDIENCE SPLIT RISK
Analyze whether the bend could split the audience.

Ask:

   ●​   Will existing subscribers ignore the new topics?
   ●​   Will new viewers ignore traditional topics?
   ●​   Could YouTube start attracting two separate viewer groups?
   ●​   Are the videos likely to recommend naturally beside each other?
   ●​   Does the channel still have one coherent viewer identity?

Score Audience Fragmentation Risk:

LOW​
MEDIUM​
HIGH




STEP 16 — DESIGN A 5-VIDEO PILOT
If the bend looks promising, design 5 pilot videos.

The pilots should gradually increase the bend.

Video 1:​
Very close to core niche

Video 2:​
Core niche + mild adjacent angle

Video 3:​
Balanced crossover

Video 4:​
Strong niche bend
Video 5:​
Most aggressive version worth testing

For each provide:

   ●​   Title
   ●​   Thumbnail concept
   ●​   Core audience appeal
   ●​   New audience appeal
   ●​   What this experiment tests
   ●​   What success would tell us
   ●​   What failure would tell us




STEP 17 — PERFORMANCE METRICS TO WATCH
Explain how I should evaluate the pilot.

Do not judge only by views.

Compare:

   ●​   CTR
   ●​   Early retention
   ●​   Average percentage viewed
   ●​   Browse impressions
   ●​   Suggested traffic
   ●​   Returning viewers
   ●​   New viewers
   ●​   Subscriber response
   ●​   Comments
   ●​   Performance versus channel baseline
   ●​   Whether viewers move between traditional and bent videos

Especially identify whether the SAME audience appears to be consuming both content types.




STEP 18 — SCORE THE NICHE BEND
Score each /5:

   1.​ Core Audience Strength
   2.​ Adjacent Desire Strength
    3.​ Audience Bridge
    4.​ Crossover Evidence
    5.​ Topic Scalability
    6.​ Title Scalability
    7.​ Thumbnail Scalability
    8.​ Celebrity Independence
    9.​ Channel Identity Clarity
    10.​Audience Cohesion
    11.​Long-Term Content Supply
    12.​Differentiation Opportunity

Total: /60

Interpretation:

51–60 = Very strong niche bend​
44–50 = Strong enough to test​
36–43 = Experimental​
30–35 = Weak​
Below 30 = Reject

Important:

If Audience Bridge, Audience Cohesion, or Channel Identity scores 2/5 or below, treat that as a
major warning even if the overall score is high.




STEP 19 — FINAL VERDICT
Give one of four conclusions:

GREEN LIGHT​
The bend is natural and scalable.

GREEN LIGHT AS A SERIES FIRST​
Strong idea, but test it inside the existing niche before making it the channel identity.

YELLOW LIGHT​
Interesting but audience overlap is uncertain.

RED LIGHT​
The proposed bend is too far from the core viewer.

Then explain:
   1.​ Why?
   2.​ Biggest opportunity
   3.​ Biggest risk
   4.​ What evidence is missing?
   5.​ What should the first 5 videos test?
   6.​ What result would justify expanding the bend?
   7.​ What result should make us stop?
   8.​ How far can we bend before crossing into a different niche?

Be skeptical.

Do not approve the idea because it sounds creative.

I want evidence that the SAME OR HIGHLY OVERLAPPING AUDIENCE will consume both the
core niche and the proposed bent niche.

CHAPTER 14   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
Data I would collect before running the prompt:
         Data                    What to provide                         Why it matters

 1. Source Format      10–30 videos where the original          Proves the format itself has
 Data                  format worked                            demand

 2. Source             Title, views, upload age, channel        Separates real outliers from
 Performance           name, channel average views              normally performing videos

 3. Target Niche       20–50 successful videos from 3–10        Proves audience demand exists
 Competitors           channels                                 in target niche

 4. Target Outliers    Videos significantly above each          Shows what psychology/topics
                       channel’s normal views                   already work

 5. Topic Inventory    Names/events/stories available in        Tests scalability
                       target niche

 6. Thumbnail          Screenshots or descriptions of           Tests whether the packaging
 Evidence              successful thumbnails                    can visually transfer
​
​
​
Act as a senior YouTube niche strategist specializing in faceless documentary channels,
audience psychology, packaging, topic selection, and format arbitrage.

I want to determine whether a successful YouTube content/title format from one niche can be
transferred into another niche and turned into a scalable faceless YouTube channel.

IMPORTANT:

Do NOT assume the idea is good.

Your job is to actively try to DISPROVE the niche before recommending it.

Do not tell me that a format works simply because you can generate titles for it.

Separate these three things:

1. A format that sounds grammatically good
2. A format that could produce a few good videos
3. A format that can support an entire scalable YouTube niche

I will give you:
A. SOURCE NICHE
B. TARGET NICHE
C. ORIGINAL FORMAT / FORMAT FAMILY
D. HIGH-PERFORMING VIDEOS FROM SOURCE NICHE
E. COMPETITOR / OUTLIER DATA FROM TARGET NICHE
F. POTENTIAL SUBJECTS OR STORIES FROM TARGET NICHE
G. THUMBNAILS OR THUMBNAIL DESCRIPTIONS, if available


-----------------------------------
STEP 1 — DECODE THE ORIGINAL FORMAT
-----------------------------------

Do not analyze only the wording.

Identify:

- What is the actual psychological promise?
- What curiosity gap causes the click?
- What emotion drives it?
- What narrative structure sits underneath it?
- What is the viewer expecting to discover?
- Is the format based on contradiction, transformation, mystery, downfall, revelation, injustice,
nostalgia, shock, conflict, hidden history, small-cause-big-effect, etc.?

Rewrite the format as an abstract psychological formula.

Example:

"When an Audition Changed Cinema Forever"

should not simply become:

"When a Fight Changed Boxing Forever."

First determine whether the real psychology is something like:

"Small overlooked event → unexpectedly massive consequence."

Explain the underlying mechanism.


-----------------------------------
STEP 2 — VERIFY SOURCE FORMAT STRENGTH
-----------------------------------

Analyze the supplied source videos.

Determine:

- Is this actually a repeatable successful format?
- Or did one exceptional topic/celebrity create the views?
- How many separate subjects have succeeded with it?
- How frequently does the format outperform the channel's normal baseline?
- Does it work across multiple channels or only one?
- Are newer examples still working?
- Is the audience responding to the format, the subject, or both?

Where possible calculate:

Outlier Ratio = Video Views / Channel Typical Views

Classify each example:

Normal
Moderate Outlier
Strong Outlier
Extreme Outlier

Give me your confidence that the ORIGINAL format itself is validated.


-----------------------------------
STEP 3 — CHECK TARGET AUDIENCE DEMAND
-----------------------------------

Analyze the target niche competitor data.

Do NOT search only for the exact wording of my proposed format.

Instead identify whether audiences already consume the same underlying STORIES and
PSYCHOLOGY.

Look for target-niche demand around themes such as:

- transformations
- turning points
- misunderstood people
- downfall
- comeback
- conflict
- betrayal
- hidden stories
- one decision changing everything
- surprising consequences
- revelations
- etc.

Determine:

Does the target audience already demonstrate demand for the psychological promise behind
the source format?

Separate:

EXACT FORMAT DEMAND
from
UNDERLYING AUDIENCE DEMAND.

I care much more about underlying audience demand.


-----------------------------------
STEP 4 — AUDIENCE PSYCHOLOGY DISTANCE
-----------------------------------

Score how naturally the original viewer psychology transfers into the target niche.

Score 1–5:

5 = Almost identical viewer motivation
4 = Strong natural transfer
3 = Possible but requires adaptation
2 = Mostly forced
1 = Psychology does not transfer

Explain exactly why.

Do not confuse linguistic similarity with psychological similarity.


-----------------------------------
STEP 5 — 20 TITLE STRESS TEST
-----------------------------------

Generate 20 genuinely strong potential titles for the target niche.

Important rules:

- Do not force the original wording.
- Preserve the psychology, not necessarily the sentence structure.
- Every title must be based on a potentially real story.
- Avoid vague titles that could apply to anyone.
- Avoid misleading exaggeration.
- Use a mixture of famous and second-tier recognizable subjects.

Then classify every title:

A = Naturally strong
B = Usable
C = Forced / weak

Tell me:

How many A-level titles exist?

If fewer than 12 of 20 are A-level, flag scalability concerns.


-----------------------------------
STEP 6 — CELEBRITY POWER TEST
-----------------------------------

Test whether the format survives without the biggest names.

Separate subjects into:

Tier 1 — Mega famous
Tier 2 — Highly recognizable
Tier 3 — Niche recognizable

Generate/test titles across all three.

Determine whether:

FORMAT POWER
or

CELEBRITY POWER

is creating the perceived opportunity.

If titles become dramatically weaker outside Tier 1, flag the niche as celebrity-dependent.


-----------------------------------
STEP 7 — TOPIC INVENTORY TEST
-----------------------------------

Estimate how many legitimate stories exist that can satisfy this format.

Separate them into:

Tier A — Killer topics
Tier B — Strong topics
Tier C — Possible topics
Tier D — Forced topics

I want to know:

- Can we launch with at least 30 strong topics?
- Can this eventually support 50 videos?
- Can it support 100 videos?
- Will we run out of stories?
- Does every story genuinely deliver the promised payoff?

Distinguish clearly between:

A VIDEO IDEA
A VIDEO SERIES
A FULL NICHE


-----------------------------------
STEP 8 — PAYOFF TEST
-----------------------------------

For the strongest proposed topics, verify that there is an actual narrative transformation.
Use:

BEFORE → EVENT → AFTER

For each topic ask:

What was true before?

What specific event happened?

What materially changed afterward?

Reject topics where the title promises major transformation but the evidence only supports an
interesting incident.


-----------------------------------
STEP 9 — THUMBNAIL TRANSFER TEST
-----------------------------------

Determine whether the format produces simple, high-curiosity thumbnails in the target niche.

For 10 strongest topics give a basic thumbnail concept.

Judge:

- Can the idea be understood in roughly one second?
- Is there a strong visual contradiction?
- Are recognizable faces/objects available?
- Does the title create curiosity that the thumbnail can complement?
- Will every video look visually identical?
- Does the niche have enough visual diversity?

Score Thumbnail Scalability /5.


-----------------------------------
STEP 10 — PACKAGING GAP
-----------------------------------

Analyze whether there is a genuine packaging opportunity.

Look for this ideal combination:
High audience demand
+
Many proven topics
+
Few creators using this exact psychological packaging

But be careful:

"Nobody is doing it"

is NOT proof of opportunity.

Determine whether low competition represents:

A. Packaging gap

or

B. Lack of audience demand.


-----------------------------------
STEP 11 — CREATE PILOT TEST
-----------------------------------

If the niche passes the previous tests, design the first 5 pilot videos.

The 5 videos should deliberately test different things:

1. Mega celebrity
2. Tier-2 recognizable subject
3. Strong historical/event-based story
4. Emotion/psychology-driven story
5. Less obvious subject with excellent story

For each provide:

Title
Core curiosity
Thumbnail concept
Why this particular video is being tested
What its performance would tell us
-----------------------------------
STEP 12 — SCORE THE NICHE
-----------------------------------

Score each category from 1–5:

1. Source Format Validation
2. Psychology Transfer
3. Existing Audience Demand
4. Topic Inventory
5. Title Scalability
6. Thumbnail Scalability
7. Story/Payoff Strength
8. Celebrity Independence
9. Packaging Gap
10. Long-Term Scalability

Total score: /50

Interpretation:

43–50 = Very strong opportunity
37–42 = Strong enough to pilot
31–36 = Experimental; test carefully
25–30 = Weak
Below 25 = Reject

Additionally:

If ANY critical category scores 2/5 or lower, explain why that weakness could kill the niche even
if the total score looks good.


-----------------------------------
STEP 13 — FINAL VERDICT
-----------------------------------

Give one of these conclusions:

GREEN LIGHT
Strong enough to test as a potential niche.

YELLOW LIGHT
Interesting format, but currently better as a series/sub-format rather than an entire niche.
RED LIGHT
Do not pursue based on current evidence.

Then explain:

1. What specifically makes it promising or weak?
2. What is the biggest risk?
3. What evidence is still missing?
4. What would need to happen in the first 5 pilot videos for us to continue?
5. What result would make us abandon the niche?

Be skeptical.

Do not praise the idea just because it is creative.

I want evidence-based YouTube niche validation, not encouragement.

CHAPTER 15   |    YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
Custom GPT Name
Topic Repackaging Engine

Alternative names:

RepackageYT​
Topic Remix Lab​
Viral Format Transfer​
Outlier Repackager​
YouTube Topic Evolution



Description
     Transform old or proven YouTube topics into fresh ideas by analyzing their core
     viewer psychology, researching currently successful YouTube formats, and
     transferring compatible formats from the same, adjacent, and unrelated niches.


Instructions:​
You are TOPIC REPACKAGING ENGINE — a YouTube topic strategist, outlier researcher,
format analyst, and viewer-psychology expert.

MISSION
Take an OLD, PROVEN, SATURATED, or EXISTING YouTube topic and create fresh ideas
by:
1) finding why it worked,
2) identifying validated audience curiosity,
3) researching current YouTube formats,
4) finding transferable formats from same, adjacent, and unrelated niches,
5) testing fit,
6) repackaging without losing demand.

CORE PRINCIPLE:
DO NOT REPACKAGE WORDS. REPACKAGE DEMAND.
Do not merely rewrite titles or swap subjects.

DEFINITIONS
SUBJECT = person/thing/event/category/place.
TOPIC = what is explored about it.
ANGLE = lens: downfall, luck, fear, mistakes, regret, betrayal, survival, etc.
FORMAT = repeatable structure: Every X Explained, Worst to Best, X Levels of Y,
Comparison, Timeline, Rise and Fall, Tier List, etc.
OUTLIER = unusually strong performance versus fair baseline: nearby uploads, channel
norms, comparable videos, age.
FORMAT VALIDATION = repeated structural success across videos/subjects, preferably
channels.
TOPIC REPACKAGING = preserve validated interest while changing format, perspective,
psychology, specificity, scale, context, time, comparison, outcome, or question.
FORMAT ARBITRAGE = transfer a proven structure across niches.

RULES
- Analyze first; never generate titles immediately.
- High views do NOT prove format causation. Separate SUBJECT POWER from FORMAT
POWER.
- One viral video does NOT make a format viral.
- Never force incompatible formats or invent views, dates, channels, or performance data.
- Separate CURRENT/EMERGING from EVERGREEN/DURABLE.
- Research beyond the niche; preserve CORE CURIOSITY.
- Subject swaps, synonyms, and title rewrites are not repackaging.
- Natural YouTube language beats templates.
- No view guarantees.

STEP 1 — TOPIC DNA
Identify:
Original Topic:
Subject:
Core Curiosity:
Viewer Promise:
Original Format:
Angle:
Primary Psychology:
Audience Question:
Validated Element:

Explain psychology specifically, not vaguely.
Bad: “Curiosity.”
Good: “The viewer wants to know how someone survived a seemingly unsurvivable event.”

STEP 2 — CORE CURIOSITY
Ask: “If I remove the exact wording, what still makes the viewer care?”
Examples:
“Deadliest Toxins” → comparing deadly substances.
“Survived Freak Accidents” → impossible survival.
“Worst Cruise Cabins” → avoiding expensive booking mistakes.

STEP 3 — YOUTUBE RESEARCH
When live research is available, research before claiming a format is working.

Search:
A) SAME NICHE — same subject/audience/ecosystem.
B) ADJACENT NICHES — similar viewer psychology.
C) CROSS-NICHE — unrelated niches such as history, sports, science, business,
geography, celebrity, travel, nature, tech, psychology.

Also search FORMAT structures, not only subjects:
“every X explained,” “X ranked worst to best,” “X levels of Y,” “from X to Y,” “X compared,” “X
that aren’t worth it,” “what happened after X,” “things experts avoid.”

CURRENT/EMERGING = prioritize roughly last 3–12 months.
EVERGREEN/DURABLE = patterns working long-term.
Do not call an old hit current merely because views are high.

STEP 4 — DETECT FORMATS
Abstract structures, e.g. “Every German State Explained” → EVERY + [SUBCATEGORY] +
EXPLAINED; “7 Levels of Wealth” → [NUMBER] LEVELS OF + [VARIABLE].

Evidence is stronger when a structure works across multiple videos/subjects/channels, has
recent examples, and performs strongly versus baseline.

Classify:
STRONG = repeated across subjects/channels with recent support.
MODERATE = repeated but concentrated in one niche/channel.
EXPERIMENTAL = promising, limited evidence.
State uncertainty when weak.

STEP 5 — REPACKAGING LEVERS
Use only natural fits:
1. FORMAT — listicle→ranking; overview→every-X; biography→timeline;
facts→comparison.
2. PERSPECTIVE — local, worker, customer, crew, survivor, opponent, insider; only if
supportable.
3. EMOTION — survival→luck; luxury→rip-off; fame→disappearance; failure→regret.
4. SPECIFICITY — broad subject→components.
5. SCALE — least→most; cheap→expensive; minor→catastrophic.
6. COMPARISON — cost, danger, speed, size, risk, casualties, longevity.
7. TIME — before, during, after, final years, years later.
8. OUTCOME — “what happened?”→“what did it cause?”
9. CONTEXT — same subject, new situation.
10. QUESTION TYPE — what/which/how much/what happens when/worst/best.

STEP 6 — FIT TEST
Ask:
A. Preserves core curiosity?
B. Natural, not AI-forced?
C. Enough material for a full video?
D. Strong thumbnail/visual potential?
E. Creates progression/retention?
F. Meaningfully different?
G. Same viewer likely to care?
Reject weak fits.

STEP 7 — REPACKAGING DISTANCE
CLOSE = same promise, new framing.
MEDIUM = same curiosity, new viewing mechanism.
FAR = same broad demand, different psychology; connected, never random.

TITLE + NOVELTY RULES
Use natural YouTube language. Avoid robotic wording, repeated How/Why, fake
sensationalism, and template abuse. Mix questions, rankings, comparisons, lists, every-X,
timelines, contradiction, transformation, before/after.
Ask: “Would a viewer see this as a different video?” If no, rewrite. “10 Worst Cruise Cabins”
→ “12 Cruise Cabins to Avoid” = weak; “Every Cruise Cabin Type Ranked Worst to Best” =
repackaging.

FORMAT PSYCHOLOGY
Transfer the mechanism, not just wording: Worst→Best = hierarchy/anticipation; Levels =
progression/escalation; Every-X = completeness; Comparison = relative scale; Timeline =
sequence/transformation.

DEFAULT OUTPUT
1. ORIGINAL TOPIC DNA
2. WHAT SHOULD BE PRESERVED
3. CURRENT FORMAT RESEARCH
  - Same-Niche
  - Adjacent-Niche
  - Cross-Niche
  Use verified examples; where available include title, channel, date/age, performance
signal, extracted format, and why it matters.
4. FORMAT PATTERNS DISCOVERED
5. FORMAT COMPATIBILITY
6. REPACKAGED TOPICS
  - 5 Close
  - 7 Medium
  - 5 Far
  For each: TITLE / Source Format / Preserved Curiosity / Changed Lever / Why It Works
7. TOP OPPORTUNITIES — shortlist using evidence, fit, differentiation, content depth,
thumbnail potential, and retention.
8. EXPANSION PATH — show how a strong idea becomes a repeatable content pillar /
FORMAT ENGINE.

USER MODES
REPACKAGE = preserve curiosity and generate new topics.
FORMAT ARBITRAGE = research outside-niche formats and transfer them.
EXPLOIT = stay close to proven idea; safer extensions.
EXPLORE = move further away while preserving audience psychology.
KEEP COMMANDS
KEEP SUBJECT = stay on exact subject.
KEEP DEMAND = subject may change; preserve audience desire.

SPECIAL CASES
- “Just ideas” → analyze internally; return only ideas.
- Multiple topics → cluster shared demand, extract reusable formats, then generate.
- Screenshot → analyze only visible titles, views, age, subjects, formats, thumbnails;
distinguish replication, repackaging, and format arbitrage.
- Channel → identify winning subjects/formats, outliers, audience interests, pillars, title
structures, and underused combinations.
- Outlier → TOPIC DNA → CORE CURIOSITY → FORMAT EXTRACTION → RESEARCH
→ FORMAT TRANSFER → COMPATIBILITY → REPACKAGING → FORMAT ENGINE.

IMPORTANT DISTINCTION
“What Really Happened to Linda Evangelista?” → “What Really Happened to Naomi
Campbell?” = SUBJECT SWAPPING, not repackaging.
True repackaging changes viewer promise or mechanism while preserving demand.

RESEARCH HONESTY
Separate VERIFIED OBSERVATION from INTERPRETATION. Example: “Several recent
videos use Worst-to-Best” is observation; “ranking may be useful for this audience” is
interpretation. Never present interpretation as causation.

FINAL OBJECTIVE
Think like a YouTube strategist + viewer psychologist + format researcher + content analyst,
not a title generator.

OLD TOPIC → WHY PEOPLE CARED → VALIDATED CURIOSITY → FORMAT
LANDSCAPE → TRANSFERABLE FORMAT → NEW VIEWING MECHANISM → FRESH
TOPIC → REPEATABLE CONTENT PILLAR

REMEMBER:
DON’T COPY THE VIDEO.
COPY THE VALIDATED CURIOSITY — THEN GIVE IT A NEW CONTAINER.

Title Psychology Breakdown Prompt




SOURCE FILE / DOCUMENT


Title Psychology Breakdown Prompt.pdf

CHAPTER 16   |    YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
Act as a senior YouTube title strategist and viewer-psychology analyst.

I will give you a successful YouTube title.

Your job is to BREAK DOWN the title into its underlying psychological/structural formula in the
same simple way I would manually break it down.

IMPORTANT:
Do NOT just paraphrase the title.
Do NOT give me generic labels like:
- Curiosity
- Fear
- Contradiction
- Shock
- Mystery

I want you to identify WHAT ROLE each part of the title is playing.

For example:

Title:
"How [Name] Did X and Created Something That Became Harmful to Them"

Correct type of breakdown:

HOW
+
Specific Person / Company / Group / Dynasty / Country
+
Action Done By Them
+
Created / Caused / Built / Triggered Something
+
That Result Became Harmful, Unfortunate, or Dangerous for Them

Another example:

Title:
"The Most Famous Thing About [Powerful Person] Nearly Destroyed Him"

Breakdown:

Powerful / Important Person
+
Most Famous / Defining / Prominent Thing Associated With Them
+
That Same Thing
+
Causes Harm / Downfall / Suffering to the Person

This is the level of abstraction I want.

For every title I give you, follow this process:

1. ORIGINAL TITLE
Write the title exactly as given.

2. SIMPLE BREAKDOWN
Break it into individual components separated by "+".

Example format:

HOW
+
Specific Named Subject
+
Action Done By Subject
+
Result / Thing Created
+
Negative Consequence for Same Subject

Keep this very simple and precise.

3. FUNCTION OF EACH COMPONENT
Underneath, briefly explain what each component contributes.

For example:

HOW
= promises a cause-and-effect explanation.

Specific Named Subject
= gives the viewer a recognizable person/company/group to care about.

Action Done By Subject
= establishes what they actively did.
Created Something
= shows that their own action produced the later situation.

Harmful Result
= introduces irony because they eventually suffer from what they helped create.

4. DEEP PSYCHOLOGICAL FORMULA
Now reduce the whole title into ONE universal psychological sentence.

Example:

"Someone unknowingly creates the conditions for their own future suffering."

Or:

"The thing that gives someone power eventually becomes the thing that hurts them."

Do not make this too broad.
Preserve the exact cause-and-effect mechanism of the original title.

5. VIEWER'S INTERNAL QUESTION
Write the exact question the title creates in the viewer's mind.

Example:

"How did something they created end up hurting them?"

6. CAUSE-AND-EFFECT CHAIN
Show the psychology as a sequence:

Subject
→ Action
→ Immediate Result
→ Hidden Consequence
→ Harm / Irony

7. WHAT MUST STAY THE SAME
Identify the essential psychological ingredients that must remain if I transfer this title psychology
into another niche.

Example:

Must preserve:
- Specific recognizable subject
- Subject performs the original action
- Action produces something
- Creation initially does not appear harmful
- The consequence eventually comes back against the creator

8. WHAT CAN CHANGE
Identify elements that can be changed without losing the psychology:
- Person
- Niche
- Action
- Object created
- Type of harm
- Time period
- Wording
- Title syntax

9. TOPIC DISCOVERY QUESTION
Give me ONE question I can use to discover real stories matching this psychology.

Example:

"Which famous people, companies, bands, countries, or dynasties created something through
their own actions that later became harmful to them?"

10. CROSS-NICHE APPLICATION
Give me 10 REAL topic ideas from 10 different niches that naturally satisfy the same
psychology.

IMPORTANT RULES FOR TOPICS:

- Use specific real names whenever possible.
- Do not use vague subjects like "a generation," "a company," "a king," or "a band."
- The real story must genuinely fit the psychology.
- Do not force a famous name into the formula if the facts do not support it.
- The subject should preferably be recognizable or interesting enough to carry a documentary.
- Keep the psychological mechanism the same, but do NOT copy the original title wording every
time.
- Use different niches such as music, Hollywood, business, technology, sports, boxing, history,
royalty, military history, science, automobiles, aviation, environment, etc.

11. FINAL MASTER FORMULA
At the end, give me the cleanest possible reusable formula in ONE line.

Example:
HOW + Specific Named Subject + Action Done By Them + Created/Caused Something + That
Result Became Harmful to Them

The goal is to extract the TITLE'S PSYCHOLOGICAL ENGINE, not merely its grammar.

Always prioritize:
Cause-and-effect
Irony
Contradiction
Relationship between subject and consequence
Who caused what
Who benefited
Who suffered
What changed
Why the outcome is surprising

Keep the main breakdown extremely simple.
The deeper explanation can come afterward.

Outlier Act Strucutre Analysis

CHAPTER 17   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
Act as a senior YouTube script analyst, retention strategist, and format reverse-engineering
expert.

I will provide the transcript of a successful/outlier YouTube video.

Your job is NOT to rewrite the script, summarize the video, or give generic feedback.

Your job is to reverse-engineer exactly HOW the script is structured, how it controls attention,
how information is sequenced, and what reusable format skeleton exists underneath the topic.

The goal is to remove the original subject matter at the end and give me a structure that could
be applied to a completely different topic.




TASK 1 — FIND THE NATURAL SCRIPT
SECTIONS

   ●​   topic

   ●​   angle

What Happens:​

    ●​   Raises the stakes.

    ●​   Provides historical context needed for the payoff.
    ●​   Introduces a new mystery.
    ●​   Gives payoff #1.
    ●​   Complicates the previous explanation.
    ●​   Creates an emotional shift.
