    ●​   Delivers the main thesis.

   ●​   one large open loop

   ●​   escalating questions
   ●​   delayed answers

   ●​   mystery
   ●​   cause-and-effect

   ●​   or another retention engine

   ●​   reveal

   ●​   jump forward/backward in time
   ●​   change from explanation to story
   ●​   warning that something bigger is coming

Create a table:

| Time / Location | Re-Hook Line or Paraphrase | Type | What It Makes Viewer Want to Know |

Do NOT label ordinary transitions as re-hooks.

Only include genuine attention resets.

At the end calculate approximately:

Average time between major re-hooks: X minutes/seconds.

Then tell me whether the script re-hooks:

   ●​ aggressively
   ●​ moderately
   ●​ lightly

and why.

    ●​   shock

Closer Formula:​
Reduce the ending to a reusable structural pattern.
TASK 8 — IDENTIFY THE SCRIPT'S ACT
STRUCTURE
Only AFTER analyzing the natural sections, combine them into broader narrative acts.

   ●​   5 acts

TASK 9 — CREATE THE FORMAT
SKELETON
This is the MOST IMPORTANT section.

Remove ALL original:

    ●​   names
    ●​   events
    ●​   dates
    ●​   places
    ●​   statistics
    ●​   movie titles
    ●​   historical figures
    ●​   examples
    ●​   specific facts

I want the winning script reduced to a reusable template.

Format it like this:

Hook

[What type of information appears here.]

Example structure:

Present the commonly accepted explanation.​
↓​
Introduce one contradiction that makes the explanation feel incomplete.​
↓​
Promise to investigate what actually happened.

Section/Act 2

Establish the necessary background without resolving the mystery.​
↓​
Introduce the first warning sign.​
↓​
End by showing that the problem began earlier than viewers expected.

Continue through the entire script.
Be SPECIFIC about the storytelling function while remaining CONTENT-NEUTRAL.

Bad skeleton:

"Give some background."​
"Talk about problems."​
"Give the answer."

Good skeleton:

"Establish why the subject was believed to be unusually safe or successful, so the later failure
creates a stronger contradiction."

"Introduce multiple warning signs but deliberately avoid explaining why they were ignored."

"Slow the timeline down around the decisive moment and reconstruct events almost
second-by-second."

"Reveal that the obvious cause was only the trigger, then expose the deeper mechanism that
made the outcome inevitable."

The skeleton should be detailed enough that I could give it to another writer with a completely
unrelated topic and they could reproduce the same narrative experience.




TASK 10 — CREATE A COMPRESSED
SKELETON
After the detailed skeleton, compress the entire video into ONE chain.

For example:

Familiar Story​
→ Contradiction​
→ Background​
→ Warning Signs​
→ Critical Moment​
→ Hidden Mechanism​
→ Consequences​
→ Reinterpretation

Do NOT copy this example unless it matches the transcript.
Create the actual chain used by the winning video.




TASK 11 — EXTRACT THE WRITER'S
RETENTION RULES
Based ONLY on evidence from the transcript, give me 5–10 rules the writer appears to follow.

Examples:

"Never completely answer a question without creating another."

"Context is introduced only when it becomes necessary to understand the next development."

"Major reveals are preceded by a simpler explanation that later gets complicated."

"Every few minutes the script changes either the question, stakes, or type of information."

Do not give generic YouTube advice.

Every rule must be traceable to something this particular script repeatedly does.




TASK 12 — TELL ME WHAT NOT TO COPY
Separate:

FORMAT — Worth Copying

Reusable elements such as:

   ●​   information sequence
   ●​   curiosity structure
   ●​   pacing
   ●​   question progression
   ●​   reveal placement
   ●​   transition style
   ●​   escalation
   ●​   ending method
CONTENT — Do Not Copy

Elements specific to this video's subject such as:

   ●​   facts
   ●​   examples
   ●​   wording
   ●​   characters
   ●​   exact jokes
   ●​   phrases
   ●​   analogies
   ●​   unique research

This distinction is important because I want FORMAT ARBITRAGE, not content copying.




FINAL OUTPUT
At the very end give me this concise summary:

Name the script's underlying format in 2–5 words.

Primary Retention Engine:​
What keeps viewers watching?

Number of Natural Sections:​
X

X

Approximately X.

Approximately X% through the video.

Describe in one sentence.

Write the whole structure in one line using arrows.

payoff placement than prose style.

Act as a senior YouTube script analyst and retention strategist.

CHAPTER 19   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
MASTER PROMPT: YouTube Topic-to-Title Psychology & Format Engine

YOUR ROLE

Act as an elite YouTube Title Strategist, Viewer Psychology Analyst, Content Packaging
Expert, and Documentary Story Architect.

You specialize in converting simple topics, facts, observations, stories, or rough ideas into
highly clickable, psychologically compelling YouTube titles.

You understand the difference between:

1. Core Story: What is actually happening?
2. Psychological Angle: Why would an audience feel compelled to watch?
3. Title Format: How should that psychological angle be expressed?

Your job is NOT to simply generate synonyms, rearrange sentences, or attach triggering
words.

Your job is to discover multiple meaningful ways to package the same underlying topic.

---

INPUT

I may provide any ONE of the following:

- A simple topic
- A historical fact
- A one-line observation
- A rough YouTube title
- A person's name and an incident
- A story or event
- A specific subject
- An interesting contradiction
- A successful title I want to develop further

Optional context:

- Target audience: [If provided]
- Niche: [If provided]
- Content style: [Documentary / Explainer / Listicle / Storytelling / Other]
- Video duration: [If provided]
- Preferred tone: [If provided]

IMPORTANT: Do not require all these details. Even if I provide only 3–5 words, begin the
analysis using what is known.
If the input is too broad, propose plausible story directions without pretending that any
unverified incident is established fact.

Do not invent scandals, deaths, losses, quotes, decisions, motivations, or outcomes.

---

PHASE 1: UNDERSTAND THE CORE TOPIC

Before generating titles, analyze the input.

Identify:

1. Subject

Who or what is the main focus?

It could be a person, country, company, historical event, object, concept, place, or group.

2. Situation

What is the subject's relevant condition, environment, role, achievement, or circumstance?

3. Trigger

What is the most attention-worthy development, behavior, decision, contrast, event, or
characteristic?

Find something meaningful. Do not force an artificial dramatic trigger.

4. Tension

What makes the story interesting?

Look for conflicts such as:

- Expectation vs Reality
- Success vs Failure
- Strength vs Weakness
- Public Image vs Private Reality
- Power vs Vulnerability
- Decision vs Consequence
- Past vs Present
- Ambition vs Limitation
- Opportunity vs Mistake
- Normal Behavior vs Unusual Outcome

5. Promise
What specific understanding, explanation, revelation, comparison, or outcome should the
audience expect from watching?

The promise must be deliverable by a real video about this topic.

Required Output

Present the five components in a clear table.

Then write:

- Core Story: One sentence summarizing the actual topic.
- Central Conflict: The strongest established or plausible tension.
- Primary Viewer Question: The main question that could motivate someone to watch.
- Core Video Promise: What the video should actually deliver.

Distinguish known facts from assumptions or angles requiring research.

---

PHASE 2: DISCOVER DIFFERENT PSYCHOLOGICAL ANGLES

Now analyze the SAME topic from different viewer-psychology perspectives.

Do not immediately generate titles.

First, discover the different questions viewers might want answered.

Explore these psychological mechanisms:

1. Contradiction: Two seemingly incompatible things existing together.
2. Mystery / Missing Explanation: Something the viewer does not yet understand.
3. Unexpected Consequence: A decision or event producing a surprising outcome.
4. Hidden Cost: The sacrifices, disadvantages, or consequences behind something admired.
5. Reversal: A significant change in position, expectations, or fortunes.
6. Self-Destruction: A person's own behavior potentially undermining their success.
7. Comparison: Two competing decisions, qualities, people, or outcomes.
8. Power and Vulnerability: Something apparently powerful having an unexpected weakness.
9. Outsider / Rule Breaker: Someone succeeding or failing while rejecting accepted norms.
10. Transformation: How something or someone moved from one condition to another.
11. Forbidden / Overlooked Knowledge: A lesser-known but verifiable part of the story.
12. Completeness / Mastery: The desire to understand an entire subject, sequence, or
category.
13. Irony: An action or advantage producing the opposite of what was expected.
14. Cause and Effect: How a specific factor produced a major result.
15. Human Behavior: The motivations, incentives, fears, and choices behind an event.
These are candidate lenses, not mandatory categories.

Select the 10 strongest genuinely distinct psychological angles for the supplied topic.

You may invent additional relevant mechanisms.

Do not force weak, repetitive, or factually unsupported angles merely to reach a number.
Explain if fewer than 10 are defensible.

For EACH angle explain:

- Psychological mechanism
- Specific aspect of the topic being emphasized
- What the viewer would naturally wonder
- What emotional or intellectual tension creates interest
- What promise the video must fulfill
- What evidence would be needed to support the angle

Critical Requirement

Different psychology must mean a different reason to watch.

For example:

"How X Became Successful"

and

"Why X Became Successful"

may use different opening words but essentially the same psychology.

Do not treat them as meaningfully different unless the actual viewer question or promise
changes.

---

PHASE 3: APPLY DIFFERENT TITLE FORMATS

For EACH psychological angle, create THREE titles using different structural formats.

Choose formats that naturally support the psychological mechanism.

Possible title formats include:

- How + Subject + Action + Unexpected Outcome
- Why + Subject + Unusual Behavior or Result
- When + Event + Changed Everything
- The Real Reason + Specific Outcome
- What Really Happened to + Subject
- How + Strength + Became a Weakness
- The Man / Woman Who + Unusual Action
- Subject + Did X + But Y Happened
- Despite X + Subject Achieved Y
- The Hidden Cost of + Something
- The Strange Story of + Specific Event
- X vs Y + Meaningful Conflict
- Every + Specific Category + Explained
- Every + Major Decision / Mistake + Ranked
- The Rise and Fall of + Subject
- From X to Y + Transformation
- The Biggest Mistake + Subject Made
- The One Decision That + Specific Outcome
- What Made + Subject + So Different
- Why Nobody Expected + Specific Outcome
- The Problem With + Specific Subject
- What X Reveals About Y
- The Untold Story Behind + Event

You may create new structural formulas if they fit better.

Important Format Rules

1. Do not force every topic into How, Why, or The Real Reason.
2. Include declarative, explanatory, comparative, narrative, and list-based formats where
relevant.
3. Do not make all titles negative.
4. Mix positive, negative, neutral, surprising, and analytical perspectives.
5. Avoid generic phrases such as "Shocked the World" unless genuinely appropriate and
substantiated.
6. Preserve specificity, natural language, and readability.
7. Trigger words should enhance an existing tension, not manufacture one.
8. Prefer recognizable people, concrete events, actions, and consequences.
9. Never invent statistics or factual claims.
10. Avoid titles that promise revelations the script cannot support.
11. Do not create three titles with essentially the same psychological hook.
12. Make titles feel like real professional YouTube titles, not AI-generated keyword
combinations.

Required Output Format

For EACH selected psychological angle provide:

Psychology: Name of mechanism

Viewer Question: What does the viewer want to know?
Angle: What specific direction are we taking?

Title A: [Title]

Formula A: [Structural breakdown]

Title B: [Title]

Formula B: [Structural breakdown]

Title C: [Title]

Formula C: [Structural breakdown]

Video Promise: What the content must deliver.

Produce up to 30 titles across 10 meaningful psychological angles.

---

PHASE 4: EXPAND INTO ALTERNATIVE CONTENT FORMATS

Now consider whether the topic can support different video CONTENT formats, not merely
different title wording.

Explore these where appropriate:

- Story-driven documentary
- Explanation / analysis
- Character study
- Cause-and-effect breakdown
- Listicle
- Ranking / tier list
- Comparison
- Chronological timeline
- Decisions and consequences
- Rise / fall / comeback
- Myth vs reality
- Complete overview ("Every...")

Suggest up to 5 strong content-format opportunities.

For each provide:

- Content format
- Recommended title
- Why this format fits the topic
- What information the video would need
- Whether the idea can reasonably support the requested video duration, if supplied

Do not claim an idea can support a long video without enough substantive material.

---

PHASE 5: TITLE SCORING AND RANKING

Select the TOP 10 titles from all candidates.

Score each title from 1–10 on:

1. Curiosity and unanswered question
2. Specificity
3. Psychological tension
4. Clarity and natural wording
5. Audience relevance
6. Credibility and ability to deliver the promise

Calculate an overall score out of 60.

Provide a ranked table containing:

- Rank
- Title
- Psychological Angle
- Title Format
- Score /60

Scores are strategic editorial judgments, not predictions of actual CTR.

Then identify:

Best Overall Title: Strongest combination of audience interest, clarity and story fit.

Best Curiosity Title: Strongest curiosity gap without misinformation.

Best Documentary Title: Best suited to a coherent long-form story.

Best Explainer Title: Best suited to analysis and explanation.

Best Listicle / Ranking Title: Only if the topic genuinely supports this format.

Briefly explain the strategic reason for each selection.

---
PHASE 6: FINAL STRATEGIC RECOMMENDATION

For the THREE strongest titles, provide:

1. Final title
2. Five-step formula breakdown
3. Psychological mechanism
4. Structural title formula
5. Central argument
6. Triggering element
7. Viewer promise
8. Expected payoff
9. Video angle
10. Essential facts or evidence needed
11. Potential thumbnail concept (simple and direct)

Finally recommend ONE title to test first, stating your assumptions about the audience and
why it is worth testing.

---

QUALITY CONTROL

Before delivering the output, internally review all generated titles.

Reject or revise any title that:

- Is only a synonym variation.
- Has no clear subject or conflict.
- Uses a weak or artificial psychological hook.
- Makes unsupported accusations or false historical claims.
- Creates an impossible viewer promise.
- Changes the factual subject of the original input without explanation.
- Uses generic clickbait instead of specific intrigue.
- Would likely require completely unrelated content to satisfy the promise.

You must balance clickability + factual credibility + audience relevance + deliverable payoff.

Be creative with angles, disciplined with facts, and strategic with formats.

---

OUTPUT LANGUAGE

Generate YouTube titles in natural, fluent English unless I specifically request another
language.

Keep explanations clear, practical, and understandable for a YouTube creator.
Avoid unnecessary academic terminology.

---

MY INPUT

Topic / Fact / One-Line Idea:

[PASTE YOUR TOPIC HERE]
MASTER PROMPT — CENTRAL ARGUMENT & VIEWER PROMISE DEVELOPMENT

YOUR ROLE

Act as a Senior YouTube Documentary Strategist, Narrative Architect, Viewer Psychology
Analyst, and Professional Script Development Expert.

You specialize in transforming YouTube video titles into powerful central arguments, clear
viewer promises, compelling narrative conflicts, and satisfying story payoffs.

You understand that a successful documentary or explainer is not simply a collection of
interesting facts.

It is built around a strong central idea that gives every piece of information a purpose.

Your task is to identify what the video is really about, what it should explore or establish, why
the audience should care, and exactly what the video must deliver.

---

INPUT

I will provide ONLY a YouTube video title.

VIDEO TITLE:

[INSERT TITLE HERE]

You must develop the strategic foundation independently.

Do not ask me to provide a central argument, promise, audience psychology, or story
structure.

If useful, identify likely audience expectations from the title, but do not invent specific
audience demographics.

---

PHASE 1 — UNDERSTAND THE TITLE'S REAL MEANING

Analyze the title beyond its surface-level wording.

Identify:

1. Main Subject

Who or what is this video fundamentally about?
2. Core Situation

What is happening, or what circumstance is being examined?

3. Primary Trigger

What specific action, event, condition, contradiction, or unusual detail makes the topic worth
exploring?

4. Underlying Tension

What creates the central conflict or unanswered question?

Look for meaningful tensions such as:

- Success vs Failure
- Expectations vs Reality
- Strength vs Weakness
- Power vs Vulnerability
- Decision vs Consequence
- Public Image vs Private Reality
- Luxury vs Hidden Operations
- Ambition vs Limitations
- Advantage vs Disadvantage
- Talent vs Discipline
- Appearance vs Reality
- Cause vs Unexpected Effect

Do not force a dramatic conflict when the topic is primarily explanatory.

5. Psychological Curiosity

Why would a viewer click this specific title?

Identify the precise question or expectation created by the wording.

Avoid generic explanations like "The title creates curiosity."

Explain what the viewer is actually curious about.

Required Output

Provide a brief analysis covering the subject, situation, trigger, tension, and psychological
curiosity.

---

PHASE 2 — DEVELOP THREE DISTINCT CENTRAL ARGUMENTS
The central argument is the main idea the entire video will explore, explain, support, or test.

It must be more specific than the topic itself.

Understand the Difference

Topic:
John Daly's lifestyle and golf career.

Weak Central Argument:
"John Daly was a golfer who enjoyed partying."

Strong Central Argument:
"John Daly's extraordinary natural talent helped him succeed at golf despite a lifestyle that
frequently conflicted with the discipline expected of elite professional athletes."

The strong argument gives the script a meaningful direction.

Generate THREE Central Arguments

Argument A — Direct Explanation

Focus on the most logical explanation or mechanism behind the title.

Argument B — Psychological / Human Conflict

Focus on the contradiction, decision-making, motivation, struggle, or human element.

Argument C — Deeper / Unexpected Perspective

Find a less obvious but defensible interpretation that adds intellectual depth to the topic.

For EACH Central Argument, Provide:

1. Central Argument: One clear, specific, powerful sentence.
2. Core Idea: What this argument is fundamentally trying to communicate.
3. Main Conflict or Question: What keeps the story interesting.
4. Why It Matters: Why the audience should care.
5. Story Direction: What the video would primarily investigate or explain.
6. Evidence Required: What facts, events, comparisons, or sources would be necessary to
develop and support the argument.
7. Strength: Rate its suitability for the supplied title out of 10, with a short justification.

Central Argument Rules

- Do not simply paraphrase the title.
- Do not write vague statements.
- Make all three arguments meaningfully different.
- Preserve the actual subject and scope of the title.
- Avoid introducing unrelated stories.
- Do not invent facts, motives, or outcomes.
- Do not create false controversies.
- Distinguish established facts from interpretations requiring evidence.
- A central argument can be explanatory rather than dramatic.
- Do not decide the conclusion of a genuinely unresolved question before examining
evidence.

---

PHASE 3 — DEFINE THE VIEWER PROMISE

The viewer promise is what someone expects to receive after clicking on the title.

This is NOT a summary of what the video discusses.

It is the specific answer, explanation, understanding, revelation, or conclusion the viewer
expects to gain.

Analyze the promise at THREE levels.

1. Explicit Promise

What does the title directly promise?

What question must be answered for the video to feel relevant?

2. Implicit Promise

What additional expectations does the title naturally create?

What does the viewer reasonably expect the video to explain, reveal, compare, or establish
beyond the literal words?

Do not invent expectations that the title does not reasonably support.

3. Emotional / Intellectual Promise

What experience or understanding should the viewer gain?

Examples:

- Surprise
- Fascination
- Understanding
- Suspense
- Perspective
- Emotional connection
- Clarity
- A satisfying explanation
- A reconsideration of an existing belief

For EACH of the Three Central Arguments:

Create one tailored viewer promise that explains what the corresponding version of the video
must deliver.

Make the promise specific and achievable.

---

PHASE 4 — IDENTIFY THE CORE NARRATIVE TENSION

A strong video needs a reason for viewers to remain interested after the initial hook.

Identify:

A. Primary Narrative Tension

What is the biggest contradiction, obstacle, question, conflict, or complexity the video
explores?

B. Secondary Tensions

Identify 3–5 supporting questions or complications that deepen the central topic.

C. Stakes

Why does the outcome or explanation matter?

What does the subject stand to gain, lose, reveal, change, or help us understand?

If the topic does not involve danger, failure, or personal stakes, identify its intellectual or
practical significance instead.

D. The Knowledge Gap

What does the viewer understand at the beginning, and what important understanding is
missing?

E. Curiosity Progression

Explain how the audience's interest could naturally evolve from an initial question into a
deeper understanding.
Do not develop an act-by-act structure.

---

PHASE 5 — DESIGN THE EXPECTED PAYOFF

A payoff is the meaningful answer or understanding the video eventually delivers.

The title creates expectations.

The central argument establishes the direction.

The payoff completes the promise.

Develop the following:

1. Primary Payoff

What is the most important answer, explanation, or conclusion the video must provide?

2. Supporting Payoffs

Identify 3–5 smaller discoveries, explanations, or realizations that would make the viewing
experience satisfying.

3. Final Understanding

After watching the complete video, what should the viewer understand differently?

Express this in one powerful sentence.

4. Promise Fulfillment Test

Answer:

"If a viewer clicked this title and watched the entire video, what exactly must they have
learned or experienced to feel that their time was worthwhile?"

5. Potential Disappointment

What could make the video feel misleading, incomplete, repetitive, or irrelevant to its title?

Explain what the future script must avoid.

Important: Do not invent a dramatic revelation merely because the title sounds mysterious.
If the evidence may support several outcomes, frame the payoff as a question to resolve
rather than a predetermined conclusion.

---

PHASE 6 — CHOOSE THE STRONGEST STRATEGIC DIRECTION

Compare the three proposed central arguments.

Evaluate them on:

1. Alignment with the original title
2. Strength of the central idea
3. Psychological interest
4. Narrative development potential
5. Specificity and clarity
6. Ability to fulfill the viewer promise
7. Factual credibility and research feasibility

Score each dimension out of 10.

Present a comparison table with total scores out of 70.

These scores represent strategic judgment, not predicted audience retention or video
performance.

Make a Final Recommendation

Select ONE central argument.

Explain:

- Why it is the strongest choice.
- Why it fulfills the title most directly.
- What makes it more compelling than the other two.
- What kind of video it would naturally support.
- What must be researched or verified before committing to the conclusion.

Do not automatically select the most dramatic interpretation.

Choose the most compelling defensible direction.

---

PHASE 7 — FINAL SCRIPT FOUNDATION

Now combine the strongest elements into a concise, practical creative brief.
Use this exact format:

VIDEO TITLE:
[Original title]

SELECTED CENTRAL ARGUMENT:
[One specific sentence explaining the main idea the video will explore or establish.]

PRIMARY VIEWER PROMISE:
[Exactly what the viewer expects the video to deliver.]

IMPLICIT PROMISE:
[The additional expectation naturally created by the title.]

MAIN PSYCHOLOGICAL TRIGGER:
[The specific psychological mechanism that makes the concept interesting.]

CORE NARRATIVE TENSION:
[The main conflict, contradiction, obstacle, or question.]

PRIMARY VIEWER QUESTION:
[The one central question the viewer wants answered.]

SECONDARY VIEWER QUESTIONS:
[3–5 supporting questions.]

NARRATIVE DIRECTION:
[A short paragraph describing the central journey of discovery or explanation.]

PRIMARY PAYOFF:
[The most important answer the video must deliver.]

SUPPORTING PAYOFFS:
[3–5 meaningful answers or discoveries.]

FINAL TAKEAWAY:
[One memorable statement expressing what viewers should understand by the end.]

ESSENTIAL RESEARCH REQUIREMENTS:
[The important evidence that must be established before writing.]

WHAT THE SCRIPT MUST AVOID:
[Specific pitfalls that could break the viewer promise.]

---

QUALITY CONTROL RULES
Before submitting your output, check the following:

1. Is the central argument more meaningful than simply restating the title?
2. Does the viewer promise directly match the title?
3. Does the argument create a coherent direction for a complete video?
4. Are the three arguments genuinely different?
5. Is the psychological tension natural rather than manufactured?
6. Is the payoff specific enough to guide the eventual script?
7. Are claims factually grounded or clearly identified as requiring verification?
8. Does the central argument avoid overpromising?
9. Could a scriptwriter use the final creative brief without guessing what the video should be
about?
10. Would the eventual viewer feel satisfied if the identified promise were delivered?

Revise weak elements before presenting them.

---

IMPORTANT RESTRICTIONS

- DO NOT write the complete script.
- DO NOT create an act structure.
- DO NOT generate a scene-by-scene outline.
- DO NOT suggest footage or editing instructions.
- DO NOT write an opening hook.
- DO NOT create titles different from the supplied title.
- DO NOT manufacture facts, motivations, events, or revelations.
- DO NOT use unnecessary academic language.
- DO NOT provide generic advice about YouTube.
- DO NOT expand into unrelated subjects.

Focus entirely on developing the central argument, viewer promise, narrative tension, and
expected payoff.

Write in clear, professional English.

Be specific, analytical, creative, and practical.

YOUR FINAL OUTPUT MUST MAKE IT EASY TO PROCEED DIRECTLY TO THE NEXT
SCRIPT DEVELOPMENT STEP: ACT STRUCTURE SELECTION.

---

MY VIDEO TITLE:

[PASTE YOUR TITLE HERE]
MASTER PROMPT — ADVANCED YOUTUBE ACT STRUCTURE & NARRATIVE
ARCHITECTURE ENGINE

YOUR ROLE

Act as an Elite YouTube Documentary Narrative Architect, Professional Story Structure
Designer, Audience Retention Strategist, and Long-Form Content Development Expert.

You specialize in transforming approved video titles, central arguments, and viewer promises
into compelling, well-organized narrative structures.

Your expertise includes:

- Long-form YouTube documentaries
- Historical documentaries
- Biographical storytelling
- Sports documentaries
- Investigative storytelling
- Educational explainers
- Psychological documentaries
- Luxury, aviation, geography, and cruise-ship content
- Ranking and listicle videos
- Comparison and cause-and-effect documentaries

Your task is to develop multiple ACT STRUCTURES and identify the one most capable of
delivering the video's central argument and viewer promise while maintaining audience
engagement.

CORE PRINCIPLE:

A great narrative structure is not simply a topic divided into chapters.

Every act must have a distinct narrative purpose.

Each act should meaningfully advance the viewer's understanding, create or resolve relevant
questions, and contribute to the video's final payoff.

---

INPUT INFORMATION

I may provide the following:

VIDEO TITLE:
[INSERT TITLE]

APPROVED CENTRAL ARGUMENT:
[INSERT FROM STEP 2]
PRIMARY VIEWER PROMISE:
[INSERT FROM STEP 2]

CORE NARRATIVE TENSION:
[INSERT FROM STEP 2]

EXPECTED FINAL PAYOFF:
[INSERT FROM STEP 2]

OPTIONAL INFORMATION:

- Target audience: [INSERT]
- Target video duration: [INSERT]
- Target spoken word count: [INSERT]
- Content format: [DOCUMENTARY / EXPLAINER / LISTICLE / COMPARISON / OTHER]
- Additional story details or research: [INSERT]
- Important constraints: [INSERT]

If I provide only the title, infer a provisional central argument and promise before developing
the structures. Clearly label your assumptions.

If I provide an approved central argument or viewer promise, preserve them.

Do not casually replace the approved direction.

If the supplied argument contains unsupported factual assumptions, identify them and build
the structures around questions that can be investigated rather than treating those
assumptions as established facts.

---

PHASE 1 — UNDERSTAND THE STORY BEFORE STRUCTURING IT

Analyze the provided information and identify:

1. Core Story

What is this video fundamentally trying to communicate?

2. Central Argument

What main idea must the video explore, explain, support, or test?

3. Viewer Promise

What must the audience receive by the end?
4. Main Narrative Tension

What conflict, contradiction, unanswered question, problem, or mechanism makes the topic
interesting?

5. Narrative Material

What kinds of information could reasonably form the story?

Examples:

- Important events
- Decisions
- Conflicts
- Causes
- Consequences
- Comparisons
- Discoveries
- Explanations
- Turning points
- Historical context
- Human experiences

6. Natural Narrative Progression

Determine how the story should logically develop.

Possible progressions include:

- Cause → Action → Consequence
- Mystery → Evidence → Explanation
- Success → Conflict → Decline
- Problem → Mechanism → Solution
- Expectation → Contradiction → Reality
- Past → Turning Point → Present
- Individual Experience → Larger System
- Question → Investigation → Answer
- Comparison → Evidence → Conclusion

Choose the progression that best fits the actual subject.

Required Output

Provide a brief Narrative Foundation Summary covering:

- Core story
- Central argument
- Viewer promise
- Main tension
- Most suitable narrative progression
- Likely content format

Do not repeat a lengthy title analysis already completed in Step 2.

---

PHASE 2 — DEVELOP FIVE DIFFERENT ACT STRUCTURES

Create FIVE complete alternative narrative structures:

1. 6-Act Structure
2. 7-Act Structure
3. 8-Act Structure
4. 9-Act Structure
5. 10-Act Structure

Each structure must be professionally developed.

Do not take one structure and mechanically divide its sections to create another.

Each option should represent a meaningful difference in narrative pacing, emphasis, depth,
or progression.

For example:

A 6-act structure may emphasize direct storytelling and efficient progression.

An 8-act structure may provide more room for context, multiple developments, escalation,
and a major payoff.

A 10-act structure may support a complex story containing multiple significant stages,
discoveries, or consequences.

However, these are possibilities, not fixed rules.

Select the actual design according to the title and available material.

---

PHASE 3 — DEVELOP EVERY ACT WITH A CLEAR PURPOSE

For EACH act in EACH structure, provide:

A. Act Number and Title

Give the act a meaningful title that describes its unique role in the video.
Avoid vague labels such as:

- More Information
- The Next Part
- Additional Details
- More Problems
- More Facts

The title should make it clear what changes or develops during that act.

B. Narrative Purpose

Explain why this act exists.

What specific contribution does it make to the central argument?

C. Main Content Direction

Briefly identify what the act should cover.

This should be a strategic content description, not the complete script.

D. Primary Viewer Question

What specific question is the audience expecting this act to answer?

E. Psychological Engagement

Identify the primary mechanism keeping the viewer interested.

Examples:

- Curiosity
- Contrast
- Suspense
- Discovery
- Human conflict
- Unexpected consequences
- Explanation
- Comparison
- Escalation
- Anticipation

Do not force emotional drama where intellectual curiosity would be more appropriate.

F. Act Payoff
What meaningful understanding, answer, discovery, or development should the viewer
receive before the act ends?

G. Forward Momentum

What naturally motivates the viewer to continue into the next act?

This might be:

- An unresolved consequence
- A new question
- A deeper explanation
- An important contradiction
- A meaningful turning point
- A necessary next stage

Do not use artificial cliffhangers when no real unanswered question exists.

Required Act Format

ACT 1 — [SPECIFIC TITLE]

- Purpose: ...
- Content Direction: ...
- Viewer Question: ...
- Psychological Engagement: ...
- Act Payoff: ...
- Forward Momentum: ...

Repeat this for every act in all five proposed structures.

---

PHASE 4 — DESIGN A STRONG OPENING ACT

For each proposed structure, pay special attention to Act 1.

The first act must immediately justify the audience's decision to click.

It should:

1. Connect directly with the title.
2. Establish the central curiosity or conflict.
3. Make the subject understandable.
4. Create an important viewer question.
5. Give enough context to understand the stakes.
6. Establish the direction of the video.
7. Naturally lead into the next act.
Possible opening approaches include:

- Start with a surprising contradiction.
- Begin at a decisive moment.
- Introduce an unusual verified event.
- Establish a major problem.
- Present two apparently incompatible facts.
- Reveal the scale of an overlooked system.
- Start with an unexpected consequence.
- Introduce a question the video will investigate.

Choose the approach most appropriate to the topic.

IMPORTANT:

Do not write the actual opening hook or narration yet.

Only design the opening act's narrative function.

Avoid beginning with unnecessary background information.

---

PHASE 5 — DESIGN THE MIDDLE ACTS FOR RETENTION

The middle section is where many documentary scripts become repetitive or lose direction.

Design the middle acts so they continually advance the viewer's understanding.

Each Middle Act Must Do at Least One of These:

- Introduce an important new development.
- Explain a necessary mechanism.
- Present meaningful evidence.
- Increase the complexity of the original question.
- Reveal an important contradiction.
- Explain an unexpected consequence.
- Compare two competing factors.
- Change the viewer's understanding.
- Develop the central argument.
- Answer a question and introduce a relevant new one.

Escalation Rules

Escalation does not always mean increasing danger or drama.

It can mean:
- Greater understanding
- More complicated consequences
- Increasingly significant evidence
- Stronger contrasts
- Broader implications
- More revealing comparisons
- A shift from a simple explanation to a deeper one

The viewer should feel that each act adds something new.

Avoid:

- Repeating the same conflict.
- Recycling the same facts.
- Repeatedly making the same emotional point.
- Delaying the answer unnecessarily.
- Including background simply to increase length.
- Introducing unrelated subtopics.
- Creating false turning points.

Information Release

Plan the order in which important information should be introduced.

The narrative should generally progress through:

Promise → Understanding → Development → Escalation → Payoff → Closure

Adapt this progression to the topic rather than following it mechanically.

The audience must receive meaningful information throughout the video.

Do not withhold all important answers until the end.

---

PHASE 6 — DESIGN THE CLIMAX & FINAL ACT

Identify where the strongest narrative or intellectual payoff should occur.

The climax may be:

- A decisive historical event.
- A major reversal.
- The consequence of a critical decision.
- A revelation supported by evidence.
- The resolution of a central mystery.
- A clear explanation of a complex system.
- The most meaningful comparison.
- The strongest understanding of the central argument.

Not every explainer or listicle requires a dramatic climax.

The payoff must emerge naturally from the available evidence.

Final Act Requirements

The final act must:

1. Fulfill the central viewer promise.
2. Resolve the most important question.
3. Connect the conclusion to the central argument.
4. Explain the significance of the story.
5. Provide a satisfying sense of completion.

Avoid using the final act exclusively for a generic summary.

Do not repeat everything already explained.

Do not create an artificial twist merely to make the ending dramatic.

Do not include a YouTube call to action.

---

PHASE 7 — MATCH THE STRUCTURE TO THE VIDEO FORMAT

The most effective act structure depends on the kind of video being created.

Apply the following principles.

A. Character-Driven Documentary

Prioritize:

- Defining contradiction
- Background necessary to understand the character
- Important decisions
- Internal or external conflict
- Major developments
- Consequences
- Final interpretation

Do not automatically narrate the subject's entire biography.
B. Historical Documentary

Prioritize:

- Context
- Causes
- Historical constraints
- Important people and decisions
- Turning points
- Consequences
- Historical significance

Maintain factual chronology where necessary.

Do not invent dramatic events for structural convenience.

C. Explanatory Documentary

Prioritize:

- Central question
- Initial explanation
- Important mechanisms
- Supporting evidence
- Deeper complexity
- Real-world implications
- Complete understanding

Create progression through knowledge rather than invented conflict.

D. Listicle / Ranking

Prioritize:

- Clear organizing principle
- Distinct entries or item groups
- Logical ranking criteria
- Variety between entries
- Increasing significance or curiosity where justified
- Strong final entries
- Satisfying overall conclusion

Acts may contain groups of items rather than one act per item.

Do not turn a 10-item list into 10 acts automatically.

E. Comparison Documentary
Prioritize:

- Comparison question
- Fair criteria
- Relevant context
- Strengths
- Weaknesses
- Important contrasts
- Consequences
- Supported conclusion

F. Investigation / Mystery

Prioritize:

- Initial mystery
- Available evidence
- Competing explanations
- Contradictions
- Discoveries
- Evidence-based resolution

Never present unsupported speculation as confirmed fact.

---

PHASE 8 — PACING & ACT DISTRIBUTION

If I provide a target video duration, estimate the total spoken-word budget.

Suggested narration speed:

- Slow documentary: 125–140 words per minute
- Standard documentary: 140–155 words per minute
- Faster explainer: 155–165 words per minute

Adjust according to the target audience, content style, and planned pauses.

For each structure, estimate:

- Percentage of total runtime allocated to each act
- Approximate duration per act
- Approximate spoken-word allocation per act

The act percentages must add up to 100%.

Do not distribute time equally unless the content genuinely supports it.
For example:

- An opening act may require less time.
- Context may need moderate time.
- Complex explanations may require more time.
- Major developments may need additional room.
- The final act should provide a complete payoff without unnecessary repetition.

If no target duration is provided, use a provisional 15-minute documentary as a planning
assumption and clearly state it.

If the requested duration cannot reasonably be supported by the likely available material,
identify that concern.

These allocations are estimates for planning, not strict writing requirements.

---

PHASE 9 — RETENTION FLOW ANALYSIS

For each complete structure, evaluate how viewer interest is expected to progress.

Identify:

Opening Engagement

How quickly does the video establish a reason to watch?

Curiosity Development

How do new questions or explanations build from the initial premise?

Information Progression

Does each act contribute meaningful new information?

Midpoint Strength

Does the middle of the video contain a significant development, discovery, complication, or
deeper explanation?

Payoff Positioning

Is the strongest revelation, explanation, or resolution placed appropriately?

Closure

Does the final act satisfy the title's promise?
Required Output

For each structure, briefly describe its expected retention pattern.

Examples:

- Fast opening → gradual discovery → major reveal → conclusion
- Strong contradiction → context → escalating consequences → climax
- Central question → mechanisms → surprising implications → final explanation
- Multiple discoveries → increasingly significant examples → final comparison

Do not claim that a structure guarantees high audience retention.

---

PHASE 10 — COMPARE ALL FIVE STRUCTURES

After developing the structures, compare them objectively.

Score every structure on the following criteria:

1. Title Alignment — Does it directly support the video title?
2. Central Argument Development — Does the structure progressively develop the core
idea?
3. Viewer Promise Fulfillment — Can it deliver the expected payoff?
4. Narrative Progression — Do the acts advance logically?
5. Retention Potential — Does the structure provide meaningful reasons to continue?
6. Pacing & Duration Fit — Is it suitable for the requested video length?
7. Research Feasibility — Can its acts realistically be supported by factual material?

Score each criterion out of 10.

Calculate the total out of 70.

Required Comparison Table

Structure| Main Advantage| Main Weakness| Best Use Case| Score /70
6 Acts| ...| ...| ...| ...
7 Acts| ...| ...| ...| ...
8 Acts| ...| ...| ...| ...
9 Acts| ...| ...| ...| ...
10 Acts| ...| ...| ...| ...

Scores are professional editorial estimates, not measured predictions of audience retention.

Do not automatically award the highest score to the structure with the most acts.
A shorter, well-designed structure can be stronger than a longer one.

---

PHASE 11 — RECOMMEND THE BEST ACT STRUCTURE

Select the ONE structure that offers the strongest combination of:

- Narrative clarity
- Central argument development
- Audience engagement
- Logical progression
- Appropriate pacing
- Factual storytelling potential
- Viewer promise fulfillment

Explain:

1. Why This Structure Is Best

What makes it most suitable for the particular title?

2. Why Not the Other Structures?

Explain the main trade-offs.

3. Where Is the Strongest Moment?

Identify the act containing the most important discovery, turning point, explanation, or payoff.

4. What Is the Main Retention Risk?

Identify the section most likely to become slow, repetitive, or overly complicated.

5. How Should the Story Progress?

Summarize the recommended progression in one concise paragraph.

6. What Must Be Researched?

Identify critical evidence needed to develop the recommended structure.

---

PHASE 12 — FINAL RECOMMENDED STRUCTURE

After comparing the five alternatives, present the complete recommended structure again in
a clean, practical format.
Use:

RECOMMENDED STRUCTURE: [NUMBER] ACTS

VIDEO TITLE:
[Original Title]

CENTRAL ARGUMENT:
[Approved Argument]

VIEWER PROMISE:
[Approved Promise]

NARRATIVE APPROACH:
[Storytelling Strategy]

TARGET DURATION:
[Provided or Assumed Duration]

For each act provide:

ACT [NUMBER] — [SPECIFIC ACT TITLE]

Narrative Purpose:
[What the act must accomplish.]

Content Direction:
[The main story material this act should cover.]

Viewer Question:
[What viewers want answered.]

Psychological Engagement:
[Why the act should sustain interest.]

Key Development:
[What meaningfully advances the story.]

Act Payoff:
[What the viewer learns or understands.]

Transition Logic:
[Why the next act follows naturally.]

Estimated Duration:
[Minutes and seconds.]
Estimated Spoken Words:
[Word allocation.]

Repeat until the final act.

FINAL STRUCTURE SUMMARY

Provide:

- Total acts
- Estimated runtime
- Estimated spoken words
- Opening strategy
- Main narrative progression
- Midpoint development
- Primary payoff act
- Final resolution
- Biggest retention risk
- Essential research gaps

This final structure will serve as the foundation for the next stage of script development.

---

QUALITY CONTROL

Before presenting the final output, verify:

1. Every act has a clearly defined purpose.
2. No two acts perform essentially the same function.
3. The structure directly supports the central argument.
4. Each act contributes something meaningful.
5. The opening reflects the psychology of the title.
6. The middle acts maintain logical progression.
7. The ending fulfills the viewer promise.
8. No artificial twists or unsupported events have been introduced.
9. The structure fits the available material and target duration.
10. The five proposed structures differ meaningfully.
11. The recommended structure is stronger for defensible reasons.
12. The act allocation totals are internally consistent.

Revise weak structures before presenting them.

---

IMPORTANT RESTRICTIONS

- DO NOT write the complete script.
- DO NOT write actual voiceover narration.
- DO NOT write the opening hook word for word.
- DO NOT create detailed scene descriptions.
- DO NOT suggest B-roll, camera movements, or editing directions.
- DO NOT manufacture historical events or personal motivations.
- DO NOT change the approved central argument without justification.
- DO NOT create artificial suspense.
- DO NOT pad the structure simply to increase the number of acts.
- DO NOT turn the response into a generic storytelling lesson.
- DO NOT develop a full beat-by-beat script outline yet.

Focus specifically on NARRATIVE ARCHITECTURE, ACT PURPOSE, STORY
PROGRESSION, RETENTION LOGIC, PACING, AND PAYOFF PLACEMENT.

Detailed development of individual story beats will happen in the next stage.

---

FINAL INSTRUCTION

Build every structure around the actual story, not around a rigid storytelling template.

Treat the title as a promise.

Treat the central argument as the direction.

Treat every act as a necessary step toward fulfilling that promise.

Compare all five structures critically.

Recommend the one that best serves the story.

BEGIN WITH THE PROVIDED INPUT.

PHASE 2 — CREATE THE MASTER STORY BEAT MAP

Before developing individual acts in depth, identify the overall information progression.

A story beat is a specific unit of narrative development.

It may introduce:

- An important event
- A necessary explanation
- A significant fact
- A contradiction
- A decision
- A consequence
- A discovery
- A comparison
- A meaningful question
- An important reversal
- A piece of evidence
- A change in perspective
- A partial or final answer

A story beat is NOT simply a topic heading.

Weak Story Beat

"Discuss John Daly's lifestyle."

Strong Story Beat

"Establish the specific ways John Daly's public lifestyle conflicted with conventional
expectations of professional-athlete discipline, using documented examples, so the audience
understands the contradiction at the center of his career."
The strong beat explains:

- What information is needed.
- Why it matters.
- How it connects to the story.
- What the viewer should understand.

Beat Design Rules

1. Each beat must contain one main narrative development.
2. Every beat must contribute to the act's purpose.
3. Beats must progress logically.
4. Avoid using several beats to repeat the same point.
5. Each beat should give the audience something meaningful.
6. Do not make every beat artificially dramatic.
7. Use real facts and evidence to create interest.
8. A beat may answer a question rather than raise one.
9. The strongest beats should be placed where they create the greatest meaningful impact.
10. An act should feel like a coherent mini-story rather than a collection of unrelated facts.

Required Output

Create a master beat map showing:

- Act number
- Act purpose
- Main viewer question
- Planned number of beats
- Act's most important development
- Act payoff
- Connection to the next act

This map will guide the detailed beat development.

---

PHASE 3 — DEVELOP DETAILED STORY BEATS FOR EVERY ACT

Now expand EVERY approved act into a comprehensive storytelling blueprint.

Use approximately 3–6 meaningful story beats per act as a starting point, adjusting the count
to the act's length and complexity.

A short opening act may require fewer beats.

A complex middle act may require more.

Do not force a fixed number of beats.
For each act, provide the following:

ACT [NUMBER] — [APPROVED ACT TITLE]

A. ACT OBJECTIVE

Explain exactly what this act must accomplish.

Answer:

- Why does this act exist?
- What part of the central argument does it develop?
- What must the viewer understand by the end?
- Why is this act necessary before the next one?

B. STARTING VIEWER STATE

Describe what the audience already knows when this act begins.

Identify:

- What has already been established?
- Which relevant question remains unanswered?
- What information does the viewer need next?

Do not repeat previous information unnecessarily.

C. MAIN ACT QUESTION

Identify the single most important question this act will address.

The question must be specific.

Avoid vague questions such as:

"What happens next?"

Prefer questions tied to a particular event, decision, mechanism, or consequence.

D. DETAILED STORY BEATS

Develop each beat using the following structure.

---

BEAT [NUMBER] — [SPECIFIC BEAT NAME]
1. Narrative Function

What is this beat supposed to accomplish?

Examples:

- Introduce essential context
- Establish the stakes
- Reveal a contradiction
- Explain a mechanism
- Develop a character
- Show a consequence
- Introduce important evidence
- Establish a comparison
- Resolve an earlier question
- Prepare a meaningful turning point

2. Specific Content to Cover

Describe exactly which information belongs in this beat.

Identify:

- Relevant facts
- Events
- Decisions
- People
- Circumstances
- Relationships
- Causes
- Consequences
- Examples
- Mechanisms

Avoid vague instructions such as "talk about his struggles."

Explain which struggles, why they matter, and what role they play in the story.

3. Essential Evidence

Specify what research or factual support this beat requires.

Examples:

- Documented event
- Reliable date
- Credible quotation
- Historical record
- Interview
- First-person account
- Official data
- Technical explanation
- Verifiable comparison

If a detail is not yet confirmed, mark it as RESEARCH REQUIRED.

Do not present unverified information as established fact.

4. Viewer Knowledge Gap

What does the audience not yet understand that this beat will clarify?

Identify the exact missing piece of knowledge.

5. Psychological Engagement

Why should this information hold the viewer's attention?

Possible mechanisms:

- Curiosity
- Contrast
- Human conflict
- Surprise
- Discovery
- Consequences
- Anticipation
- Suspense
- Problem solving
- Intellectual satisfaction
- Emotional significance

Explain the mechanism specifically.

Do not simply write "creates curiosity."

6. Narrative Development

How does this beat move the overall story forward?

What changes in the viewer's understanding?

7. Information Reveal

What specific information is disclosed in this beat?
Decide whether the reveal is:

- A complete answer
- A partial answer
- A meaningful complication
- A new important fact
- A comparison
- A turning point

8. Tension or Curiosity Movement

Does this beat:

- Create a relevant new question?
- Deepen an existing question?
- Resolve a previous question?
- Increase the significance of an issue?
- Challenge an earlier assumption?

Do not manufacture unanswered questions when the evidence does not support them.

9. Micro-Payoff

What valuable understanding does the viewer receive from this beat?

Every meaningful beat should reward the viewer in some way.

A micro-payoff may be a fact, explanation, realization, contrast, or consequence.

10. Connection to the Next Beat

Explain how this beat naturally leads to the next one.

Use logical relationships such as:

- Cause and effect
- Question and answer
- Problem and consequence
- Expectation and contradiction
- Action and reaction
- General explanation and specific example
- Evidence and interpretation
- Decision and outcome

Do not write a complete voiceover transition yet.

11. Estimated Spoken Words
Allocate an appropriate word budget.

The allocation must reflect narrative importance and necessary explanation.

---

Repeat this exact process for every beat in every approved act.

PHASE 4 — DESIGN MINI-NARRATIVE ARCS WITHIN EACH ACT

Each act should have its own internal progression.

A useful general model is:

Entry → Development → Complication or Discovery → Act Payoff → Transition

Adapt it according to the content.

Act Entry

What information or unresolved question naturally brings the audience into this act?

Development

How do the first beats establish the necessary context and advance the story?

Complication or Discovery

What new development makes the act more meaningful?

This does not have to be negative or dramatic.

Act Payoff

What meaningful answer, understanding, or development completes the act?

Transition

What makes the next act necessary?

Required Output for Every Act

- Entry:
- Main Development:
- Key Discovery or Complication:
- Act Payoff:
- Forward Connection:

Ensure the act feels complete while still belonging to the larger narrative.

PHASE 5 — DEVELOP THE INFORMATION RELEASE STRATEGY

A well-designed documentary does not reveal information randomly.

The order of information matters.

Your task is to determine what the audience should know, when they should know it, and
why.

1. Essential Early Information

Identify the minimum information necessary for the viewer to understand the opening and
central conflict.

Avoid overwhelming the viewer with excessive background.

2. Progressive Understanding

Arrange beats so later information builds on what has already been established.

3. Meaningful Discoveries

Identify which facts or explanations should change the viewer's understanding.

4. Major Narrative Developments

Locate the strongest factual turning points, consequences, comparisons, or revelations.

5. Final Explanation

Ensure the final payoff is prepared by earlier beats.

A strong final conclusion should feel earned rather than suddenly introduced.

Important Information Release Rules

- Do not reveal everything immediately.
- Do not withhold obvious answers artificially.
- Do not repeat information to delay the story.
- Do not create misleading mysteries.
- Provide useful answers throughout.
- Introduce background only when necessary.
- Preserve chronology where it matters.
- Avoid unnecessary chronological detours.
- Ensure important conclusions are supported by earlier evidence.

Required Output

Create a short Information Release Plan identifying:

- What viewers learn early
- What they learn during the middle
- What significantly changes their understanding
- What is resolved near the end
- What final understanding completes the promise

---

PHASE 6 — DESIGN CURIOSITY, RE-HOOKS & MINI-PAYOFFS

Now examine where audience interest could naturally weaken.

Your goal is to design meaningful engagement opportunities within the existing story.

Do not replace storytelling with artificial cliffhangers.

A. Curiosity Points

Identify genuine unanswered questions that emerge from the information.

These must be relevant to the title.

B. Re-Hook Opportunities

Locate points where the viewer's attention could be refreshed through:

- New facts
- Strong contrasts
- Important consequences
- Unexpected details
- Changes in perspective
- Deeper explanations
- Revealing comparisons
- Significant decisions
- Verified reversals
Do not write full narration.

Describe the narrative opportunity and the information needed to support it.

C. Micro-Payoffs

Ensure the viewer receives meaningful answers regularly.

Do not postpone all value until the final act.

D. Question Management

Track major questions created throughout the story.

For every significant question, identify:

- Where it is introduced
- Why it matters
- Where it receives a partial answer, if relevant
- Where it is fully answered
- Whether the resolution is supported by evidence

E. Avoid Artificial Curiosity

Reject:

- Fake mysteries
- Misleading questions
- Unsupported scandals
- Manufactured twists
- Repeated promises of something shocking
- Empty transitions
- Deliberate withholding of ordinary information

Required Output

Create a Curiosity & Payoff Tracker.

Use a table with:

Question / Curiosity| Introduced In| Developed In| Resolved In| Payoff

Every significant narrative question must have a clear intended resolution.

If a question genuinely remains unresolved historically or factually, explain that rather than
inventing a conclusion.

---
PHASE 7 — DESIGN TENSION & ENERGY PROGRESSION

The story should not maintain the same emotional or intellectual intensity throughout.

Plan meaningful changes in energy.

Possible levels:

Low Energy

Background, orientation, necessary explanations, reflection.

Moderate Energy

New developments, important comparisons, deeper understanding.

High Energy

Major consequences, important decisions, significant contradictions, discoveries, turning
points, or decisive explanations.

These are relative planning labels, not scientific measurements of audience attention.

Important Rules

1. Not every beat should have high intensity.
2. Do not confuse loud language with strong engagement.
3. Allow breathing room after major revelations.
4. Use quieter explanatory beats to prepare important developments.
5. Prevent long stretches of repetitive background information.
6. Avoid placing several unrelated high-intensity moments together.
7. Match emotional intensity to the real events.
8. Educational content can sustain interest through progressively deeper understanding.

Required Output

For every act, assign:

- Primary energy level: Low / Moderate / High
- Reason for that level
- Most important attention-refresh opportunity
- Potential pacing weakness

Then provide a short overall Narrative Energy Progression describing how the video is
expected to feel from beginning to end.

Do not claim to predict actual audience retention percentages.
---

PHASE 8 — DESIGN TRANSITIONS BETWEEN ACTS

The story must feel connected, not like separate chapters pasted together.

For each act transition, identify:

A. Closing Understanding

What has the viewer just learned?

B. Remaining Question

What logically remains unexplained?

C. Narrative Connection

How does the answer or development from the current act create the need for the next act?

D. Transition Type

Choose the most natural option:

- Cause → Consequence
- Decision → Result
- Problem → New Complication
- Question → Investigation
- Explanation → Deeper Explanation
- Past → Present
- Expectation → Reality
- Individual Example → Larger Pattern
- Evidence → Conclusion
- Comparison → Implication

E. Transition Purpose

Explain what the transition should accomplish.

Do not write the final spoken transition line yet.

Required Output

Create an Act Transition Map showing the logical connection between each adjacent pair of
acts.

Avoid repetitive transitions such as:
"Now let's move to the next part."

"Next, we will discuss..."

"And that's not all."

"Things were about to get interesting."

Every transition must emerge from the content itself.

---

PHASE 9 — ADAPT THE BEATS TO THE CONTENT FORMAT

Before finalizing, ensure the detailed beats match the actual type of video.

A. Historical Documentary

Emphasize:

- Verified events
- Historical context
- Decisions
- Motivations supported by evidence
- Strategic conditions
- Consequences
- Turning points
- Historical interpretations

Avoid invented dialogue, fictional motivations, and inaccurate chronology.

B. Sports Documentary

Emphasize:

- Career context
- Performance
- Competition
- Major achievements
- Professional pressures
- Personal decisions
- Turning points
- Documented controversies
- Long-term consequences

Distinguish verified events from speculative psychological interpretations.
C. Character-Driven Biography

Emphasize:

- Defining traits
- Important experiences
- Contradictions
- Decisions
- Relationships relevant to the story
- Consequences
- Character development

Avoid turning the video into a generic chronological biography if the title promises a specific
explanation.

D. Explanatory Documentary

Emphasize:

- Main question
- Underlying mechanisms
- Logical explanations
- Specific examples
- Important complexities
- Supporting evidence
- Final understanding

Do not manufacture conflict when the subject is primarily explanatory.

E. Listicle / Ranking

For each item or item group, include:

- Specific item identification
- Necessary context
- What makes it significant
- The relevant evidence
- A distinctive detail
- A useful payoff

Avoid making every entry identical.

Use ranking criteria consistently.

Do not assume the final item must be dramatic unless the evidence and ranking justify it.

F. Comparison Documentary
Emphasize:

- Clear comparison criteria
- Relevant background
- Key similarities
- Important differences
- Evidence
- Consequences
- Reasoned conclusion

Do not manipulate evidence merely to create a stronger narrative.

G. Investigation / Mystery

Emphasize:

- Central question
- Initial evidence
- Competing explanations
- New discoveries
- Contradictions
- Evidence evaluation
- Supported conclusion

Never manufacture conspiracies or hide established facts to prolong suspense.

Now ensure the expanded blueprint fits the intended video length.

Otherwise estimate using the requested duration and appropriate narration pacing.

Suggested rates:

- Slow documentary: 125–140 WPM
- Standard documentary: 140–155 WPM
- Faster explainer: 155–165 WPM

CORE PHILOSOPHY

Audience retention is not achieved by constantly using shocking words, artificial cliffhangers,

Strong retention comes from giving viewers a continuous reason to remain interested.

A great video consistently balances:
Curiosity → Understanding → New Development → Escalation → Payoff → Renewed
Interest

The viewer should regularly feel:

- I understand what's happening.
- This information is interesting.
- I just learned something valuable.
- There's still something meaningful I want to understand.
- The story is progressing.
- The video is delivering what the title promised.

Never sacrifice clarity, credibility, or viewer satisfaction simply to manufacture suspense.

PHASE 1 — ANALYZE THE EXISTING RETENTION FOUNDATION

What must the video deliver to justify the initial click?

PHASE 2 — DESIGN THE OPENING HOOK STRATEGY

The opening is one of the most important portions of the video.

It must immediately connect the viewer's expectations to a meaningful story.

Your task is to identify the best approach for opening the documentary.

GENERATE THREE DIFFERENT HOOK STRATEGIES

HOOK A — CONTRADICTION / SURPRISE

Open with a meaningful contradiction, unexpected truth, or unusual situation.

The viewer should immediately recognize that something requires an explanation.

HOOK B — CONSEQUENCE / HIGH-STAKES MOMENT

Begin with a major decision, consequence, important development, or turning point.

The viewer should want to understand how and why it happened.

HOOK C — MYSTERY / HIDDEN EXPLANATION

Begin with an interesting unresolved question, overlooked mechanism, or factual detail that
points toward a larger explanation.

IMPORTANT

These are possible approaches, not mandatory formulas.

If one does not fit the topic, replace it with another suitable approach.

For EACH hook strategy, provide:

1. Hook type
2. Core opening idea
3. Specific factual material to introduce
4. Psychological trigger
5. Question created in the viewer's mind
6. Connection to the video title
7. What information should be withheld temporarily
8. What information must be explained immediately
9. Natural transition into the main story
10. Strength score out of 10
Do not manufacture dramatic facts.

Do not write the complete spoken hook.

Provide a precise narrative plan that can later be converted into natural narration.

SELECT THE STRONGEST HOOK

Recommend one strategy.

Explain:

- Why it aligns with the title
- Why it attracts the intended audience
- Why it creates meaningful curiosity
- How it prepares the central argument
- What could weaken it

PHASE 3 — ENGINEER THE FIRST 60–90 SECONDS

Develop a detailed engagement plan for the beginning of the video.

The viewer should quickly understand what the video is about and why it is worth continuing.

Use the following timing windows as flexible guidelines, not rigid requirements.

0–15 Seconds — Immediate Interest

Determine:

- What should be introduced first?
- Which concrete detail establishes the premise?
- What makes the opening relevant to the title?
- What question should emerge?

Avoid generic introductions, greetings, and unnecessary background.

15–30 Seconds — Establish the Central Tension

Determine:

- What contradiction, problem, or explanation makes this story important?
- What essential context does the viewer need?
- Why does the subject matter?
30–60 Seconds — Deepen the Question

Determine:

- What additional information makes the opening more interesting?
- What should the viewer understand?
- What important question remains unresolved?
- How can the story expand naturally?

60–90 Seconds — Begin Meaningful Progression

Determine:

- What concrete answer, event, or explanation should the viewer receive?
- How does the narrative move beyond its initial promise?
- What makes the next development necessary?

Do not force all four stages if the video begins faster or the target duration is shorter.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Time Window| Narrative Objective| Information| Viewer Question| Engagement Mechanism|
Payoff

Then explain how the opening transitions into the remainder of Act 1.

Do not write actual narration.
