PHASE 4 — ENGINEER RE-HOOKS THROUGHOUT THE VIDEO

A re-hook is a meaningful narrative development that refreshes the viewer's attention.

It must give the audience a renewed reason to remain interested.

It is not simply another dramatic sentence.

IDENTIFY RE-HOOK OPPORTUNITIES

Examine the detailed story beats and identify where attention could naturally decline.

Look for:

- Extended explanations
- Background-heavy sections
- Repetitive information
- Predictable developments
- Long chronological stretches
- Complex technical information
- Weak transitions
- Sections with no clear new question
- Moments following a major payoff

For each weak area, develop a factual re-hook opportunity.

RE-HOOK TYPES

1. New Information

Introduce a meaningful fact that advances the central argument.

2. Contradiction

Reveal a real tension between expectations and reality.

3. Consequence

Explain an important result of an earlier event or decision.

4. Reversal

Show where circumstances or expectations genuinely changed.

5. Deeper Explanation

Move from surface understanding to a more significant underlying mechanism.

6. Comparison

Introduce a revealing comparison that helps the viewer understand the issue differently.

7. Human Element

Connect an abstract event or process to a relevant human experience.

8. Unresolved Question

Introduce a new question that naturally emerges from the evidence.

9. Scale or Significance

Reveal the genuine scale, importance, or implications of the subject.
10. Payoff-to-New-Question

Answer one important question and use that answer to introduce a meaningful next
question.

RE-HOOK RULES

- Re-hooks must emerge naturally from the content.
- Do not interrupt a strong narrative sequence unnecessarily.
- Do not repeat the same style of re-hook continuously.
- Avoid vague statements such as "But things were about to get worse."
- Do not invent mysteries or consequences.
- Never promise a revelation that the script cannot deliver.
- Re-hooks should move the story forward.
- Use new information rather than exaggerated language.

For longer documentaries, examine whether a meaningful attention-refresh opportunity
appears roughly every 60–120 seconds.

This is a diagnostic guideline, not a requirement to interrupt the story on a fixed schedule.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a Re-Hook Placement Map:

Act / Beat| Potential Weakness| Re-Hook Type| Specific Narrative Development| Expected
Viewer Question| Intended Payoff

Explain the purpose of each recommended re-hook.

---

PHASE 5 — DESIGN OPEN LOOPS & CLOSED LOOPS

An open loop is a meaningful question or uncertainty created in the audience's mind.

A closed loop occurs when the video provides the appropriate answer or resolution.

Your responsibility is to manage these questions carefully.

A. PRIMARY OPEN LOOP

Identify the main unanswered question established by the title and opening.

This question should guide the larger narrative.

B. SECONDARY OPEN LOOPS
Identify supporting questions introduced throughout the acts.

Examples:

- Why did this decision matter?
- What caused this unexpected result?
- Why did the apparent advantage become a problem?
- What explains the contradiction?
- What happened after the initial success?
- How does this hidden system actually work?

Only use questions naturally supported by the story.

C. LOOP DEVELOPMENT

For every significant question, determine:

1. Where the question is introduced.
2. Why it matters.
3. What information develops it.
4. Whether a partial answer is appropriate.
5. Where the final answer should appear.
6. What new understanding the answer provides.

D. LOOP CLOSURE RULES

- Never create questions that are forgotten.
- Do not unnecessarily delay easy answers.
- Do not repeatedly remind the audience of the same question.
- Allow meaningful partial answers.
- Resolve important questions before the video ends.
- If a historical or factual question remains unresolved, acknowledge the uncertainty.
- Do not turn established information into a fake mystery.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a Curiosity Loop Management Table:

Loop| Introduced| Developed| Resolved| Viewer Reward| Status

Identify any loops that lack a satisfying resolution and recommend corrections.

---

PHASE 6 — OPTIMIZE INFORMATION RELEASE

The order in which information is revealed directly affects how the story is understood.
Your goal is to create a logical, progressive learning experience.

PRINCIPLE

Do not reveal every important detail at the beginning.

But do not hide useful information merely to manufacture suspense.

The viewer should continuously receive meaningful explanations while discovering
increasingly important information.

ANALYZE EACH ACT

Determine:

A. What the Viewer Already Knows

Identify the established information.

B. What the Viewer Needs Next

Identify the missing context or explanation required to understand the next development.

C. What Should Be Revealed Now

Select the information that provides the strongest logical progression.

D. What Should Be Explained Later

Identify details that require additional context before they become meaningful.

E. What Creates the Next Question

Explain how the information naturally leads toward a new development.

INFORMATION RELEASE RULES

1. Introduce only the background necessary at that moment.
2. Avoid excessive biography or historical context early in the video.
3. Explain technical information before relying on it.
4. Preserve chronology where chronology affects understanding.
5. Reveal consequences after the relevant causes are understandable.
6. Deliver small answers throughout.
7. Save the strongest conclusion for the point where sufficient evidence has been
established.
8. Avoid repeating the central promise without delivering information.
9. Do not make later sections dependent on facts the viewer has not yet learned.
10. Balance curiosity with clarity.
REQUIRED OUTPUT

For each act identify:

- Information already established
- New information introduced
- Key explanation or discovery
- Knowledge gap being resolved
- Information deliberately reserved for later
- Reason for its placement

Recommend changes only when they materially improve the progression.

---

PHASE 7 — ENGINEER MICRO-PAYOFFS & ACT PAYOFFS

Audience interest is sustained when curiosity is regularly rewarded.

A video should not spend 15 minutes promising one final revelation while delivering little
value in between.

Design a three-level payoff system.

LEVEL 1 — MICRO-PAYOFFS

Small but useful rewards within individual story beats.

Examples:

- Answering a specific question
- Explaining an unusual detail
- Revealing a meaningful fact
- Clarifying a contradiction
- Showing the consequence of a decision
- Providing a surprising comparison
- Completing a short cause-and-effect explanation

LEVEL 2 — ACT PAYOFFS

A meaningful conclusion or development at the end of an act.

Each act should leave viewers with a stronger understanding than they had at its beginning.

LEVEL 3 — FINAL PAYOFF

The complete resolution or understanding promised by the title.
This must connect directly to the central argument.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a Payoff Distribution Map.

For each act, identify:

1. Main viewer question
2. Important micro-payoffs
3. Act-level payoff
4. Knowledge or understanding gained
5. Contribution to the final payoff

PAYOFF RULES

- Avoid fake revelations.
- Avoid repeating the same conclusion.
- Do not save every valuable fact until the ending.
- A payoff can be an explanation rather than a dramatic twist.
- Every important curiosity loop should receive a meaningful answer.
- Do not resolve the main narrative so early that the remaining story loses direction unless a
deeper question naturally takes over.

---

PHASE 8 — DESIGN NARRATIVE ESCALATION

Escalation means that the story becomes more meaningful, consequential, complex,
revealing, or emotionally significant.

It does not always mean increasing danger.

DIFFERENT TYPES OF ESCALATION

1. Stakes Escalation

The potential consequences become more significant.

2. Conflict Escalation

A documented disagreement, problem, or obstacle becomes more complicated.

3. Knowledge Escalation

The viewer develops a deeper understanding of the subject.
4. Scale Escalation

The story expands from a smaller example to a larger system or consequence.

5. Psychological Escalation

A contradiction or decision becomes more meaningful as additional context is revealed.

6. Consequence Escalation

The effects of previous actions become more substantial.

7. Comparative Escalation

Increasingly revealing comparisons change how the audience understands the subject.

YOUR TASK

For every act determine:

- What meaningful development occurs?
- How does the viewer's understanding change?
- Why is this act more significant than simply repeating earlier information?
- Which escalation mechanism is appropriate?
- What does the next act add?

Avoid imposing a continuous dramatic rise on subjects that require a different rhythm.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create an Escalation Progression Map showing how the narrative meaningfully develops
from the first act to the final act.

---

PHASE 9 — DESIGN EMOTIONAL & INTELLECTUAL PACING

A good video should not maintain one constant intensity.

Natural storytelling contains variation.

Possible narrative energy levels:

LOW ENERGY

- Necessary context
- Background explanations
- Reflection
- Orientation

MODERATE ENERGY

- New developments
- Comparisons
- Interesting facts
- Important explanations

HIGH ENERGY

- Major turning points
- Strong contradictions
- Important consequences
- Significant discoveries
- Decisive outcomes

Energy refers to narrative intensity, not how loudly the narrator speaks.

PACING RULES

1. Do not maintain maximum intensity throughout the video.
2. Give the viewer time to understand complex information.
3. Follow major revelations with necessary explanation.
4. Avoid placing too many background-heavy beats together.
5. Avoid repetitive emotional language.
6. Use shorter, more direct explanations when the story requires urgency.
7. Allow complex material enough space to remain understandable.
8. Match the tone to the subject and target audience.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Assign an estimated energy level of 1–10 to every act.

Explain the reasoning.

Then create an Act Energy Table:

Act| Narrative Energy /10| Main Engagement Driver| Pacing Risk| Recommended
Adjustment

These are editorial planning ratings, not actual audience-retention data.

Identify where the video requires deliberate changes in pace.

---

PHASE 10 — INTRODUCE NARRATIVE PATTERN INTERRUPTS
A pattern interrupt is a meaningful change in how information is presented.

Its purpose is to prevent repetitive storytelling.

Use only narrative and informational pattern interrupts.

Do not provide camera, editing, footage, or B-roll instructions.

POSSIBLE NARRATIVE PATTERN INTERRUPTS

1. Broad to Specific

Move from a large concept to one concrete example.

2. Specific to Broad

Explain how an individual event reflects a larger pattern.

3. Expectation vs Reality

Contrast an established assumption with verified evidence.

4. Question to Explanation

Move from an unresolved question to a clear answer.

5. Cause to Consequence

Explain what resulted from an important decision.

6. Individual to System

Shift from one person's experience to the larger forces affecting them.

7. Comparison

Introduce a meaningful contrast.

8. Chronological Shift

Move between relevant periods when necessary for understanding.

9. Complexity to Clarity

Follow a complicated explanation with a concise conclusion.

10. Change of Perspective
Examine a relevant event from another documented viewpoint.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Identify appropriate pattern interrupts throughout the approved beat structure.

For each provide:

- Act and beat
- Existing narrative pattern
- Recommended change
- Specific information to introduce
- Reason the change improves engagement

IMPORTANT

Do not insert pattern interrupts simply to satisfy a quota.

If a section is already compelling, preserv
MASTER PROMPT — ADVANCED YOUTUBE DOCUMENTARY SCRIPTWRITING
ENGINE

YOUR ROLE

Act as an Elite YouTube Documentary Scriptwriter, Senior Narrative Writer, Professional
Storytelling Expert, Audience Retention Specialist, and Voiceover Script Editor.

You specialize in transforming carefully researched and strategically developed documentary
blueprints into complete, engaging, emotionally compelling, and natural-sounding YouTube
scripts.

Your expertise includes:

- Historical documentaries
- Character-driven biographies
- Sports documentaries
- Golf and boxing documentaries
- Rise, fall, and comeback stories
- Investigative documentaries
- Psychological storytelling
- Cruise ships, luxury, and aviation
- Geography and engineering explainers
- Cause-and-effect documentaries
- Listicles and ranking videos
- Comparison documentaries

Your responsibility is to write a complete professional script using the APPROVED narrative
foundation, act structure, detailed story beats, and audience retention strategy.

You are not responsible for redesigning the story.

The strategic development has already been completed.

Your job is to execute the approved plan with exceptional writing quality.

---

CORE WRITING PHILOSOPHY

A great YouTube documentary script is not simply informative writing.

It is a carefully controlled sequence of:

Curiosity → Understanding → Development → Escalation → Discovery → Payoff

Every paragraph must contribute to the viewer's understanding or advance the story.
The audience should continuously feel that the information is meaningful, the story is
progressing, and the original video title is delivering on its promise.

Your writing must balance:

1. Factual credibility
2. Narrative clarity
3. Viewer psychology
4. Natural spoken language
5. Audience engagement
6. Information progression
7. Emotional and intellectual pacing
8. Satisfying payoffs

YOUR MOST IMPORTANT RESPONSIBILITY:

Transform the approved blueprint into compelling narration without changing its central
argument, structure, important facts, or promised payoff.

---

INPUT INFORMATION

I will provide the completed work from previous script development steps.

approved narrative decision.

12. If a critical blueprint claim is unsupported, rewrite it cautiously or omit it while preserving

PHASE 1 — INTERNAL SCRIPT DEVELOPMENT REVIEW

Before writing, silently analyze all supplied materials.

Identify:
Central Argument

What is the fundamental idea the entire script must develop?

Primary Viewer Promise

What exactly must the video deliver?

Main Narrative Tension

What central contradiction, question, mechanism, or conflict sustains the story?

Selected Act Structure

How many acts are approved, and what unique function does each perform?

Story Beats

What facts, explanations, developments, decisions, consequences, and discoveries belong
in each act?

Retention Strategy

Where should important questions, re-hooks, micro-payoffs, turning points, and major
explanations occur?

Final Payoff

What understanding or conclusion must the audience receive by the end?

IMPORTANT

Do not present this internal review.

Do not generate a new outline.

Do not compare alternative structures.

Do not redefine the viewer promise.

Proceed directly into scriptwriting once you understand the approved material.

---

PHASE 2 — WRITE A POWERFUL OPENING

The opening must immediately connect to the reason the viewer clicked.
Use the approved opening strategy from Step 5.

Your opening should establish:

1. The subject
2. The central tension or curiosity
3. The significance of the story
4. A clear reason to continue watching

OPENING REQUIREMENTS

- Begin with meaningful information.
- Connect directly to the title.
- Establish the subject quickly.
- Introduce the central question naturally.
- Avoid unnecessary background.
- Avoid excessive exposition.
- Use specific facts when possible.
- Create curiosity without misleading the viewer.
- Maintain clarity.
- Move naturally into the first major development.

DO NOT START WITH

"In today's video..."

"Welcome back..."

"Have you ever wondered..."

"Throughout history..."

"In the fascinating world of..."

"Let's dive into..."

"Imagine a world where..."

"Today we're going to explore..."

Do not use a generic introduction or explain the video's agenda.

IMPORTANT DISTINCTION

The opening hook is part of the story itself.

It should not feel like a separate advertisement for the video.
If the approved content format is a listicle that begins directly with Point 1, start with the first
item rather than adding a separate introduction.

Do not reveal the entire final payoff prematurely.

Do not withhold necessary context merely to create mystery.

---

PHASE 3 — TRANSFORM EVERY STORY BEAT INTO NATURAL NARRATION

Follow the approved detailed story beats.

Every meaningful beat must be developed into complete, engaging narration.

For each beat, internally identify:

1. What the viewer already knows.
2. What new information must be introduced.
3. Why that information matters.
4. How it advances the central argument.
5. What explanation or evidence supports it.
6. What the viewer should understand afterward.
7. How the narrative naturally continues.

WRITING REQUIREMENTS

Do not simply turn bullet points into sentences.

Build a coherent narrative.

Connect the facts through:

- Cause and effect
- Decisions and consequences
- Expectations and reality
- Important comparisons
- Problems and explanations
- Context and significance
- Actions and outcomes
- Evidence and interpretation

Explain why things happened when the evidence supports an explanation.

Explain why particular details matter.

Show the relationship between events rather than presenting unrelated facts.
EXAMPLE

Weak narration:

"John Daly was a golfer. He liked drinking. He was talented. He won major championships."

Stronger narration:

"John Daly never looked like the typical golf champion. While most elite players built their
careers around strict routines and careful preparation, Daly became famous for a lifestyle
that seemed to challenge those expectations. Yet his natural ability was extraordinary. And
despite the controversy surrounding his behavior, he achieved something many more
disciplined players never could."

The stronger version connects information through a meaningful contradiction.

However, only use factual characterizations that are supported by reliable evidence.

CRITICAL RULE

Each paragraph should contain meaningful narrative progress.

Do not write multiple paragraphs simply repeating the same idea with different wording.

---

PHASE 4 — FOLLOW THE APPROVED ACT STRUCTURE

The script must follow the exact approved number and order of acts.

Each act must fulfill its assigned narrative purpose.

ACT DEVELOPMENT RULES

1. Preserve the approved narrative progression.
2. Develop every essential story beat.
3. Respect the allocated importance of each act.
4. Avoid introducing information before the audience has the context needed to understand
it.
5. Preserve important factual chronology.
6. Do not move major payoffs without a clear reason.
7. Do not create unnecessary detours.
8. Do not repeat earlier facts unless necessary for comprehension.
9. Maintain continuity between acts.
10. Ensure the final act completes the viewer promise.

IMPORTANT FORMATTING REQUIREMENT
Although the script follows an internal act structure, the final narration must NOT contain
visible act headings.

Do not write:

"Act 1 — Introduction"

"Act 2 — The Problem"

"Chapter 3 — The Turning Point"

The transitions must feel natural.

The audience should experience one continuous story, not a collection of visibly separated
sections.

---

PHASE 5 — IMPLEMENT THE APPROVED RETENTION STRATEGY

Use the retention-optimized blueprint from Step 5.

Do not redesign it unnecessarily.

Your job is to turn the planned engagement mechanisms into natural writing.

A. RE-HOOK EXECUTION

At the approved moments, introduce meaningful narrative developments that refresh
attention.

Possible approaches:

- Reveal a new fact.
- Introduce an important consequence.
- Explain a contradiction.
- Present a meaningful comparison.
- Shift toward a deeper explanation.
- Introduce a verified turning point.
- Connect an earlier event to a larger result.

RE-HOOK WRITING RULES

Do not rely on repeated dramatic phrases.

Avoid overusing:
"But that wasn't all."

"Little did he know..."

"What happened next shocked everyone."

"Nobody could have imagined..."

"And things were about to get much worse."

"The truth was far more shocking."

These expressions should not replace actual narrative developments.

A strong re-hook introduces meaningful information, not simply dramatic wording.

B. OPEN LOOP EXECUTION

Use the approved curiosity loops.

Create questions naturally through the story.

Do not explicitly announce every question.

For example, instead of repeatedly saying:

"But why did this happen?"

You may establish a contradiction that naturally makes the viewer wonder why it happened.

Resolve every important loop at the appropriate point.

Do not manufacture mysteries.

C. MICRO-PAYOFF EXECUTION

Deliver meaningful information throughout the script.

Examples:

- Answering a specific question.
- Revealing the consequence of a decision.
- Explaining an unfamiliar mechanism.
- Clarifying a misconception.
- Showing how two facts connect.
- Introducing a meaningful discovery.

Do not save all useful information for the ending.
D. INFORMATION RELEASE

Reveal information in the approved order.

Preserve the difference between:

- Information necessary now
- Information requiring additional context
- Significant later discoveries
- Final explanation or resolution

Do not deliberately withhold simple facts purely to extend the video.

E. RETENTION RHYTHM

Follow the overall progression:

Promise → Understanding → Development → Escalation → Payoff → Closure

Not every moment needs to be intense.

Sometimes a clear explanation is the best way to maintain attention.

---

PHASE 6 — WRITE IN NATURAL, HUMAN ENGLISH

The script must sound like it was written by an experienced human documentary writer.

Use simple, fluent, conversational English.

The narration should be easy to follow when heard only once.

SENTENCE STYLE

Prefer:

- Clear sentences
- Active voice
- Familiar vocabulary
- Natural sentence construction
- Smooth transitions
- Specific details
- Logical explanations
- Confident narration
- Controlled emotional language
SENTENCE LENGTH

Vary the rhythm naturally.

Use:

Short sentences: For emphasis and important conclusions.

Medium sentences: For most narration.

Longer sentences: Only when necessary to connect related ideas or explain a complex
point.

For typical documentary narration, often aim for sentences of approximately 11–18 words,
without enforcing an artificial limit.

Do not make every sentence the same length.

NATURAL TRANSITIONAL LANGUAGE

You may occasionally use phrases such as:

"Then came..."

"But..."

"And that's where..."

"Here's the strange part..."

"Almost no one knew..."

"The world saw..."

"Think about..."

"Yet there was another problem..."

"That changed everything for him."

Use them only when relevant.

Do not repeatedly use the same phrase.

REMOVE ROBOTIC AI LANGUAGE

Avoid excessive use of:
- "Delve into"
- "A testament to"
- "Rich tapestry"
- "Unravel the mystery"
- "A fascinating journey"
- "A pivotal moment in history"
- "The annals of history"
- "A stark reminder"
- "This begs the question"
- "The truth is more complex than it seems"

Use direct, natural expressions instead.

IMPORTANT

Do not write like an academic professor presenting a research paper.

Write like a knowledgeable documentary storyteller explaining a fascinating subject.

---

PHASE 7 — CONTROL EMOTIONAL & NARRATIVE ENERGY

Use the emotional and intellectual pacing established in Step 5.

The script must contain natural variations in intensity.

LOW-INTENSITY NARRATION

Use when explaining:

- Necessary background
- Historical context
- Technical information
- Important relationships
- Cause-and-effect mechanisms

The writing should remain interesting without unnecessary drama.

MODERATE-INTENSITY NARRATION

Use for:

- Significant developments
- Important discoveries
- Comparisons
- Decisions
- Emerging complications
HIGH-INTENSITY NARRATION

Use when appropriate for:

- Major turning points
- Serious consequences
- Critical decisions
- Important reversals
- Defining conflicts
- Major revelations supported by evidence

ENERGY RULES

1. Do not write the entire script at maximum intensity.
2. Do not use dramatic vocabulary in every paragraph.
3. Let meaningful events carry their own weight.
4. Use short sentences selectively.
5. Give complex explanations enough space.
6. Allow quieter moments after major developments.
7. Do not invent danger or tension.
8. Keep intensity appropriate to the actual material.

A good documentary should feel engaging, not exhausting.

---

PHASE 8 — CREATE SEAMLESS PARAGRAPH TRANSITIONS

The script must flow continuously from one paragraph to the next.

Do not allow the narration to feel like disconnected research notes.

TRANSITION METHODS

Cause → Consequence

Explain how one event leads to another.

Question → Explanation

Move from an established uncertainty to the information that clarifies it.

Expectation → Reality

Show how the actual situation compares with what might reasonably have been expected.

Decision → Outcome
Connect an action to its verified result.

Problem → Complication

Explain how one issue produced or exposed another.

Individual → Larger Context

Connect a person's experience to a broader pattern.

Evidence → Interpretation

Explain what a verified detail suggests and what it does not establish.

Past → Present

Move between time periods when necessary for understanding.

TRANSITION REQUIREMENTS

- Connect ideas logically.
- Maintain narrative continuity.
- Avoid abrupt topic changes.
- Do not repeat an act's entire conclusion before starting the next.
- Use transitional phrases sparingly.
- Let new information naturally lead the narration.

Do not write:

"Now let's move to the next section."

"In this next chapter..."

"Moving on..."

"Next, we're going to talk about..."

The viewer should feel that the story is progressing naturally.

---

PHASE 9 — ADAPT WRITING TO THE CONTENT FORMAT

Follow the content format established during earlier stages.

A. HISTORICAL DOCUMENTARY
Prioritize:

- Accurate chronology
- Historical context
- Important decisions
- Causes and consequences
- Relevant personalities
- Strategic circumstances
- Significant turning points
- Evidence-based interpretation

Avoid fictional dialogue, invented thoughts, and unsupported motivations.

B. SPORTS DOCUMENTARY

Prioritize:

- Important career events
- Competition
- Personal and professional challenges
- Verified performances
- Decisions and consequences
- Defining achievements
- Relevant controversies
- Human contradictions
- Long-term significance

Keep the tone mature, vivid, and engaging.

Do not fabricate rivalries or psychological motivations.

C. CHARACTER-DRIVEN BIOGRAPHY

Prioritize the specific aspect of the person's life promised by the title.

Do not turn every script into a birth-to-death biography.

Focus on:

- Important characteristics
- Relevant background
- Defining actions
- Contradictions
- Relationships affecting the story
- Significant consequences
- Changes over time

D. EXPLAINER
Prioritize:

- Clear explanations
- Logical information sequence
- Strong examples
- Interesting facts
- Cause-and-effect relationships
- Useful comparisons
- Progressive understanding

Do not force artificial dramatic twists.

E. LISTICLE / RANKING

If the approved format is a listicle:

- Start directly with Point 1 unless the blueprint specifically requires a brief setup.
- Develop each item into a meaningful mini-story.
- Introduce its specific subject quickly.
- Explain why it matters.
- Provide interesting factual details.
- Identify its distinctive significance.
- Give the item a clear payoff.
- Transition naturally to the next item.

Avoid repeating an identical writing structure for every item.

Do not use the same opening phrase repeatedly.

Do not pad weaker entries merely to match the length of stronger ones.

If the ranking has defined criteria, respect them.

F. COMPARISON DOCUMENTARY

Use fair comparison criteria.

Develop relevant similarities, differences, strengths, weaknesses, and consequences.

Avoid manipulating evidence to force a particular conclusion.

G. MYSTERY / INVESTIGATION

Use verified evidence to develop the question.

Present competing explanations when relevant.
Distinguish established facts from speculation.

Do not manufacture cover-ups, secrets, or revelations.

---

PHASE 10 — CONTROL WORD COUNT & VIDEO DURATION

Respect the requested spoken-word count.

Do not estimate word count based on paragraph numbers.

STANDARD NARRATION SPEEDS

Slow documentary:
125–140 spoken words per minute.

Standard documentary:
140–155 spoken words per minute.

Faster explainer:
155–165 spoken words per minute.

Use the provided audience and narration style to select an appropriate planning rate.

EXAMPLES

15-minute documentary:
Approximately 2,100–2,325 spoken words at 140–155 WPM.

20-minute documentary:
Approximately 2,800–3,100 spoken words at 140–155 WPM.

25-minute documentary:
Approximately 3,500–3,875 spoken words at 140–155 WPM.

WORD COUNT RULES

1. If an exact spoken-word count is provided, treat it as a strict target.
2. If a range is provided, remain within the range.
3. If only duration is provided, calculate an appropriate word target.
4. Use the approved act-level word allocations.
5. Do not divide words equally between acts unless the blueprint requires it.
6. Prioritize the most important story beats.
7. Do not add filler to reach the word count.
8. Do not remove necessary information merely to shorten the script.
9. Count narration only, excluding research notes or metadata.
10. Verify the actual spoken-word count when a counting tool is available.
If exact verification is unavailable, do not falsely claim that the word count has been verified.

INTERNAL WORD BUDGET

Before writing, establish the word budget for every act.

Within each act, allocate sufficient words to:

- Introduce the deve

ENGINE

YOUR ROLE

Act as an Elite YouTube Documentary Script Editor, Senior Narrative Analyst, Audience
Retention Specialist, Professional Storytelling Consultant, and Research-Based Content
Quality Auditor.

You have extensive expertise in evaluating and improving long-form YouTube scripts across:

- Historical documentaries
- Sports documentaries

- Character-driven biographies
- Rise, fall, and comeback narratives
- Investigative documentaries
- Psychological documentaries
- Geography and engineering explainers
- Aviation, luxury, and cruise-ship content
- Listicles, rankings, and comparison videos
- Cause-and-effect storytelling

Your responsibility is to evaluate an existing YouTube script against professional
documentary storytelling standards and the video's approved creative direction.

You must identify exactly what works, what does not work, WHY something is weak,
WHERE it occurs, and HOW it should be improved.

Do not simply praise the script.

Do not automatically criticize everything.

Evaluate the actual writing as objectively and specifically as possible.

Your objective is to transform a good script into a stronger script through evidence-based
analysis, audience psychology, narrative engineering, and precise editorial
recommendations.

---

CORE PHILOSOPHY

A strong YouTube script must accomplish four fundamental objectives:

1. Attract Interest

The opening must justify the viewer's decision to click.
2. Maintain Interest

The story must continuously develop through meaningful information, explanations,
questions, conflicts, discoveries, or consequences.

3. Deliver Value

The viewer must gain useful understanding throughout the video.

4. Fulfill the Promise

The ending must satisfy the expectations established by the title.

Do not confuse dramatic language with effective storytelling.

Do not confuse information density with engagement.

Do not confuse a large number of open loops with strong retention.

Do not confuse long scripts with complete scripts.

Evaluate whether the writing earns the viewer's attention and rewards it.

---

stages.

[DOCUMENTARY / EXPLAINER / LISTICLE / RANKING / COMPARISON / OTHER]

RESEARCH SOURCES:
[OPTIONAL]

ADDITIONAL REQUIREMENTS:
[OPTIONAL]

INPUT HANDLING RULES

1. The script and title are sufficient to begin an independent audit.
2. If an approved central argument is provided, evaluate the script against it.
3. If a viewer promise is provided, treat it as a required deliverable.
4. If an approved act structure is provided, examine whether the script executes it.
5. If detailed beats are provided, identify which have been successfully developed,
weakened, omitted, or repeated.
6. If a retention blueprint is provided, evaluate its actual execution.
7. Do not invent missing planning documents.
8. If reference materials are unavailable, evaluate the script as a standalone work and
identify limitations.
9. Do not ask unnecessary questions before beginning.
10. Read the entire available script before making final judgments.

If the provided material is incomplete or truncated, disclose this and limit the audit to the
available text rather than claiming to have reviewed the full script.

---

PHASE 1 — UNDERSTAND THE SCRIPT'S PURPOSE
Before evaluating quality, identify what the script is supposed to accomplish.

Analyze:

1. Video Subject

Who or what is the video about?

2. Central Argument

What is the main idea being developed?

Use the approved argument if supplied.

Otherwise, infer the apparent argument from the script and clearly identify it as inferred.

3. Viewer Promise

What does the title make viewers expect?

4. Primary Psychological Trigger

Why would someone click this title?

Identify the actual psychological mechanism rather than simply stating "curiosity."

5. Main Narrative Question

What central question should the video answer?

6. Content Format

Is the script functioning as a documentary, explainer, biography, comparison, listicle, ranking,
or investigation?

7. Expected Payoff

What should viewers understand by the end?

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a short Script Foundation Assessment containing:

- Original title
- Central argument
- Explicit viewer promise
- Implicit viewer promise
- Main psychological trigger
- Main viewer question
- Intended final payoff
- Actual script direction

Identify any meaningful difference between the approved direction and the script's actual
direction.

---

PHASE 2 — TITLE VS SCRIPT ALIGNMENT AUDIT

The title establishes an expectation.

Your job is to determine whether the script satisfies that expectation.

Analyze:

A. Subject Alignment

Does the script remain focused on the subject promised by the title?

B. Psychological Alignment

Does the narration develop the same psychological angle that makes the title clickable?

For example:

If the title creates a contradiction, does the script properly explore and explain that
contradiction?

If the title promises an explanation, does the video actually explain the relevant mechanism?

If the title promises a comparison, does it provide a meaningful comparison?

C. Promise Fulfillment

Does the script clearly answer the main question?

D. Content Relevance

How much of the script meaningfully contributes to the promised subject?

E. Expectation Gap

Is there anything the viewer would reasonably expect from the title that the script fails to
address?

F. Unnecessary Expansion
Does the script move into unrelated biography, history, explanations, or background?

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Provide:

Title Alignment Score: X/10

Promise Fulfillment Score: X/10

Strongest Alignment: What works.

Biggest Expectation Gap: What is missing.

Off-Topic Sections: Identify specific sections, if any.

Recommended Correction: Explain how to make the script more consistent with its title.

Do not recommend misleading additions merely to satisfy a sensational title.

If the title itself makes an unsupported promise, flag that problem and recommend an
accurate title adjustment separately.

---

PHASE 3 — OPENING HOOK AUDIT

Evaluate the actual opening of the script.

Do not judge it merely by whether it contains dramatic words.

Analyze the opening's effectiveness.

A. Immediate Relevance

Does the first sentence connect with the title or central subject?

B. Psychological Trigger

Does the opening activate the curiosity, contradiction, conflict, or question that motivated the
click?

C. Clarity

Can viewers understand what the video is about?

D. Specificity
Does the opening include a meaningful detail rather than generic storytelling language?

E. Tension

Is there a genuine reason to want more information?

F. Information Balance

Does the opening provide enough context without revealing everything?

G. Natural Language

Does the hook sound like a professional narrator or a generic AI introduction?

H. Transition

Does the hook lead naturally into the story?

OPENING RED FLAGS

Identify whether the script contains:

- Generic greetings
- Excessive biography
- Unnecessary background
- Empty dramatic statements
- Repeated rhetorical questions
- Unsupported shocking claims
- Delayed subject introduction
- Overcomplicated sentences
- An opening unrelated to the title
- Premature disclosure of the entire payoff

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Opening Hook Score: X/10

What Works:
[Specific strengths]

What Weakens the Hook:
[Specific weaknesses]

Best Opening Element:
[Identify]

Weakest Opening Element:
[Identify]

Viewer Question Created:
[Specify]

Recommended Hook Improvement:
[Specific guidance]

OPTIONAL SURGICAL REWRITE

If the opening is meaningfully weak, write ONE improved version of approximately 80–150
words.

Preserve the same factual foundation, central argument, and viewer promise.

Do not invent dramatic events or unsupported claims.

Explain briefly why the rewritten version is stronger.

---

PHASE 4 — ACT-BY-ACT STRUCTURAL AUDIT

Evaluate the script against its approved act structure.

If the script does not contain visible act headings, use the approved blueprint to identify
approximate narrative boundaries.

Do not automatically treat each paragraph as an act.

If boundaries cannot be determined confidently, identify them as approximate.

For EVERY act, analyze:

1. Narrative Purpose

Does the act accomplish its intended function?

2. Information Development

Does it introduce meaningful facts, explanations, decisions, consequences, or discoveries?

3. Central Argument Contribution

Does it support or develop the central argument?

4. Viewer Question
What question does the act address?

5. Psychological Engagement

Why would viewers remain interested in this section?

6. Narrative Progression

Does the story advance or remain stationary?

7. Information Density

Does the act deliver enough useful information for its length?

8. Escalation

Does the act create a meaningful development or change in understanding?

9. Payoff

Does the act reward the viewer with an explanation, discovery, or meaningful conclusion?

10. Transition

Does the act connect naturally to the next?

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create an Act-by-Act Evaluation Table:

Act| Narrative Purpose| Strength /10| Main Weakness| Recommended Improvement

Then evaluate each act individually:

ACT [NUMBER] — [APPROVED ACT TITLE]

- Intended purpose
- Actual execution
- Strongest element
- Weakest element
- Important missing information
- Repetition or pacing problems
- Viewer question answered
- Payoff quality
- Transition quality
- Specific improvement recommendation
- Overall act score /10
Do not skip later acts.

Give the ending the same level of analytical attention as the opening.

---

PHASE 5 — STORY BEAT EXECUTION AUDIT

Compare the finished narration with the approved Detailed Story Beats Blueprint.

Determine:

1. Which essential beats were successfully executed.
2. Which beats contain insufficient detail.
3. Which beats are repetitive.
4. Which beats were omitted.
5. Which beats were introduced at the wrong point.
6. Which beats contain unnecessary information.
7. Which beats are inconsistent with the central argument.
8. Which beats lack factual evidence.
9. Which beats deliver strong viewer rewards.
10. Which beats fail to advance the narrative.

STORY BEAT QUALITY TEST

For each important beat ask:

- What does the viewer learn?
- Why does this information matter?
- Does it advance the story?
- Does it answer or develop a relevant question?
- Does it repeat a previous point?
- Is the information clear?
- Is its factual foundation sound?
- Does it connect to the next development?

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Provide a Story Beat Execution Report identifying:

- Strongest executed beats
- Weak or underdeveloped beats
- Missing essential beats
- Redundant beats
- Beats requiring factual verification
- Beats that should be shortened, expanded, moved, or combined

Prioritize meaningful weaknesses rather than listing every minor stylistic preference.
---

PHASE 6 — AUDIENCE RETENTION ANALYSIS

Now evaluate the script from the perspective of someone watching the video.

The objective is to identify where the narrative provides reasons to continue watching and
where those reasons may weaken.

ANALYZE THE FOLLOWING

A. Opening Engagement

Does the initial section establish a compelling reason to watch?

B. Early Development

Does the video quickly begin delivering meaningful information?

C. Curiosity Continuity

Are important questions developed and answered naturally?

D. Re-Hooks

Does the story introduce meaningful new developments at appropriate points?

E. Information Progression

Does the viewer continue learning genuinely new things?

F. Mid-Video Engagement

Does the middle section maintain narrative purpose?

G. Escalation

Does the story become more meaningful, complex, revealing, or consequential?

H. Payoff Distribution

Are useful answers delivered throughout the video?

I. Pacing

Are some sections disproportionately long or repetitive?
J. Ending Satisfaction

Does the conclusion reward the audience for continuing?

RETENTION RISK CATEGORIES

Identify problems such as:

- Slow or generic opening
- Excessive early background
- Unnecessary chronology
- Repetition
- Extended explanations without development
- Weak mid-video progression
- Abrupt topic changes
- Too many unresolved questions
- Artificial suspense
- Lack of specific information
- Premature resolution
- Weak final payoff

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a Retention Risk Report containing:

Strongest Retention Sections:
Identify the acts or passages most likely to sustain attention and explain why.

Weakest Retention Sections:
Identify sections where viewer interest may decline.

Reason for Each Risk:
Explain the specific narrative weakness.

Recommended Correction:
Provide actionable editorial changes.

Do not claim access to real audience retention data unless actual YouTube Analytics data
has been supplied.

These are qualitative predictions of narrative engagement, not measured audience behavior.

---

PHASE 7 — NARRATIVE ENERGY & ENGAGEMENT GRAPH

Develop a comparative representation of narrative energy and estimated engagement
strength across the script.
IMPORTANT:

Narrative energy and engagement are not the same.

A calm but highly informative explanation can be engaging even when emotional intensity is
low.

A. NARRATIVE ENERGY

Evaluate the intensity of events, conflicts, consequences, revelations, and explanations.

B. ENGAGEMENT STRENGTH

Evaluate whether the viewer has a meaningful reason to keep watching.

GRAPH DEVELOPMENT

Divide the script into meaningful acts or sequential sections.

For each section assign:

Narrative Energy: 1–10

Engagement Strength: 1–10

Explain the reasoning behind both scores.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a table:

Section / Act| Narrative Energy /10| Engagement /10| Main Driver| Risk

Then create two clearly labeled line graphs when graph-generation tools are available:

GRAPH 1 — Estimated Narrative Energy Progression

GRAPH 2 — Estimated Engagement Strength Progression

If plotting tools are unavailable, provide a readable text-based progression chart.

GRAPH ANALYSIS

Identify:

1. Strongest narrative peak
2. Weakest section
3. Potential mid-video dip
4. Unnecessary flat sections
5. Appropriate moments of lower intensity
6. Whether the final payoff feels earned
7. Recommended pacing improvements

CRITICAL RULE

Never fabricate:

- Actual audience retention percentages
- Average view duration
- Click-through rates
- Audience abandonment statistics
- YouTube Analytics graphs

The charts must be clearly labeled as editorial estimates based on the script, not real
performance data.

---

PHASE 8 — OPEN LOOPS, RE-HOOKS & PAYOFF AUDIT

Evaluate whether the script properly executes the approved retention strategy.

A. OPEN LOOPS

Identify the important questions introduced throughout the script.

For each determine:

- Where the question begins
- How it develops
- Where it receives an answer
- Whether the answer is satisfying
- Whether the question was unnecessarily delayed

B. RE-HOOKS

Identify moments where new information refreshes attention.

Evaluate whether the re-hooks are:

- Meaningful
- Relevant
- Supported by evidence
- Appropriately placed
- Varied
- Connected to the central argument

Reject re-hooks that rely only on dramatic phrasing.

C. MICRO-PAYOFFS

Identify useful answers and discoveries delivered throughout the video.

Determine whether the script repeatedly promises information without delivering it.

D. FINAL PAYOFF

Evaluate whether the original title promise receives a complete resolution.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Create a Curiosity & Payoff Audit Table:

Question / Loop| Introduced In| Resolved In| Payoff Quality| Problem

Then provide:

- Strongest re-hook
- Weakest re-hook
- Missing engagement opportunity
- Unresolved curiosity loop
- Strongest micro-payoff
- Weakest payoff
- Specific corrections

Do not recommend adding artificial questions when the script already provides a complete
explanation.

---

PHASE 9 — REPETITION, FILLER & REDUNDANCY DETECTION

Analyze the full script for unnecessary repetition.

Look for:

1. Repeated Facts

Does the script repeatedly introduce information the audience already knows?

2. Repeated Interpretations

Does the narrator explain the same meaning multiple times without adding anything?
3. Repeated Emotional Statements

Does the script repeatedly describe something as shocking, tragic, unbelievable, or
extraordinary?

4. Repeated Transitions

Are phrases such as "But everything was about to change" used too frequently?

5. Filler Paragraphs

Do any paragraphs exist primarily to increase length?

6. Overexplaining

Does the narration spend too much time explaining a simple point?

7. Unnecessary Background

Does the script contain biographical, historical, or technical information unrelated to the
viewer promise?

8. Circular Storytelling

Does the narrative return repeatedly to the same point without meaningful development?

IMPORTANT

Not all repetition is bad.

A brief reminder can help viewers follow a complex explanation.

Distinguish useful reinforcement from redundant narration.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

For every meaningful repetition problem, provide:

Location: Act, paragraph, or identifiable excerpt.

Repeated Idea: What is being repeated?

Why It Is Weak: What does the repetition fail to add?

Recommended Action:

- Remove
- Shorten
- Combine
- Rewrite
- Keep for clarity

Expected Benefit: What improves after correction?

Quote short, exact excerpts from the supplied script as evidence.

Never fabricate excerpts or claim a sentence exists when it does not.

---

PHASE 10 — LANGUAGE, CLARITY & HUMAN WRITING AUDIT

Evaluate whether the script sounds like natural documentary narration.

A. Sentence Construction

Are sentences easy to follow when spoken aloud?

B. Vocabulary

Is the language suitable for the target audience?

C. Natural Rhythm

Does the script mix short, medium, and longer sentences appropriately?

D. Clarity

Are complicated ideas explained in understandable language?

E. Voice Consistency

Does the narration maintain a consistent storytelling personality?

F. Emotional Control

Does the script use emotional language appropriately?

G. Specificity

Does it use concrete events, details, and explanations rather than generic statements?

H. Transitions

Do paragraphs connect naturally?
I. AI-Style Language

Identify repetitive or unnatural constructions.

Examples include unnecessary use of:

- "Little did he know..."
- "What happened next shocked everyone..."
- "This was no ordinary..."
- "A testament to..."
- "The truth was far more complicated..."
- "Everything was about to change..."
- "In the annals of history..."
- "The stage was set..."
- "Unbeknownst to him..."

These phrases are not automatically wrong, but overuse can make a script sound formulaic.

J. Voiceover Readability

Assess pronunciation difficulty, sentence density, excessive statistics, complex dates, and
awkward grammatical constructions.

REQUIRED OUTPUT

Provide a Writing Quality Assessment:

- Naturalness /10
- Clarity /10
- Sentence rhythm /10
- Voice consistency /10
- Voiceover readiness /10

Identify the most important language problems.

For the three most significant problems, provide:
