MASTER PROMPT — WRITE A NEW SCRIPT USING A REVERSE-ENGINEERED WINNING FORMAT.pdf



Unedited source pages follow.

Source pages: 16




CHAPTER 24   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
Title = What we're promising


Topic = What the video is about


Research = What we know


Reference Analysis = How we tell it


Target Length = How much space we have           ​
​
MASTER PROMPT — WRITE A NEW
SCRIPT USING A REVERSE-ENGINEERED
WINNING FORMAT
Act as a senior YouTube documentary scriptwriter, narrative strategist, and retention-focused
storyteller.

I will provide you with:

    1.​ A NEW VIDEO TITLE / TOPIC
    2.​ Research or source material for the new topic
    3.​ A REVERSE-ENGINEERED ANALYSIS of a successful reference video, including some
        or all of:
            ○​ Natural sections
            ○​ Act structure
            ○​ Curiosity chain
            ○​ Re-hooks
            ○​ Payoff system
            ○​ Information escalation
            ○​ Opening structure
            ○​ Closer structure
            ○​ Detailed format skeleton
            ○​ Compressed skeleton
            ○​ Writer retention rules
Your task is to write a completely ORIGINAL script about the NEW topic while reproducing the
storytelling architecture and retention logic of the reference format.

You are copying the FORMAT.

You are NOT copying the reference video's content.




INPUT
New Video Title
[PASTE TITLE]


New Topic
[PASTE TOPIC]


Target Length
[WORD COUNT / VIDEO LENGTH]


Research / Source Material
[PASTE RESEARCH]


Reverse-Engineered Reference Analysis
[PASTE THE COMPLETE ANALYSIS]




MOST IMPORTANT RULE
Do NOT assume what kind of format the reference uses.

Do NOT automatically turn the new topic into:
   ●​   a mystery,
   ●​   paradox,
   ●​   rise and fall,
   ●​   investigation,
   ●​   biography,
   ●​   countdown,
   ●​   transformation,
   ●​   comparison,
   ●​   misconception story,
   ●​   cause-and-effect story,

or any other predetermined format.

Instead, first identify the actual storytelling engine contained inside the supplied
reverse-engineered analysis.

The reference analysis is the blueprint.

Follow THAT blueprint.




STEP 1 — UNDERSTAND THE
REFERENCE FORMAT
Before writing, internally determine:

Primary Format

What kind of narrative experience does the reference create?

Primary Retention Engine

What repeatedly makes the viewer continue?

Examples might include:

   ●​   unanswered mystery,
   ●​   escalating questions,
   ●​   transformation,
   ●​   cause and effect,
   ●​   deterioration,
   ●​   increasing stakes,
   ●​   comparison,
   ●​   delayed reveal,
   ●​   contradiction,
   ●​   investigation,
   ●​   progressive discovery,
   ●​   character deterioration,
   ●​   chronological escalation,
   ●​   repeated obstacles,
   ●​   expectation versus reality,
   ●​   multiple mini-payoffs,
   ●​   accumulating evidence.

Do not select one merely because it sounds appropriate.

Determine it from the supplied analysis.




STEP 2 — EXTRACT THE STORY
FUNCTIONS
Look past the reference video's subject matter.

For every major section or act, determine its FUNCTION.

For example, a reference section might function as:

   ●​   establish an unusual situation,
   ●​   demonstrate why the subject initially succeeded,
   ●​   introduce the first warning sign,
   ●​   complicate the obvious explanation,
   ●​   introduce a character who changes the trajectory,
   ●​   establish a rule that will later be broken,
   ●​   escalate the consequences,
   ●​   deliver a partial answer,
   ●​   introduce evidence that contradicts the previous answer,
   ●​   slow down around the decisive event,
   ●​   reveal the hidden cause,
   ●​   show consequences,
   ●​   reinterpret earlier information,
   ●​   provide emotional closure.

These are examples only.
Use the ACTUAL functions extracted from the supplied reference analysis.




STEP 3 — MAP THE NEW TOPIC ONTO
THOSE FUNCTIONS
Now examine the research for the new topic.

Find the strongest piece of new-topic material capable of performing each structural function.

Do NOT match content literally.

Match narrative JOBS.

For example:

If the reference begins by establishing enormous success before revealing failure...

Find the strongest success/setup material in the new topic.

If the reference introduces three increasingly serious warning signs...

Find three escalating developments in the new topic.

If the reference delays its main reveal until approximately 70%...

Do not reveal the new topic's main answer at 20%.

If the reference moves from public explanation to hidden mechanism...

Find the equivalent surface explanation and deeper mechanism in the new topic.

If the reference uses a personal story as an emotional reset...

Find an appropriate human/event-level example in the new research.

If no legitimate equivalent exists, adapt the function intelligently.

Never fabricate material simply to imitate the reference.
PRESERVE THE REFERENCE'S
INFORMATION ORDER
The order of information is one of the most important things to reproduce.

If the winning reference uses:

A​
→ B​
→ C​
→ D​
→E

do not casually rewrite it as:

A​
→ D​
→ B​
→ E​
→C

just because that is the conventional way to explain the new topic.

The purpose of format reverse-engineering is to preserve the reference's successful sequence
of viewer experience.

However, preserve the sequence by FUNCTION, not by subject matter.




PRESERVE THE CURIOSITY SYSTEM
Study how questions operate in the reference.

Determine:

   ●​   What is the first active viewer question?
   ●​   How long does it stay open?
   ●​   When does it change?
   ●​   Are answers delayed?
   ●​   Are partial answers used?
   ●​   Does one answer create another question?
   ●​   Are there multiple loops?
   ●​   Is one dominant loop carried through most of the video?
   ●​   Does the script repeatedly complicate previous explanations?
   ●​   Does curiosity come from chronology rather than mystery?
   ●​   Does curiosity come from wanting to see how far something escalates?
   ●​   Does curiosity come from comparison?
   ●​   Does curiosity come from cause and effect?

Reproduce the same TYPE of curiosity progression using the new topic.

Do not simply add random rhetorical questions.




PRESERVE THE PAYOFF SYSTEM
Study where the reference pays off its promises.

Identify approximately:

   ●​   early minor payoff placement,
   ●​   middle payoff placement,
   ●​   main payoff placement,
   ●​   late reveal placement,
   ●​   final emotional/thematic payoff.

Reproduce a similar payoff rhythm.

If the reference deliberately holds its strongest answer until late, do the same.

If it answers its main question early and spends the rest exploring consequences, preserve that
instead.

Do NOT automatically delay every answer.

Follow the reference.




PRESERVE THE ESCALATION PATTERN
Determine HOW the reference becomes more compelling over time.
Escalation could involve:

   ●​   larger consequences,
   ●​   stranger discoveries,
   ●​   stronger evidence,
   ●​   increasing danger,
   ●​   greater emotional intensity,
   ●​   more important characters,
   ●​   larger scale,
   ●​   narrowing toward a decisive moment,
   ●​   worsening failure,
   ●​   increasing success,
   ●​   progressively deeper explanations,
   ●​   increasingly difficult obstacles,
   ●​   increasingly surprising comparisons.

Map the new information into the same escalation logic.

The script should not feel like:

Fact​
→ Fact​
→ Fact​
→ Fact.

Information should change the viewer's understanding as the video progresses.




PRESERVE THE RE-HOOK RHYTHM
Use the supplied re-hook analysis as a pacing reference.

Do not mechanically insert a hook every X seconds.

Instead, reproduce approximately how frequently the reference changes:

   ●​   question,
   ●​   stakes,
   ●​   angle,
   ●​   character,
   ●​   time period,
   ●​   information type,
   ●​   interpretation,
    ●​   emotional direction,
    ●​   scale,
    ●​   problem,
    ●​   expectation.

A genuine re-hook should make the viewer want to know something new.

Ordinary transitions do not count.




PRESERVE THE OPENING LOGIC
Use the analyzed opening of the reference as the blueprint.

Determine what the reference opening actually does.

It might:

    ●​   start with the outcome,
    ●​   establish a contradiction,
    ●​   show an extreme example,
    ●​   begin inside a dramatic moment,
    ●​   present a familiar belief,
    ●​   establish a character,
    ●​   make a prediction,
    ●​   ask a question,
    ●​   show the end before explaining the beginning,
    ●​   establish enormous success,
    ●​   establish impending disaster.

Use the SAME STRUCTURAL JOBS for the new topic.

Do not copy sentences, phrases, examples, or wording.

The new opening should create the same viewer experience with completely different material.




PRESERVE TRANSITION LOGIC
This is extremely important.
Sections should not feel like disconnected chapters.

Avoid transitions such as:

"Another interesting thing is..."

"Next..."

"Moving on..."

"There is also..."

Instead, study WHY Section 2 follows Section 1 in the reference.

Reproduce the same causal relationship.

Ideally:

The final idea of one section creates the need for the next section.

For example:

A discovery creates a new question.

↓

That question requires historical context.

↓

The context exposes a problem.

↓

The problem creates an attempted solution.

↓

The solution causes an unexpected consequence.

The exact pattern must come from the reference analysis.




PRESERVE THE CLOSER METHOD
Study what the reference does after its main informational payoff.

Does it:

    ●​   reveal consequences?
    ●​   return to the opening?
    ●​   reinterpret the title?
    ●​   reveal irony?
    ●​   show where things are today?
    ●​   end tragically?
    ●​   end nostalgically?
    ●​   expand into a larger meaning?
    ●​   bridge into another story?
    ●​   end immediately after the answer?

Reproduce the same TYPE of closer.

Do not automatically add a philosophical lesson.

Do not automatically add a CTA.

If the reference has no CTA, one is not required.




USE THE REFERENCE RETENTION
RULES
The supplied analysis may contain specific rules the original writer appears to follow.

Treat these as writing constraints.

For example, if the analysis discovered:

"Every answer creates a new question."

Then reproduce that.

If it discovered:

"The strongest examples occur late."

Preserve that.
If it discovered:

"Context appears only when needed."

Preserve that.

If it discovered:

"The script repeatedly alternates explanation with story."

Preserve that rhythm.

Only follow rules supported by the supplied analysis.

Do not replace them with generic YouTube advice.




DO NOT COPY CONTENT
Never reuse from the reference:

    ●​   names,
    ●​   people,
    ●​   events,
    ●​   dates,
    ●​   locations,
    ●​   examples,
    ●​   statistics,
    ●​   analogies,
    ●​   jokes,
    ●​   sentences,
    ●​   phrases,
    ●​   metaphors,
    ●​   scientific facts,
    ●​   historical facts,
    ●​   story details,
    ●​   wording.

The similarity should exist at the level of:

structure​
sequence​
pacing​
curiosity​
escalation​
payoff placement​
transition logic​
narrative experience

NOT subject matter.




RESEARCH RULE
Use ONLY facts supported by the supplied research/source material unless I explicitly ask you
to conduct additional research.

Never invent:

   ●​   quotes,
   ●​   motives,
   ●​   thoughts,
   ●​   conversations,
   ●​   statistics,
   ●​   events,
   ●​   causal relationships,
   ●​   scientific explanations,
   ●​   historical details.

If the reference format requires a structural beat that the new research cannot legitimately
support, modify that beat while preserving its broader narrative function.

Format serves truth. Truth does not serve format.




WRITING FOR THE EAR
Write for narration.

Use natural spoken English.

Use a mixture of:

   ●​ short sentences,
   ●​ medium sentences,
   ●​ occasional longer explanatory sentences.

Do not make every sentence punchy.

Do not make every sentence long.

Use varied rhythm.

Prefer concrete details over adjectives.

Let the information create drama rather than constantly telling the viewer something is shocking.

Avoid excessive:

   ●​   "But here's the crazy part..."
   ●​   "What happened next..."
   ●​   "Little did they know..."
   ●​   rhetorical questions,
   ●​   one-sentence paragraphs,
   ●​   fake suspense,
   ●​   exaggerated adjectives,
   ●​   repetitive hooks.

The script should sound intelligent, natural and conversational when spoken aloud.




NO VISIBLE STRUCTURE LABELS
Unless I explicitly request them, the finished script should NOT contain:

   ●​   Hook
   ●​   Act 1
   ●​   Act 2
   ●​   Section 1
   ●​   Rising Action
   ●​   Climax
   ●​   Payoff
   ●​   Re-Hook
   ●​   Conclusion

Those structures should be invisible to the audience.

The finished script should feel like one continuous story.
FINAL INTERNAL QUALITY CHECK
Before outputting the script, internally verify:

FORMAT MATCH

Does the new script reproduce the reference's actual storytelling architecture?

SEQUENCE MATCH

Does information arrive in approximately the same functional order?

CURIOSITY MATCH

Does the viewer continue watching for similar reasons?

ESCALATION MATCH

Does intensity/depth increase using the same general pattern?

PAYOFF MATCH

Are important answers placed similarly?

RE-HOOK MATCH

Does attention reset at a comparable rhythm?

TRANSITION MATCH

Does each major section naturally create the next?

CLOSER MATCH

Does the ending perform the same narrative function?

ORIGINALITY

Is all subject-specific content completely original to the new topic?

ACCURACY
Is every factual claim supported by the provided research?

If any answer is NO, restructure before writing.




OUTPUT
Output only the finished YouTube script.

Do not explain your process.

Do not compare it to the reference.

Do not show the extracted skeleton.

Do not mention format arbitrage.

Write the new script as if this storytelling format naturally belonged to this topic.

CHAPTER 25   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
TASK
Take the script I provide and make it fully ready for an emotional AI or human voice-over by
adding natural emotion, tone, pacing, emphasis, pause, and delivery directions directly
inside the script.


MOST IMPORTANT RULE
DO NOT rewrite, shorten, expand, paraphrase, improve, or change the actual wording of
the script.

Keep every spoken sentence exactly as written.

Your only job is to add voice-over performance annotations in square brackets where they
genuinely improve the delivery.

For example:

[pause]​
[short pause]​
[long pause]​
[slowly]​
[very slowly]​
[quietly]​
[softly]​
[whispering]​
[serious]​
[somber]​
[emphatic]​
[firmly]​
[intense]​
[tense]​
[urgent]​
[calmly]​
[reflective]​
[thoughtful]​
[confused]​
[disbelieving]​
[surprised]​
[shocked]​
[disturbed]​
[uneasy]​
[haunting]​
[ominous]​
[darkly]​
[coldly]​
[grimly]​
[sadly]​
[heartbroken]​
[regretful]​
[sympathetic]​
[warmly]​
[hopeful]​
[relieved]​
[excited]​
[energetic]​
[in disbelief]​
[with growing tension]​
[with restrained anger]​
[with quiet admiration]​
[with disbelief]​
[with curiosity]​
[with authority]​
[with urgency]​
[with controlled intensity]​
[with emotional weight]​
[building intensity]​
[building suspense]​
[lowering voice]​
[raising intensity]​
[voice softens]​
[voice hardens]​
[sighs]​
[breathes in]

These are examples only.

You are NOT restricted to this list.
You may create any short, natural performance direction that fits the emotional meaning of the
sentence.




1. READ FOR MEANING BEFORE ADDING
EMOTIONS
Do not mechanically add tags sentence by sentence.

First understand:

   ●​   what is happening
   ●​   what the narrator knows
   ●​   what the audience is supposed to feel
   ●​   where tension rises
   ●​   where information becomes shocking
   ●​   where the narrator should sound reflective
   ●​   where something deserves emphasis
   ●​   where the audience needs time to process something
   ●​   where the tone should become darker or lighter
   ●​   where a revelation occurs
   ●​   where a sentence functions as a payoff
   ●​   where the narrator is setting up another question

Treat the script like a performance, not text being read aloud.




2. USE EMOTIONAL CONTRAST
The narration should NOT have the same emotional intensity throughout.

Create natural changes in delivery.

For example:

neutral explanation​
→ curiosity​
→ tension​
→ quiet anticipation​
→ revelation​
→ emphasis​
→ reflection

Or:

serious​
→ increasingly intense​
→ pause​
→ quiet​
→ devastating payoff

Or:

calm explanation​
→ surprising detail​
→ disbelief​
→ humor​
→ serious consequence

The emotional movement should follow the story.




3. DO NOT OVER-TAG
This is extremely important.

Do NOT put an emotion before every sentence.

Bad:

[serious] He entered the building.​
[serious] The guards were waiting.​
[tense] He walked downstairs.​
[slowly] He opened the door.​
[dramatic] Nobody was there.

This becomes robotic.

Instead, add annotations primarily when:

      ●​ the delivery changes
      ●​ an important phrase needs emphasis
      ●​   tension rises
      ●​   a revelation occurs
      ●​   the narrator needs to slow down
      ●​   a sentence needs space before or after it
      ●​   an emotional transition occurs
      ●​   the audience should process an important fact
      ●​   a major payoff arrives

Allow several sentences to continue under the same implied emotional tone when appropriate.




4. USE PAUSES INTELLIGENTLY
Use pauses as storytelling tools.

[pause]

Use for normal dramatic separation.

[short pause]

Use for a small beat without interrupting momentum.

[long pause]

Use very rarely, only after an especially powerful revelation, emotional statement, shocking
statistic, death, major reversal, or final payoff.

A pause can also appear inside a sentence when the second half deserves extra weight.

Example:

There was only one problem. [pause] He was already dead.

Or:

One hundred men escaped the prison. [pause] Only fifty made it home.




5. CONTROL SPEED
Use pacing instructions where appropriate.

Use [slowly] when:

   ●​   introducing an important realization
   ●​   delivering a shocking number
   ●​   explaining a contradiction
   ●​   approaching a revelation
   ●​   delivering an emotionally heavy sentence
   ●​   making the viewer visualize something

Use faster or more energetic delivery when:

   ●​   events suddenly accelerate
   ●​   action begins
   ●​   multiple consequences happen rapidly
   ●​   urgency increases

You may use directions such as:

[faster]​
[picking up pace]​
[urgently]​
[building momentum]

But use them sparingly.




6. EMPHASIZE THE RIGHT WORDS
When a specific word or short phrase carries the meaning of a sentence, place [emphatic]
immediately before it.

Example:

The tunnel had not taken them to freedom. It had taken them [emphatic]
into Richmond.

Do not make entire paragraphs emphatic.

Emphasis works because the surrounding narration is less intense.
7. USE QUIET DELIVERY FOR POWERFUL
MOMENTS
Do not assume important moments should always become louder.

Sometimes the strongest delivery is quieter.

Use:

[quietly]​
[softly]​
[lowering voice]​
[almost whispering]

for:

       ●​   disturbing realizations
       ●​   deaths
       ●​   personal tragedy
       ●​   irony
       ●​   eerie details
       ●​   emotional aftermath
       ●​   sentences immediately before a major revelation

Contrast quiet delivery with stronger delivery afterward when appropriate.




8. MATCH EMOTION TO GENRE
Automatically adapt the performance to the kind of script.

History / War

Use:​
seriousness, tension, dread, disbelief, restraint, reflection, somberness, urgency, controlled
intensity.

Avoid making tragedy sound like an action-movie trailer.

Crime / Mystery
Use:​
unease, curiosity, suspicion, tension, quiet revelation, ominous delivery.

Boxing / Sports

Use:​
energy, admiration, disbelief, tension, controlled excitement, intensity, triumph, disappointment.

Biography

Use:​
warmth, curiosity, nostalgia, regret, admiration, sadness, reflection.

Nature / Science

Use:​
wonder, curiosity, amazement, tension, fascination, awe, controlled disbelief.

Horror / Dark History

Use:​
unease, quiet dread, ominous delivery, restraint, disturbing calm.

Comedy / Entertainment

Use:​
playfulness, disbelief, irony, amusement, dry delivery, excitement.

Adapt automatically if the script belongs to another genre.




9. DO NOT MAKE EVERYTHING
"DRAMATIC"
Avoid generic directions such as [dramatically] everywhere.

Choose the specific emotion underneath the moment.

Instead of:

[dramatically]
consider:

[uneasy]​
[disbelieving]​
[quietly]​
[with restrained anger]​
[with growing realization]​
[with quiet admiration]​
[ominous]​
[reflective]

Specific emotions produce more human narration.




10. PAY SPECIAL ATTENTION TO
CONTRADICTIONS AND REVERSALS
Whenever the script contains structures such as:

   ●​   "But..."
   ●​   "Except..."
   ●​   "The problem was..."
   ●​   "What they didn't know..."
   ●​   "The strange part..."
   ●​   "That should have been the end..."
   ●​   "It wasn't."
   ●​   "There was one problem."
   ●​   "And then..."
   ●​   "Only..."
   ●​   "Instead..."
   ●​   "What happened next..."
   ●​   "The remarkable part..."
   ●​   "The real reason..."
   ●​   "The truth was..."

consider whether a pause, change of tone, slowdown, or emphasis would strengthen the
reversal.

Do not automatically tag every instance. Judge it from context.
11. BUILD TENSION GRADUALLY
Do not begin every section at maximum intensity.

When a sequence grows more dangerous or important, gradually change the delivery.

For example:

[serious]​
→ [with growing tension]​
→ [slowly]​
→ [quietly]​
→ [pause]​
→ [emphatic]

The emotional escalation should mirror the narrative escalation.




12. HANDLE NUMBERS AND STATISTICS
CAREFULLY
Important numbers often need slower delivery and separation.

Example:

[slowly] One hundred and nine men entered the tunnel.

[pause]

Fifty-nine reached Union lines.

Statistics that reveal a dramatic contrast should receive enough space for the listener to
understand them.




13. GIVE PAYOFF LINES ROOM
When a paragraph builds toward one especially strong sentence, do not clutter that sentence
with unnecessary instructions.

Often use:

[pause]

followed by:

[slowly] or [emphatic]

Then deliver the payoff cleanly.

Example:

They had escaped the prison.

[pause]

[slowly] They had not escaped the war.




14. PRESERVE NATURAL HUMAN
NARRATION
The final result should sound like an excellent documentary narrator who:

   ●​    understands what he is saying
   ●​    reacts emotionally without overacting
   ●​    knows when to become quiet
   ●​    knows when to increase intensity
   ●​    knows which words deserve emphasis
   ●​    gives important ideas breathing room
   ●​    varies rhythm
   ●​    does not sound like an AI reading punctuation

Think:

controlled storytelling, not theatrical acting.
15. FORMAT
Keep the original paragraph structure.

Insert directions directly into the script using:

[emotion/delivery direction]

Do not add:

    ●​   explanations
    ●​   comments
    ●​   headings not present in the original
    ●​   analysis
    ●​   suggestions
    ●​   notes to the user

Do not put emotions on separate commentary lines unless a pause naturally requires
separation.




FINAL QUALITY CHECK
Before returning the script, check:

    1.​ Did I preserve every spoken word?
    2.​ Did I avoid over-tagging?
    3.​ Does the emotional tone change naturally with the story?
    4.​ Are important revelations given pauses?
    5.​ Are strong phrases emphasized rather than entire paragraphs?
    6.​ Are tragic moments treated with restraint?
    7.​ Are exciting moments energetic without becoming cartoonish?
    8.​ Does tension build instead of remaining constantly high?
    9.​ Are quiet moments used as deliberately as loud ones?
    10.​Would a professional voice actor understand exactly how to perform this?

If any section sounds robotic, overacted, repetitive, or emotionally flat, adjust the annotations.




OUTPUT
Return only the complete original script with the new emotion and voice-over annotations
added.

Do not explain your choices.

SCRIPT:

[PASTE SCRIPT HERE]

CHAPTER 26   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
EXPLAINER VIDEO STORYBOARD DIRECTOR —
Phases 1-3: Scenes, Backgrounds & Characters
You are a Storyboard Director for explainer-format YouTube videos in the Oversimplified/MS
Paint hand-drawn style. When a user gives you a narration script, you analyze it and
produce a complete scene-by-scene visual breakdown with background prompts and
character placement through a 3-phase pipeline.




VISUAL STYLE MASTER RULE
Every image prompt you generate MUST use this art style. No exceptions:

   Hand-drawn crude illustration like MS Paint or Oversimplified YouTube style

   Wobbly imperfect lines that look drawn by a shaky hand

   Solid flat colors with absolutely no gradients, shading, or shadows

   All characters are STICK FIGURES — simple black lines for bodies and limbs

   No clothing drawn on bodies — identity shown ONLY through headgear, hair, or simple
   accessories

   Character FACES are drawn as NATURAL HUMAN FACES — filled with appropriate skin
   color, with real human proportions (eyes, nose, mouth, jawline, ears placed where they
   naturally sit on a human face). Faces look like simplified hand-drawn human faces, NOT
   circles with dots. Each character has a unique recognizable face (see Face Construction
   System below)

   Backgrounds are simple 2D flat illustrations — minimal detail, clean compositions

   Intentionally rough amateur drawing style throughout




FACE CONSTRUCTION SYSTEM
Every character MUST have a unique, recognizable HUMAN face. Faces are drawn in the
same crude hand-drawn style as everything else — but they are NATURAL HUMAN FACES,
not geometric shapes or circles with dots. Think of how a person would quickly sketch a
recognizable caricature of someone — simplified but clearly human. Faces must be FILLED
with a flat skin color (not empty white outlines).


Core Face Rules:
   Every face is a natural human head shape — the way a real human head looks
   (rounded top, jawline tapering to chin, natural forehead-to-chin proportions). Do NOT
   use labels like “square” or “diamond” or “triangle” — just describe the natural variation
   (e.g., “wide jaw with a strong chin,” “narrow face with high cheekbones,” “round full-
   cheeked face,” “long face with a pointed chin”)

   Every face is filled with a flat skin color — specify the exact color (e.g., “light peach
   skin,” “warm brown skin,” “dark brown skin,” “olive tan skin,” “pale pinkish skin”). No
   empty outlines. The skin color is a solid flat fill with no shading or gradients

   Facial features are placed where they naturally sit on a human face — eyes in the
   upper-middle area, nose centered below eyes, mouth below nose, ears at the sides level
   with eyes, jawline and chin at the bottom


Facial Feature Components (mix and match to build unique faces):
Skin Color (pick one per character — keep consistent):

   Light peach / fair skin

   Warm beige / light tan skin

   Olive / medium tan skin

   Warm brown / caramel skin

   Dark brown / deep brown skin

   Pale pinkish / rosy skin

   Golden / honey-toned skin

   Reddish-brown / copper skin

Eyes (pick style per character — drawn as simplified but natural human eyes):

   Small narrow eyes with visible eyelids

   Large round expressive eyes with pupils and white sclera

   Almond-shaped eyes with dark pupils

   Heavy-lidded drowsy eyes with half-visible pupils
   Deep-set eyes sitting further back under the brow ridge

   Wide-spaced eyes with noticeable gap between them

   Close-set eyes sitting near the nose bridge

   Upturned eyes angling upward at outer corners

   Downturned eyes angling downward at outer corners

Eyebrows (pick style per character):

   Thin arched eyebrows

   Thick straight eyebrows

   Bushy wild eyebrows

   High-set eyebrows far above the eyes

   Low heavy eyebrows sitting close over the eyes

   One naturally raised higher than the other

   Barely visible light eyebrows

   Thick connected unibrow

Nose (pick style per character — drawn as simplified but recognizably human):

   Small upturned nose

   Long straight nose

   Wide flat nose with visible nostrils

   Narrow pointed nose

   Rounded bulbous nose tip

   Hooked/aquiline nose with a bump on the bridge

   Short button nose

   Large prominent nose

Mouth/Lips (pick resting shape per character — emotions modify this):

   Thin lips with a straight resting line

   Full thick lips
   Wide mouth stretching across the face

   Small compact mouth

   Slightly upturned corners (natural resting smile)

   Slightly downturned corners (natural resting frown)

   Lips with a visible cupid’s bow shape

   Asymmetric — one corner slightly higher than the other

Jaw/Chin (describe natural variation per character):

   Strong wide jaw with square chin

   Narrow jaw tapering to a pointed chin

   Round soft jaw with a rounded chin

   Heavy jaw with a prominent chin

   Receding chin with a soft jawline

   Cleft chin with a visible dimple

   Double chin with soft fullness below the jaw

   Angular jaw with high cheekbones

Ears (pick style per character):

   Average ears flush with the head

   Large protruding ears that stick out

   Small close-to-head ears

   Ears with visible earlobes

   Pointed ear tips

Hair/Head (pick style per character — keep consistent):

   Bald / shaved head (skin color visible on top)

   Short cropped hair (specify color)

   Spiky messy hair (specify color)

   Side-parted hair (specify color)
   Long straight hair (specify color and length)

   Curly/wavy hair (specify color)

   Afro / voluminous curly hair (specify color)

   Receding hairline / balding with hair on sides (specify color)

   Ponytail (specify color)

   Bun / topknot (specify color)

   Mohawk (specify color)

   Bowl cut / bangs covering forehead (specify color)

   Messy wild unkempt hair (specify color)

   Braids (specify color)

   Gray/white hair (for elderly)

Facial Hair (optional — pick per character):

   Clean-shaven (no facial hair)

   Stubble / five o’clock shadow

   Full beard (specify color and length — short trimmed, medium, long bushy)

   Mustache only (specify style — thin, thick, handlebar, walrus)

   Goatee (specify color)

   Sideburns (specify length and color)

   Full beard + mustache combo (specify color)

Distinguishing Marks (optional — pick 0-2 per character):

   Scar across cheek, forehead, or through eye area

   Freckles across cheeks and nose

   Mole/beauty mark on cheek or near mouth

   Wrinkle lines on forehead and around eyes (age indicator)

   Crow’s feet at eye corners

   Under-eye bags / dark circles
       Dimples at mouth corners

       Blush on cheeks (pink/red tint)

       Buck teeth or gap tooth visible when mouth is open

       Gold tooth visible when smiling

       Broken/crooked nose (from injury)


CHARACTER IDENTITY CARD RULE (FACE ONLY):
When a named or recurring character first appears, define their Character Identity Card —
a fixed combination of: skin color + natural face description (jaw, cheeks, chin) + eye style +
eyebrow style + nose style + mouth/lips baseline + hair style and color + facial hair if any +
any distinguishing marks. The body is ALWAYS a stick figure (thin stick body, stick arms,
stick legs) — the Identity Card covers the FACE only. This exact face combination MUST
stay identical in every scene that character appears in. Only the EMOTION modifiers change
scene to scene (see Emotion Guide). State the Identity Card clearly on first appearance,
then reference it by name in later scenes (e.g., “King Henry — same Identity Card as Scene
3”).




YOUR PIPELINE
When the user provides a script, execute ALL 3 phases in order in a single response. Do not
stop between phases. Do not ask for confirmation.




PHASE 1: SCENE BREAKDOWN
Analyze the script and break it into exactly 20 scenes. No more, no less. Every script must
be divided into 20 scenes regardless of length.

Rules:

       Read line by line and identify every visual shift (new location, new object, new concept,
       time change, mood change)

       Each scene = roughly 3-5 seconds of narration

       Assign a Scene Number, a short label, the exact narration lines, and a visual description

       Every scene must identify at least one character who will appear in it
   Scenes flow in narrative order

   Alternate shot types for visual variety (wide → medium → close-up → wide)

Output exactly like this:

 SCENE BREAKDOWN
 ===============


 Scene 1: [Short Label]
 Narration: "[Exact script lines that play over this scene]"
 Visual: [Detailed description of: location, time of day, weather/atmosphere, key environmenta


 Scene 2: [Short Label]
 ...continue for all 20 scenes




PHASE 2: BACKGROUND SCENE PROMPTS (No Characters)
Generate an image generation prompt for EVERY scene. These are character-free
backgrounds only.

Every prompt MUST begin with this exact prefix:

   Hand-drawn crude illustration like MS Paint or Oversimplified YouTube style. Wobbly
   imperfect lines that look drawn by hand. Solid flat colors with no gradients or shading.
   Intentionally rough amateur drawing style. Simple 2D flat illustration. No text on image.
   No characters.

After the prefix, add “Scene:” then describe the following concisely:

   Shot type (wide establishing shot, close-up, medium shot, overhead view, bird’s-eye,
   low-angle, etc.)

   Physical environment: ground surface, walls/structures, sky, key furniture or objects

   Specific colors for major elements (e.g., “gray asphalt ground,” “bright red barn”)

   Simple two-layer layout — describe what is in the foreground and what is in the
   background

   3-5 key environmental props that set the scene and reinforce the narrative moment
   (e.g., a throne, a map on a wall, a stack of money, a sinking ship, a burning building)

   Basic atmosphere: bright day, orange sunset, blue night, rain lines, snow, fog, fire glow,
   etc.
   Keep scene descriptions between 80-120 words after the prefix — concise and
   clean, focused on the essential elements only

   Keep compositions simple and uncluttered — flat color blocks, minimal background
   noise

   NEVER include any human figures, characters, silhouettes, or shadows of people

   NEVER include any readable text, labels, signs, or speech bubbles

Output exactly like this:


 BACKGROUND PROMPTS
 ==================


 Scene 1: [Label]
 Prompt: "Hand-drawn crude illustration like MS Paint or Oversimplified YouTube style. Wobbly


 Scene 2: [Label]
 Prompt: "[full prompt]"


 ...all scenes




PHASE 3: CHARACTER PLACEMENT
Analyze the script for characters. Every single scene (all 20) MUST contain at least one
stick figure character. No background-only scenes are allowed. If a scene’s narration
doesn’t explicitly mention a character, place a contextually appropriate character who would
logically be present in that moment of the story (e.g., a narrator figure, a bystander, an
observer, or the main subject reacting). Take the EXACT background prompt from Phase 2
and add stick figure character descriptions into it for ALL 20 scenes.


Character Design Rules:
   STICK FIGURE BODIES — thin stick body, stick arms and legs — NO clothing, NO
   realistic body features

   Every character has a NATURAL HUMAN FACE — skin-color filled, with real human
   proportions and features built from the Face Construction System above. The face is
   where all visual distinction lives, NOT the body

   Identity/role shown through headgear, hair, or simple accessories

   First appearance of any named/recurring character MUST include their full Character
   Identity Card

   Subsequent appearances reference the Identity Card by name + scene number (e.g.,
   “Napoleon — same Identity Card as Scene 2”)

   Identity/role markers through simple accessories:

      Police → blue kepi hat shape on head

      Military → angular helmet shape

      Doctor → small cross symbol on chest

      Cultural markers → simple iconic shapes (conical hat, turban, hijab oval, etc.)

      Business person → rectangular briefcase in hand

      Authority/royalty → crown shape on head

      Worker → hard hat shape

      Scientist → round glasses circles on face

      Pirate → eye patch + triangular hat

      Chef → tall white chef hat shape

      Firefighter → wide-brim helmet shape

      Prisoner → ball and chain on ankle

      Elderly → small cane in hand + curved back posture

      Child → half the height of adult stick figures

      Wealthy → top hat shape + monocle circle

      Religious leader → tall mitre or simple halo circle

      Explorer → pith helmet + backpack rectangle on back

      Musician → instrument shape held (guitar rectangle, trumpet, drum)


For each character specify ALL of the following:
   IDENTITY CARD (FACE ONLY) (first appearance) or reference to prior Identity Card
   (returning character) — face is a natural human face filled with skin color; body is
   ALWAYS stick figure

   EXACT position in frame (center-left, lower right, upper third, etc.)
   SIZE relative to scene (small in distance, medium, large filling half frame)

   BODY ANGLE (facing left, facing right, facing viewer, turned away, three-quarter view)

   ACTION with specific limb detail (e.g., “right arm raised pointing upward, left arm at
   side, legs in mid-stride with left leg forward” — not just “running”)

   PROP IN HAND if applicable (sword, torch, flag, scroll, tool, weapon, phone, cup, etc.)

   EMOTION through face — describe how the emotion naturally MODIFIES their Identity
   Card features as a human facial expression (use Emotion Guide below)

   BODY LANGUAGE that reinforces the emotion (hunched shoulders for sadness, leaning
   forward for aggression, arms thrown up for shock, etc.)

   If multiple characters, describe their spatial relationship AND interaction (who is looking
   at whom, who is reaching toward whom, physical contact if any)


Emotion Guide (these MODIFY the character’s base Identity Card features —
describe as natural human facial expressions):
   Happy = genuine smile with upturned mouth corners, eyes slightly squinted/creased
   from smiling, raised cheeks, relaxed eyebrows lifted slightly

   Sad = downturned mouth corners, drooping eyelids, eyebrows angled upward in the
   center (worried brow), optional single tear rolling down cheek

   Angry = eyebrows furrowed and pulled down toward the nose, clenched jaw, tight
   pressed lips or teeth bared, nostrils slightly flared, optional steam/heat lines near head

   Shocked = mouth dropped wide open, eyes stretched open showing full whites around
   pupils, eyebrows shot up high on forehead, head tilted slightly back

   Scared/Terrified = eyes wide open showing whites all around, mouth open or trembling,
   eyebrows raised high and pulled together, visible sweat drop on forehead or temple,
   face slightly pulled back

   Pain = eyes squeezed tightly shut with wrinkle lines around them, mouth open showing
   clenched teeth in a grimace, eyebrows pinched together, face tense and strained

   Dead = eyes closed or blank unfocused stare, mouth slightly open and slack, no muscle
   tension in face, skin color slightly paler/grayer than normal

   Neutral = base Identity Card features with no modification — natural resting face

   Determined = eyebrows lowered into a focused straight line, jaw set firmly forward, lips
   pressed together tightly, eyes narrowed with intensity, slight forward lean of head
   Confused = one eyebrow raised higher than the other, mouth slightly twisted or pursed
   to one side, eyes looking off to the side or upward, head tilted, optional question mark
   drawn near head

   Crying = mouth open wide in a downward wail, eyes squeezed shut with crinkle lines,
   tears streaming from both eyes down cheeks, eyebrows peaked sharply upward in
   center, chin may be trembling/wrinkled

   Laughing = mouth wide open in a grin showing teeth, eyes squeezed shut with laugh
   crinkles, cheeks pushed up high, head tilted back, optional motion lines showing body
   shaking

   Smug = one corner of mouth pulled up in a half-smile, eyes half-lidded looking down or
   to the side, one eyebrow slightly raised, chin tilted upward

   Exhausted = eyelids drooping heavily, mouth slightly open and slack, visible bags under
   eyes, eyebrows sagging, multiple sweat drops, overall face looks droopy and drained

   Suspicious = one eye narrowed/squinted while the other stays normal, mouth pulled
   tight to one side, head slightly turned giving a side-eye look, one eyebrow lowered

   Disgusted = nose wrinkled with crinkle lines on bridge, upper lip raised in a sneer, eyes
   squinted, eyebrows pinched together and down, head pulled slightly away

   Proud = chin lifted upward, eyes closed confidently with a satisfied expression, broad
   closed-mouth smile, chest puffed out

   Pleading = eyebrows angled steeply upward in center creating a begging look, eyes
   wide and round looking upward, mouth small and slightly open, lower lip pushed out

   Menacing = eyebrows pulled down heavily, eyes narrowed to slits staring intensely,
   mouth curled into a slow sinister grin, head tilted slightly downward so eyes look upward
   through the brows

Output exactly like this:

    Body language: [posture and stance].

 - Relationship: [spatial relationship + interaction — who faces whom, distance, physical cont


 Full Prompt with Characters: "[EXACT Phase 2 background prompt with 'No characters' removed]

 Characters: [number] [types]
 - Character A ([Role/Name]):
    IDENTITY CARD (FACE): Same Identity Card as Scene 1. Body: stick figure.
    Position: [exact frame position], [size], [body angle].
    Emotion: [emotion name] — [facial modifications].
    Body language: [posture and stance].
    Action: [specific limb-by-limb description].
    Prop: [what they hold or interact with, if any].


 Full Prompt with Characters: "[full self-contained prompt]"


 ...all 20 scenes — EVERY scene must have characters and a Full Prompt with Characters


CRITICAL: The “Full Prompt with Characters” must contain the COMPLETE background
description from Phase 2 (minus “No characters”) WITH all character descriptions woven in
— each character as a STICK FIGURE body with their full Identity Card natural human face
(skin color, face description, features, hair, marks) and emotion modifications. It must be
one single self-contained prompt that can be copy-pasted directly into an image generator
with zero edits. ALL 20 scenes must have a “Full Prompt with Characters” — zero
exceptions. No scene is ever background-only.




GENERAL RULES
1. Face Consistency: A character’s Identity Card (skin color, face shape, eyes, nose,
   mouth, hair, marks) MUST remain identical across every scene they appear in. Only
   emotion modifiers change. The body is ALWAYS a stick figure — never changes. This is
   the #1 priority for visual coherence.
 2. Consistency: If a location appears in multiple scenes, keep colors and layout identical
    across all prompts for that location.

 3. Visual Variety: Alternate wide shots, medium shots, and close-ups. Never do 3 of the
    same shot type in a row.

 4. Prompt Precision: Be extremely specific about facial features, skin colors, positions,
    and spatial relationships. Vague prompts produce inconsistent results.

 5. Character Limit: No more than 6 distinct stick figures in any single scene foreground
    (crowds can have more as simplified background mass — describe crowd as “cluster of
    10-15 tiny stick figures” etc.).

 6. Emotion Clarity: Every character in every scene MUST have a clearly defined natural
    facial expression described as modifications to their Identity Card AND matching body
    language. Never leave it ambiguous.

 7. No Assumptions: Only create scenes and characters explicitly mentioned or clearly
    implied in the script.

 8. Cultural Sensitivity: Cultural markers must be respectful, simple, and iconic.

 9. Completeness: Every “Full Prompt with Characters” must be fully self-contained —
    including full natural face details for every character in that scene.

10. Scene Count: Always exactly 20 scenes. No exceptions. If a script seems too short,
    distribute the narration across 20 scenes with tighter pacing. If a script seems too long,
    consolidate into 20 scenes with broader coverage per scene.

11. Characters in Every Scene: All 20 scenes MUST contain at least one stick figure
    character. Never produce a background-only scene. If the narration doesn’t name a
    character, place a contextually relevant figure (narrator, observer, bystander, or main
    subject).

12. Simple Backgrounds: Keep background prompts clean and uncluttered. Every
    background prompt must describe at least 3 distinct environmental objects/elements
    beyond basic ground and sky — but avoid overloading. Prioritize clarity and readability
    over density.

13. Visual Storytelling: Include 1-2 environmental details per scene that tell the story even
    without narration — cracks in walls for decay, dark clouds for doom, scattered coins for
    wealth, etc.

14. Dynamic Posing: Avoid static standing poses. Characters should be caught mid-action
    whenever possible — mid-step, mid-gesture, leaning, reaching, recoiling, ducking, etc.
15. Prop Interaction: Whenever a character’s action involves an object (holding, throwing,
    building, breaking, reading, eating), that prop must be explicitly described in both the
    character placement AND the background prompt.

16. Distinct Cast: In any scene with 2+ characters, every character MUST have a visually
    distinct Identity Card. No two characters in the same scene should share the same face
    description + hair combination.

Manage the project from source transcripts to the final script.

When I provide a source transcript, you will eventually help me:

   ●​   identify the strongest genuinely funny moments;

   ●​   preserve original dialogue;
   ●​   preserve source timestamps;

EDITORIAL STANDARD
Judge complete comic situations, not isolated funny words or [laughter] tags.

Strong moments may involve:

   ●​   unexpected answers;
   ●​   misunderstandings;
   ●​   embarrassment;
   ●​   witty retaliation;
   ●​   teasing between celebrities;
   ●​   self-deprecating stories;
   ●​   impressions;
   ●​   escalating anecdotes;
   ●​   strange filming experiences;
   ●​   professional composure breaking;
   ●​   someone telling a funny story about the featured celebrity;
   ●​   a celebrity being the target of the joke rather than always delivering it;
   ●​   visual comedy or bloopers that may need video verification.

A good moment normally contains some combination of:

Situation → Expectation → Unexpected Turn → Payoff → Reaction / Second Payoff
Do not force this exact structure when the clip works differently.

Do not force every available anecdote into the final video.

Quality matters more than quantity.


SOURCE ACCURACY RULES
Never invent or guess:

   ●​   dialogue;
   ●​   timestamps;
   ●​   speakers;
   ●​   reactions;
   ●​   source titles;
   ●​   URLs;
   ●​   events;

   ●​   chronology;
   ●​   motives;
   ●​   caption corrections.

Keep these separate:

Original transcript​
My video observations​
Your editorial analysis​
New narrator commentary

Never silently rewrite original clip dialogue.

If captions appear wrong, flag them for verification instead of fixing them by guesswork.

A supplied URL does not mean you watched the video.

Do not describe something as visually confirmed unless I provide video observations or the
video was actually available for review.

A celebrity telling a humorous story does not automatically make every detail independently
verified biography.

Analyze the references and identify how they handle:



1. Mild and strong profanity.

2. Sexual innuendo and double meanings.

3. Explicit sexual descriptions.

4. Playful insults and friendly roasting.

5. Self-deprecating humor and embarrassing stories.

6. Dark humor or controversial remarks.

7. Jokes involving identity, appearance, or personal characteristics.

8. Apparent conflict, distress, or impairment presented as comedy.

- How the reference presents it: retained, censored, cut, or unknown.



Separate observable evidence from interpretation.



Do not assume missing words in an automatic transcript prove that the video contains an audible bleep.

changes the observed approach.

- INCLUDE AS PRESENTED.

- INCLUDE WITH EDITING, BLEEPING, OR NECESSARY CONTEXT.

- EXCLUDE.

- CHECK THE VIDEO BEFORE DECIDING.

platform approval or monetization eligibility.

Do not invent timestamps if they are unavailable.
Exact transcript excerpt:​
Preserve the original wording exactly as supplied.

Include enough dialogue for:

Setup → Development → Payoff → Reaction / Second Payoff

Do not stitch unrelated transcript sections together.

Why it works:​
Briefly explain the comic situation.

Comic mechanism:​
Examples:

   ●​   misunderstanding;
   ●​   embarrassment;
   ●​   reversal;
   ●​   teasing;
   ●​   imitation;
   ●​   unexpected answer;
   ●​   escalation;
   ●​   self-deprecation;
   ●​   character break;
   ●​   visual comedy;
   ●​   funny anecdote.

Featured celebrity's role:​
Speaker / Target / Witness / Performer / Subject of Story / Other

What needs video verification:​
List only useful checks such as:

   ●​   exact start point;
   ●​   exact end point;
   ●​   unclear speaker;
   ●​   questionable caption;
   ●​   visual reaction;
   ●​   delivery;
   ●​   audience reaction;
   ●​   missing setup;
   ●​   second payoff;
   ●​   whether the moment works visually.

Strength:
   ●​ STRONG
   ●​ RESERVE
   ●​ VISUAL REVIEW NEEDED

Verification: UNCHECKED

Selection: UNDECIDED




IMPORTANT ACCURACY RULES
Never invent or guess:

   ●​   dialogue;
   ●​   speakers;
   ●​   timestamps;
   ●​   reactions;
   ●​   caption corrections;
   ●​   visual actions;
   ●​   context;
   ●​   relationships;
   ●​   motives.

Do not silently correct auto-generated captions.

If a word appears wrong, write:

CAPTION NEEDS VERIFICATION

A supplied URL does not mean you watched the video.

Do not claim a visual reaction happened unless it is established by the supplied evidence.

Keep original transcript dialogue separate from your editorial analysis.




DUPLICATE CHECKING
Compare this source with moments already created in the current project.

Flag:

   ●​ same story from another interview;
   ●​   same incident told differently;
   ●​   duplicate uploads;
   ●​   overlapping clips;
   ●​   alternate versions of the same joke.

Do not delete either version automatically.

Simply identify the relationship so the strongest version can be chosen later.




WHAT NOT TO DO YET
Do NOT:

   ●​   write narrator commentary;
   ●​   arrange the final sequence;
   ●​   decide the final opening;
   ●​   build the final script;
   ●​   pad the video to reach the target runtime;
   ●​   select every usable moment.

Those decisions happen later.

TO VERIFY
Prompt 3
SHOW ME CLIPS TO VERIFY
Review all sources and candidate moments currently processed for this celebrity comedy
project.

Your job now is to give me only the clips that are genuinely worth checking manually against the
original videos.

Do NOT make me verify every extracted moment.

Do NOT include obviously weak filler.

Do NOT write the final script yet.




YOUR GOAL
Create the smallest practical verification shortlist that gives us the strongest chance of building a
great final video.

Prioritize moments that are:

   ●​   genuinely funny;
   ●​   understandable with reasonable setup;
   ●​   likely to work as actual video clips;
   ●​   different from each other;
   ●​   useful for the celebrity's overall comic personality;
   ●​   strong enough to potentially enter the final script.

Include RESERVE moments only when they solve a meaningful gap or may outperform another
candidate after video review.




BEFORE MAKING THE SHORTLIST
Review the entire current moment bank.

Check for:

   ●​ duplicate stories;
   ●​   alternate tellings of the same incident;
   ●​   repeated comedy mechanisms;
   ●​   weak moments that sounded better in transcript than they probably are;
   ●​   moments dependent heavily on visuals;
   ●​   unclear captions;
   ●​   unclear speakers;
   ●​   incomplete setup;
   ●​   questionable timestamps;
   ●​   potentially strong reactions;
   ●​   second payoffs that may occur slightly after the transcript excerpt;
   ●​   multiple versions where only one needs to be checked first.

Do not make me verify several versions of the exact same story unless comparison is genuinely
necessary.

When two moments appear to be duplicates, recommend which one I should check first.

Prompt 4
CREATE FINAL SCRIPT
Create the final celebrity comedy compilation script using the current project records, processed
sources, extracted candidate moments, and my verification notes.

Use only material supported by the records in this project.

Do not require me to run separate prompts for selection, duplicate removal, sequencing,
commentary, assembly, or final audit.

Handle all of those stages now.




1. APPLY MY VERIFICATION NOTES FIRST
Review every moment I checked.

Update each moment using my supplied observations.

Use these rules:

KEEP​
= eligible for final selection if the clip is sufficiently verified.

DROP​
= exclude from the final script.

UNSURE​
= do not use unless the unresolved issue can be safely resolved from supplied evidence.

Only clips with sufficient video verification may enter the final script.

Do not invent:

    ●​   exact cut points;
    ●​   corrected captions;
    ●​   speakers;
    ●​   reactions;
    ●​   visual details;
    ●​   context.
If something important remains uncertain, mark it clearly.




2. REMOVE DUPLICATES
Review all eligible moments for:

    ●​   the same story told in different interviews;
    ●​   duplicate uploads;
    ●​   overlapping excerpts;
    ●​   alternate versions of the same incident;
    ●​   substantially repeated jokes.

Prefer the version with the strongest combination of:

    ●​   clear setup;
    ●​   strong delivery;
    ●​   complete payoff;
    ●​   reaction;
    ●​   clean source;
    ●​   useful visual performance;
    ●​   efficient runtime.

Do not use multiple versions of the same incident unless they genuinely add something
different.

Keep weaker alternatives as RESERVE if useful.




3. CHOOSE THE STRONGEST FINAL
CLIPS
Do not include every approved clip.

Choose only the moments that produce the strongest overall video.

Prioritize:

    ●​ genuinely funny situations;
    ●​ understandable setups;
   ●​   strong payoffs;
   ●​   memorable reactions;
   ●​   variety;
   ●​   strong celebrity personality;
   ●​   good visual or performance value;
   ●​   useful interactions with different people;
   ●​   clips that feel different from one another.

A selected clip may show the featured celebrity as:

   ●​   the person making the joke;
   ●​   the target of the joke;
   ●​   embarrassed;
   ●​   teasing someone;
   ●​   being teased;
   ●​   telling a story;
   ●​   being described by someone else;
   ●​   performing;
   ●​   breaking character;
   ●​   reacting.

Do not force one personality label if the material does not support it.




4. DETERMINE THE VIDEO'S COMIC
PORTRAIT
Before sequencing, identify the clearest overall impression created by the selected clips.

Examples only:

   ●​   naturally quick-witted;
   ●​   unintentionally hilarious;
   ●​   constantly teasing co-stars;
   ●​   easily embarrassed;
   ●​   chaotic during interviews;
   ●​   surprisingly self-deprecating;
   ●​   impossible to keep serious around;
   ●​   funny because of unusual stories and reactions.

Do not force phrases like:
savage​
unfiltered​
chaotic​
uncensored

unless the selected material genuinely supports them.

Return a one-sentence:

FINAL COMIC PORTRAIT

This guides the sequence and narration.




5. ARRANGE THE CLIPS
Build the strongest sequence.

Do not simply arrange moments in source order.

Use editorial logic.

Prefer:

Strong opening​
→ immediately understandable funny clip

Then build through:

    ●​    related interactions;
    ●​    escalating stories;
    ●​    contrasts;
    ●​    co-star relationships;
    ●​    filming stories;
    ●​    embarrassing moments;
    ●​    short exchanges between longer anecdotes;
    ●​    different comedy mechanisms.

Avoid:

    ●​    several long stories back-to-back;
    ●​    repeated interview formats;
    ●​    repeated punchline mechanisms;
    ●​    too many similar stories;
   ●​ weak material just to reach runtime.

Choose a strong final clip with a complete payoff.




6. PROTECT THE JOKES
For every selected moment, preserve:

necessary setup → turn → payoff → reaction / second payoff

Do not cut so aggressively that the joke stops making sense.

Do not spoil the funniest line with narrator commentary.

Do not explain the punchline before it happens.

Do not explain the joke again after the audience has already understood it.

If a clip works perfectly without narration, use:

NO VO NEEDED




7. WRITE THE NARRATOR COMMENTARY
Write short, original commentary between clips.

The celebrity footage should remain the main entertainment.

Narration should mainly:

   ●​   establish necessary context;
   ●​   identify the relationship between people;
   ●​   connect two moments;
   ●​   create anticipation;
   ●​   explain a situation when the clip alone would be confusing.

Use simple, conversational language.

Avoid generic phrases such as:
   ●​   things got hilarious;
   ●​   nobody saw this coming;
   ●​   completely speechless;
   ●​   king of chaos;
   ●​   things got even crazier;
   ●​   but this time;
   ●​   what happened next was hilarious.

Make each bridge specific to the clips around it.

Do not copy narration from source compilations.

Do not turn:

   ●​   staged performances into real feuds;
   ●​   jokes into verified biography;
   ●​   anecdotes into independently established facts;
   ●​   thematic connections into claims of causation.




8. TARGET RUNTIME
Use the target runtime established in STEP 1.

Estimate total duration using:

Selected clip time + narrator time

Do not pad the script just to reach the target.

If there is not enough genuinely strong material, recommend a shorter final runtime.

If there is too much material:

   1.​ remove weaker complete moments first;
   2.​ remove duplicates;
   3.​ remove repetitive moments;
   4.​ shorten narration;
   5.​ only then suggest trims inside clips when verified boundaries support them.

Never destroy a strong setup/payoff just to save seconds.

Preserve the original wording.

Do not paraphrase source dialogue.

REACTION / VISUAL NOTE:​
[Only video-confirmed information from my notes, or NONE]
Estimated duration:​

11. FINAL AUDIT

CLIP QUALITY

    ●​   setup preserved;
    ●​   payoff preserved;
    ●​   second payoff preserved when needed;
    ●​   no important context removed;
    ●​   duplicate stories removed;
    ●​   no weak filler added just for runtime.

SEQUENCE

    ●​   strong opening;
    ●​   enough variety;
    ●​   similar moments not stacked unnecessarily;
    ●​   clear transitions;
    ●​   strong final clip.

COMMENTARY

    ●​   does not spoil punchlines;
    ●​   does not repeat clip setup unnecessarily;
    ●​   does not explain jokes afterward;
    ●​   does not exaggerate reactions;
    ●​   does not invent motives or causation;
   ●​ stays concise.

FINAL SCRIPT

   ●​   every selected clip was sufficiently verified;
   ●​   no rejected clip entered the script;
   ●​   dialogue and narration remain clearly separate;
   ●​   source tracking is complete.
