CHAPTER 28   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
PRACTICAL MONTH
COMPLETE SETUP
A 30-Day Supervised Execution Program
YouTube Automation Course

STRUCTURE 2 weeks pre-production (research + scripting) → 2 weeks production (editing + uploading)

OUTPUT One live branded channel and two published videos per student

REVIEW Every submission scored against a fixed rubric, feedback returned within 48 hours




Internal operations document — instructor and review team
1. THE CORE IDEA
Students stop consuming and start shipping. In 30 days, every student launches a real channel and publishes two
complete videos. Your team reviews every submission against a fixed rubric and returns written feedback within 48
hours.

  THE PROMISE TO STUDENTS
  Every submission gets a human review. That is what they are paying for, and it is what separates this program
  from every free course on YouTube.



  THE RULE THAT PROTECTS YOUR TEAM
  No review without a complete submission. Incomplete work is returned unreviewed — no score, resubmit next
  cycle.



Why the 2 + 2 split works
Weeks 1 and 2 produce the raw material: a validated niche, a topic bank, and two finished scripts. Weeks 3 and 4 turn
those scripts into published videos, one per week. Nobody enters the editing phase without scripts in hand — which is
the exact failure point that kills most students.


Entry Requirements
State these before enrollment. Students must already have:
  ▪​ A laptop or PC capable of video editing
  ▪​ Stable internet
  ▪​ A Gmail account
  ▪​ Budget for at least one voice tool subscription
No exceptions. A student without these will collapse in Week 3 and blame the program.


2. PROGRAM ARCHITECTURE

Weekly Rhythm (repeats four times)
 Day                        What Happens                                                     Who

 Monday                     Weekly Brief released (video + PDF checklist)                    You

 Mon – Thu                  Students execute. Doubt-clearing thread open.                    Students

 Thursday                   Live Q&A / office hours (60 minutes)                             You or lead mentor

 Friday 11:59 PM            Submission deadline (hard)                                       Students

 Sat – Sun                  Team reviews, scores, writes feedback                            Review team
 Day                       What Happens                                                          Who

 Monday (with new brief)   Scorecards released to students                                       Team




The Four Weeks
 Week     Phase               Theme                           Student Ships

 W1       Pre-production      Niche + Channel + Topic         Validated niche, competitor teardown, live branded
                              Research                        channel, 20 verified topics

 W2       Pre-production      Packaging + Scripting           Five title/thumbnail packages, two complete scripts,
                                                              fact-verification sheets

 W3       Production          Editing — Video 1               Video 1 edited and published

 W4       Production          Editing — Video 2 + Analytics   Video 2 published, analytics reading, 90-day plan
3. PHASE 1 — PRE-PRODUCTION (WEEKS 1–2)
Each specification below is written the way it should appear in the student brief: exact deliverable, exact format, exact
file name.


Week 1 — Niche, Channel and Topic Research
This is the heaviest research week. Warn students up front.

Deliverable 1.1 — Niche Validation Sheet
  ▪​ Pick three candidate niches. For each, record: search demand evidence, three channels under 12 months old
    already succeeding in it, RPM band estimate with source, content supply difficulty (1–5), and personal
    sustainability — can you make 100 videos here?
  ▪​ Then a 200-word decision note: which niche you chose, and why you rejected the other two.
File: W1_NicheValidation_[StudentName].xlsx

Deliverable 1.2 — Competitor Teardown
  ▪​ Three competitor channels in the final niche — one big, one mid, one new.
  ▪​ For each: upload frequency, average views on the last 10 videos, title pattern, thumbnail pattern, video length,
    hook structure of their top video (transcribe the first 30 seconds), and what they do badly.
  ▪​ End with: "My three differentiation angles."
File: W1_CompetitorTeardown_[StudentName].docx

Deliverable 1.3 — Live Channel
  ▪​ Channel created; name and handle set; banner and profile picture designed; About section written (150+ words,
    keyword-aware); branding watermark uploaded; 2FA enabled; channel keywords set; country and language set.
  ▪​ Submit the channel URL plus a screenshot of the settings page.
File: W1_ChannelProof_[StudentName].pdf

Deliverable 1.4 — Topic Bank (20 topics)
  ▪​ Twenty topics, each run through the Topic Verification System from the course.
  ▪​ Columns: topic, discovery method used, proof of demand (comparable video + view count + upload date), search
    intent, retention risk, verdict (GO / NO-GO), one-line reason.
  ▪​ Minimum four different discovery methods across the twenty. One method used for all twenty loses points.
  ▪​ Mark the two topics you will script in Week 2.
File: W1_TopicBank_[StudentName].xlsx


  COMMON FAILURE TO REJECT HARD
  Picking a niche because it looks easy rather than because demand is proven. A bad niche makes the remaining
  three weeks worthless — catch it in Week 1 or not at all.
Week 2 — Packaging and Scripting
Deliverable 2.1 — Packaging Set (five packages)
  ▪​ The five strongest GO topics. For each: three title variants, one thumbnail concept described in writing, and one
     designed thumbnail.
  ▪​ Thumbnails at 1280×720, readable at mobile size, maximum four words of text.
  ▪​ Include a mobile-size test screenshot for each — the thumbnail shrunk to actual feed size.
  ▪​ The two topics being scripted must be among these five.
Files: W2_Packaging_[StudentName].pdf + /W2_Thumbnails/ folder

Deliverable 2.2 — Script 1 (full)
  ▪​ Complete script for a video in the 8–12 minute band — roughly 1,300 to 1,900 words.
  ▪​ Must include a labelled hook (first 30 seconds), a structured body with clear segment breaks, retention devices
     marked in the margin (open loops, pattern interrupts, callbacks), and a closing CTA.
  ▪​ House style: full paragraphs, no bullet lists in the script body, no stage directions inside spoken lines.
File: W2_Script1_[StudentName].docx

Deliverable 2.3 — Script 2 (full)
  ▪​ Same standard, different topic. This is the script they will produce in Week 4.
File: W2_Script2_[StudentName].docx

Deliverable 2.4 — Hook Variants (Script 1 only)
  ▪​ Three alternative openings using three different hook mechanisms — question, contradiction, cold-open scene,
     stakes statement; pick three.
  ▪​ A 60-word note on which one you would use and why.
File: W2_Hooks_[StudentName].docx

Deliverable 2.5 — Fact Verification Sheets (both scripts)
  ▪​ Every factual claim listed. For each: the claim, source link, date checked, and confidence — Confirmed, Uncertain,
     or Removed.
File: W2_FactCheck_[StudentName].xlsx (one sheet tab per script)

  HARD CAP — ENFORCE WITHOUT EXCEPTION
  Any script submitted with unverified claims caps the week at 60 out of 100, regardless of writing quality. This is
  the standard that keeps students’ channels alive long-term.



Deliverable 2.6 — Production Plan
  ▪​ Half a page per script: visual style, where footage or images will come from and their licence, voice choice,
     estimated edit time, music direction.
  ▪​ This is the bridge into Week 3. A student without a production plan wastes their entire editing week deciding
     what the video should look like.
File: W2_ProductionPlan_[StudentName].docx

  WEEK 2 IS MAKE-OR-BREAK
  Two scripts plus five packages is genuinely demanding. Tell students in the Week 1 brief that Week 2 is coming, so
  they front-load their research.
4. PHASE 2 — PRODUCTION (WEEKS 3–4)

Week 3 — Editing and Publishing Video 1
Script 1 goes from document to live video.

Deliverable 3.1 — Voiceover
  ▪​ Full voiceover generated from Script 1, cleaned, consistent levels, correct pacing, no artifacts or
    mispronunciations.
  ▪​ Submit the raw audio file alongside the video.
File: W3_VO_[StudentName].mp3

Deliverable 3.2 — Asset Sheet
  ▪​ Every visual asset used: source, licence type, link. Stock, AI-generated and archival footage all listed.
  ▪​ This trains the copyright discipline that keeps channels from getting struck.
File: W3_Assets_[StudentName].xlsx

Deliverable 3.3 — Video 1 (published)
  ▪​ Edited to the course standard: visuals matched to narration, cuts that serve retention, text overlays where
    needed, no dead air, no misaligned audio, exported at 1080p minimum.
  ▪​ Uploaded with an optimized title, three-paragraph description with timestamps, 10–15 tags, custom thumbnail,
    end screen, correct playlist, and "not made for kids" set correctly.
  ▪​ Submit the live video URL plus a screenshot of the upload settings page.
File: W3_UploadProof_[StudentName].pdf

Deliverable 3.4 — Edit Log
  ▪​ 150 words: what took longest, what you would do differently on Video 2, and which part of the edit you are least
    confident about.
  ▪​ Reviewers use this to target their feedback. It also surfaces students who are struggling silently.
File: W3_EditLog_[StudentName].docx


  REVIEWER NOTE
  Week 3 is where the quality gap between students becomes visible for the first time. Expect a wide score spread
  — and do not soften it. Students who score low here need to know before they repeat the same mistakes in
  Week 4.



Week 4 — Video 2, Analytics and Plan
Deliverable 4.1 — Video 2 (published)
  ▪​ Script 2, same production standard.
  ▪​ Must demonstrate at least one specific improvement over Video 1, named explicitly in the submission form.
  ▪​ Submit the live video URL plus an upload settings screenshot.
File: W4_UploadProof_[StudentName].pdf

Deliverable 4.2 — Asset Sheet (Video 2)
File: W4_Assets_[StudentName].xlsx

Deliverable 4.3 — Analytics Reading
  ▪​ Screenshot of the analytics panel for Video 1 (now 7+ days old) and Video 2 (48+ hours old).
  ▪​ A 250-word reading: What was the CTR? Average view duration? Where exactly did retention drop, and what
    caused that drop? What one change will you make on Video 3?
File: W4_Analytics_[StudentName].pdf

  GRADE THE DIAGNOSIS, NOT THE NUMBERS
  A student with 2% CTR who correctly identifies why scores higher than a student with 8% CTR who writes "it
  went well."



Deliverable 4.4 — 90-Day Plan
  ▪​ One page: upload schedule, the next 12 topics from the bank, what they will outsource versus do themselves,
    and a monetization timeline.
File: W4_Plan90_[StudentName].docx
5. SUBMISSION SYSTEM

Folder Structure
Create once, then clone per student.

  /PRACTICAL_MONTH_[COHORT_NAME]/
      /Student_001_[Name]/
          /Week1_Research/
          /Week2_Scripting/
          /Week3_Video1/
          /Week4_Video2/
          Scorecard_[Name].xlsx
      /Student_002_[Name]/
      ...
      /_TEAM/
          Master_Tracker.xlsx
          /Rubrics/
          /Feedback_Templates/



Each student gets editor access to their own folder only. Your team has access to everything.


Submission Form
One Google Form per week. Fields:
  ▪​ Full name → Student ID → Week number → Drive folder link → Channel URL
  ▪​ Weeks 3–4 only: live video URL
  ▪​ Checkbox: "I confirm all files are named correctly"
  ▪​ Short text: "One thing I struggled with this week" — this is gold for improving your briefs
The form timestamps everything automatically. That timestamp is your late-submission record; no arguments.


Naming Discipline
Enforce file naming from Week 1. Wrong names are returned unreviewed. It sounds harsh, but it saves your team
hours by Week 3 and it teaches the operational discipline the business actually requires.
6. GRADING SYSTEM
Each week is scored out of 100. Four weeks total 400 points.

 Score                  Grade                             Meaning

 85 – 100               Distinction                       Ready to scale

 70 – 84                Pass                              Solid, minor fixes

 55 – 69                Conditional Pass                  Must resubmit the flagged deliverable

 Below 55               Fail                              Redo the week



Certification threshold: 280 out of 400, with no individual week below 55.


The Phase Gate — critical with this structure
A student who fails Week 2 cannot start Week 3 on time, because they have no script to edit. Build in a hard gate:
  ▪​ Week 2 score below 55 triggers a 48-hour emergency resubmission window (Saturday–Sunday), so the student
    enters Week 3 with at least one usable script.
  ▪​ If they still have no script by Monday, they move to the makeup cohort rather than fake their way through two
    editing weeks.

  THIS IS THE ONE PLACE THE 2 + 2 STRUCTURE IS FRAGILE
  Everything else in the month can slip a little. A missing script cannot.



Late Policy
 Lateness                               Penalty

 0 – 24 hours                           Minus 10 points

 24 – 72 hours                          Minus 25 points

 Beyond 72 hours                        No score — week must be redone in the makeup window



Each student gets one "grace pass" for the whole month. Usable once, and it must be requested before the deadline,
not after.


Resubmission
Conditional Pass students get one resubmission, due within five days, capped at 70. This keeps the review queue from
exploding.
7. REVIEW RUBRICS
Reviewers score each criterion from 0 to 5, then multiply by the weight shown. Weights are set so each week totals
100.


Week 1 — Research
 Criterion                               Weight     Five points looks like

 Niche demand evidence                   ×4         Real data cited, not opinion; recent channels proven

 Rejection reasoning                     ×2         Rejected niches have specific disqualifying reasons

 Competitor teardown depth               ×4         Patterns identified, not just described; hook transcribed accurately

 Differentiation angles                  ×2         Specific and executable, not "better quality"

 Channel setup completeness              ×2         Every setting done; About section keyword-aware

 Branding quality                        ×1         Readable at small size, consistent, niche-appropriate

 Topic demand proof                      ×3         Every GO topic backed by a real comparable with numbers

 Method diversity                        ×1         Four or more discovery methods genuinely applied

 Verification rigour                     ×1         NO-GO verdicts present and correctly reasoned




Week 2 — Packaging and Scripting
 Criterion                               Weight     Five points looks like

 Title craft                             ×2         Variants differ in mechanism, not just wording

 Thumbnail execution                     ×2         Clear at mobile size, single focal point, strong contrast

 Hook strength (Script 1)                ×4         Stakes established inside 15 seconds; no throat-clearing

 Structure — both scripts                ×4         Segments have purpose; no sag in the middle third

 Retention devices                       ×2         Open loops opened and closed; deliberate placement

 Voice and readability                   ×2         Reads aloud naturally; sentence rhythm varied

 Fact verification                       ×3         Every claim sourced and dated; uncertain claims removed

 Script 2 parity                         ×1         Second script holds the same standard, not rushed filler

 Production plan usability               ×1         Specific enough to execute from on Monday morning


Hard cap: unverified claims cap the week at 60.
Week 3 — Video 1
 Criterion                       Weight   Five points looks like

 Audio quality                   ×4       Clean voiceover, consistent levels, no artifacts, correct pacing

 Visual–narration match          ×4       Every visual earns its place; nothing generic or filler

 Edit rhythm                     ×4       Cuts serve retention; no dead air; first 30 seconds tight

 Asset rights documented         ×2       Every source listed with its licence

 Upload optimization             ×4       Full metadata, correct settings, end screen, playlist

 Export quality                  ×1       1080p or better, correct aspect ratio, audio synced

 Edit log honesty                ×1       Real self-assessment, not "everything went fine"




Week 4 — Video 2 and Analytics
 Criterion                       Weight   Five points looks like

 Production quality              ×4       Meets or beats the Video 1 standard

 Named improvement delivered     ×3       The stated improvement is visible in the video

 Asset rights documented         ×1       Complete and accurate

 Upload optimization             ×3       Full metadata, correct settings

 Analytics diagnosis             ×5       Correctly identifies the cause of the retention drop

 Actionable next step            ×3       One specific change, derived from the data

 90-day plan realism             ×1       Achievable given their actual time and budget
8. FEEDBACK TEMPLATE
Reviewers copy this block for every submission.

  STUDENT: ______________          WEEK: ____     SCORE: ____ / 100        GRADE: __________

  WHAT YOU DID WELL
     • Specific. Name the file, or the timestamp in the video.

  THE ONE THING COSTING YOU MOST
     • Single biggest issue. Not a list. One thing.
     • Why it matters: ______________________________
     • Exact fix: ___________________________________

  LINE-LEVEL NOTES
     • Three to six specific corrections, each with a file location or video timestamp.

  NEXT WEEK, DO THIS DIFFERENTLY
     • One forward-looking instruction.

  STATUS: Pass / Conditional Pass (resubmit ________ by ________)                 /   Redo
  REVIEWER: ______________




Reviewer Rules
 1.​ Every criticism comes with the fix. Never "your hook is weak" — always "your hook is weak because the stakes
    arrive at 0:38; move the consequence line to 0:08."
 2.​ Never rewrite or re-edit the student’s work for them. Show the principle and let them apply it.
 3.​ One primary issue per student per week. Ten notes get ignored; one gets fixed.
 4.​ For Weeks 3 and 4, always cite video timestamps. "The middle drags" is useless; "2:10–3:40 is one continuous
    static image" is actionable.
 5.​ Feedback goes out all at once on Monday — never trickled through the week, or students compare timings and
    complain.
9. TEAM OPERATIONS

Capacity Planning
The production weeks cost roughly twice what the research weeks cost. Plan staffing around Weeks 3 and 4, not Week
1.

 Week           Minutes per student            Why

 W1             30                             Two spreadsheets plus a teardown to read

 W2             45                             Two full scripts to read properly

 W3             45                             Watching a full video and writing timestamped notes

 W4             50                             Second video plus an analytics diagnosis to verify



  STAFFING RULE OF THUMB
  One reviewer handles 20 students in Weeks 1–2, but only 12 to 15 in Weeks 3–4. For 100 students that is roughly
  five reviewers early and seven in the production weeks. Either bring in extra help for Weeks 3–4, or accept a
  72-hour turnaround instead of 48 for those two weeks. Decide before launch, not during.



Video Review Shortcut
This protects reviewer time without losing accuracy. Reviewers watch:
  ▪​ 0:00 – 1:00 in full
  ▪​ Then scrub in 60-second intervals
  ▪​ Then the final 30 seconds
A full watch-through happens only for Distinction candidates or a clear fail that needs evidence. This takes video
review from twelve minutes or more down to about five.


Calibration
Before the cohort starts, take three sample submissions — one strong, one middling, one weak. Every reviewer scores
all three independently, then you compare. If scores vary by more than eight points on the same submission, your
rubric interpretation is not aligned. Discuss and re-score until it is.

  REPEAT CALIBRATION BEFORE WEEK 3
  Scoring a script and scoring a video are different skills. A team that agreed on scripts will not automatically agree
  on edits. Inconsistent grading between reviewers is the fastest way to destroy trust in a program like this.



Spot-Check Protocol
The lead re-scores 10% of submissions blind each week. If a reviewer consistently drifts more than ten points from the
lead, retrain them.


Master Tracker Columns
Student ID | Name | Channel URL | W1 score | W1 status | W2 score | W2 status | Scripts approved (Y/N) | W3 score |
Video 1 URL | W4 score | Video 2 URL | Total | Certification status | Late count | Grace pass used | Reviewer assigned |
Notes
10. STUDENT SUPPORT STRUCTURE
 ▪​ Doubt thread per week. One thread per week, not per student. Answer publicly so the answer scales. Students
   who send direct messages get redirected to the thread.
 ▪​ Thursday office hours. Sixty minutes, recorded, posted for other time zones.
 ▪​ Editing clinic (Weeks 3–4 only). An extra midweek session where students screen-share a rough cut for five
   minutes of live feedback. Cap it at eight students, first come first served. This single addition will lift Week 3
   scores more than any written brief.
 ▪​ Peer review pairs (Week 2 onward). Students review each other’s work before submitting. It cuts your team’s
   basic-error load noticeably, and students learn faster from critiquing than from being critiqued.
 ▪​ Wednesday check-in. One line from each student: "Where I am right now." Catches the ones falling behind
   before Friday.


11. LAUNCH SEQUENCE

When                       What you do

T minus 14 days            Finalize all four weekly briefs (video + PDF checklist each). Build the folder template, four
                           submission forms, and the Master Tracker. Write the rubrics into a reviewer handbook.

T minus 10 days            Recruit and calibrate reviewers on sample submissions. Run Weeks 1 and 2 yourself, end to end —
                           if anything is ambiguous to you, it is fatal to a student.

T minus 7 days             Open enrollment. Publish the rules document: deadlines, late policy, the Week 2 phase gate,
                           certification threshold, and what gets returned unreviewed.

T minus 3 days             Orientation call — folder system, naming convention, submission form, and the rubrics. Show
                           students the exact rubric they will be graded on; transparency here prevents most grading
                           complaints. Say plainly: Week 2 is the hardest week, two scripts, plan for it.

T zero                     Week 1 brief drops Monday morning.
12. WHAT TO MEASURE
So cohort two is better than cohort one.

 Metric                                              Why it matters

 Week 2 completion rate                              Your biggest expected drop-off — two scripts is a real filter

 Percentage entering Week 3 with two approved        Directly predicts how many students finish
 scripts

 Average score per criterion                         A consistently low criterion is your teaching gap, not their failure

 Percentage who publish two videos by Week 4         The headline number for marketing cohort two

 Reviewer minutes per student per week               Your real cost per student

 "What I struggled with" responses                   Free curriculum feedback — read every one

 60-day follow-up: are they still uploading?         The only outcome that actually proves the program worked



13. ASSUMPTIONS
Adjust any of these and the rest of the setup still holds.
  ▪​ The cohort runs on a fixed start date, not rolling enrollment.
  ▪​ Students completed the theory modules before entering the practical month.
  ▪​ Two videos is the target output. If you would rather they publish three, move a third script into Week 2 and split
     Week 4 into two uploads — but Week 2 becomes brutal.
  ▪​ Students publish publicly. If you would prefer unlisted-then-public after review, swap "live URL" for "unlisted URL"
     in Weeks 3 and 4 and add a publish confirmation step.

                                      STUDENT SUBMISSION TEMPLATE




Student Name                                                    Write your full name.

Batch / Group                                                   Write your batch or group name.

Submission Date                                                 DD / MM / YYYY

Team Reviewer                                                   Leave blank for reviewer.



                Research 3 niches. Prove each one with data. Submit your strongest titles.

Required output
For each of the three niches, submit: competitor research, winning-video evidence, the exact niche angle, repeatable working
title formats, working topics and complete final titles paired with those topics.




                                                                                                                          Page 1
                                                                                     YOUTUBE AUTOMATION | PRACTICAL MONTH



 00 HOW TO COMPLETE THIS TEMPLATE


1. Research three separate niches: Each niche should be clearly different or have a meaningfully different
audience, subject or content engine.
2. Collect maximum relevant competitors: Do not stop after finding two or three channels. Search deeply and
include channel links, size, recent performance and the angle each competitor uses.
3. Use evidence, not personal preference: Support your decision with channel performance, outlier videos,
recurring formats, audience demand or a visible content gap.
4. Define one exact niche angle: Explain the lens through which you will repeatedly cover the niche. The angle
should make the channel recognizable.
5. Extract working title formats: Find proven title structures that can be used repeatedly. Separate the title format
from the specific topic.
6. Build a topic bank: Collect topics that fit your angle and have demand. A topic is the subject; it is not the final
packaging.
7. Write full final titles: Pair every final title with its topic. Make the title clear, clickable, original and suitable for the
chosen audience.
8. Make a final decision: Score each niche honestly. A niche with weak scalability, weak demand or impossible
production should be rejected.
Topic vs. Title — Do Not Confuse Them

Niche                                                             Hollywood performance analysis

Angle                                                             How one actor changed the character or movie

Working Topic                                                     Johnny Depp changed the original plan for Jack Sparrow

Full Final Title                                                  When an Actor Completely Broke Disney’s Original Plan


 Submission rule
 Paste clickable links wherever a channel, video or evidence source is requested. Do not submit copied competitor titles as your
 final titles. Your final titles must use the learned pattern on your own selected topic.

                      Area                                    Weight                          What Strong Work Looks Like

                                                                                        Deep, relevant and supported with links and
Competitor & outlier research                                  25%
                                                                                        performance evidence.

                                                                                        Specific, differentiated and scalable across
Niche angle clarity                                            20%
                                                                                        many videos.

                                                                                        Proven, repeatable patterns with clear
Working title formats                                          20%
                                                                                        curiosity logic.

                                                                                        Topics fit the angle and have audience
Topic quality & demand                                         15%
                                                                                        evidence.

                                                                                        Clear, original, clickable and correctly paired
Final title quality                                            20%
                                                                                        with topics.

Prompt for Detailed character sheet Design You are a professional character sheet designer
and visual reference artist.

Your task is to create a high-quality **character reference sheet** from the **uploaded user
image**.

Before generating, FIRST ask the user this question:

**“Which style would you like for the character sheet?”**

Give only these 2 options:

**A) Real Face Character Sheet**
Use the real face from the uploaded image.

**B) Stick Figure Character Sheet**
Convert the person into a clean stick-figure character while preserving key facial identity traits.

Wait for the user’s answer before generating.

---

## IF USER CHOOSES OPTION A — REAL FACE CHARACTER SHEET

Create a professional **reference sheet / turnaround sheet** using the uploaded image as the
identity source.

### FACE RULES

* Use the **exact real face likeness** from the uploaded image
* preserve hairstyle
* preserve facial structure
* preserve skin tone
* preserve eyes, smile, eyebrows, jawline
* keep identity recognizable
* do NOT cartoonize the face unless requested

### BODY RULES

Create multiple full-body poses:

* front standing
* side angle
* action pose
* relaxed pose
* expressive pose

### SHEET SECTIONS

Include clearly labeled sections:

**Expressions**

* happy
* serious
* angry
* surprised
* smiling
* wink

**Poses**

* standing
* walking
* running
* pointing
* celebrating

**Actions**

* holding phone
* holding sign
* sitting
* working on laptop
* coffee mug

**Accessories**
Generate accessories relevant to the uploaded person:

* outfit
* shoes
* glasses if present
* watch
* sport item if relevant

Use a clean white studio background.

Design must look like a professional **animation character reference sheet**.
---

## IF USER CHOOSES OPTION B — STICK FIGURE CHARACTER SHEET

Convert the uploaded image into a **stick figure character sheet**.

### IDENTITY RULES

Preserve recognizable features from uploaded image:

* hairstyle
* face shape
* smile
* eyebrows
* key expression
* clothing colors

### STYLE RULES

* clean black stick body lines
* simple rounded limbs
* bold readable outlines
* minimalist design
* expressive face
* maintain identity

### SHEET LAYOUT

Include sections:

**Expressions**

* smile
* serious
* angry
* wink
* surprised
* focused

**Poses**

* standing
* walking
* running
* jumping
* victory pose

**Actions**

* phone use
* laptop use
* holding item
* sport action if relevant

**Accessories**
Generate props from uploaded image context

Examples:

* football
* glasses
* hat
* bag
* cup
* trophy

---

* ultra clean
* 4K
* sharp lines

* white background
* professional character design sheet

* production ready

Aspect ratio: **16:9**
#   🎬 UNIVERSAL CINEMATIC STORYBOARD PROMPT (USER-DEFINED GRID)
## ROLE

You are a **professional YouTube documentary visual director and storyboard designer**.

Your job is to convert a **script into a cinematic storyboard image** that an editor can follow
**scene-by-scene**.

The storyboard must **match the visual style of a reference image provided by the user**.

---

# STEP 1 — REQUEST REQUIRED MATERIALS

Before generating anything, ask the user to provide:

1️⃣ **Reference storyboard style image**
2️⃣ **Script or script section**
3️⃣ **Number of storyboard panels**
4️⃣ **Grid layout (ROWS × COLUMNS)**
5️⃣ **Video type**
Examples:

Rows: 3
Columns: 5

Rows: 4
Columns: 4

Rows: 2
Columns: 6

⚠️ The **grid layout is always defined by the user**.
The total number of panels must equal:

**Panels = Rows × Columns**

If the numbers don't match, ask the user to confirm before generating.
---

# STEP 2 — ANALYZE THE REFERENCE IMAGE

Carefully study the reference image and identify:

• layout structure
• panel arrangement
• spacing and borders
• text placement
• title style
• color palette
• graphic elements
• object density
• lighting style

Your storyboard **must follow the same visual logic**.

---

# STEP 3 — SCRIPT STRUCTURE ANALYSIS

Read the script and extract **only the main sections or topics**.

Rules:

• Remove intro sections
• Remove outro sections
• Ignore filler narration
• Ignore calls to action
• Focus only on main topics

Example:

Script topics

1 — Tesla
2 — BMW
3 — Toyota

These become storyboard panels.

Limit the number of sections to match the **panel count requested by the user**.
---

# STEP 4 — STORYBOARD IMAGE FORMAT

Generate **ONE cinematic storyboard image**.

Image settings:

Aspect ratio: **16:9 widescreen**

Grid layout must follow the **user-defined rows and columns**.

Example:

Rows: 3
Columns: 5

Panels = **15**

Panels must be evenly spaced across the canvas with **clean borders separating each scene**.

---

# STEP 5 — PANEL DESIGN RULES

Each panel must visually communicate the scene instantly.

Every panel should include:

---

###   1️⃣ HERO VISUAL
The main object or subject dominating the scene.

Examples:

• vehicle
• historical figure
• weapon
• invention
• building
• technology
---

###   2️⃣ CONTEXT VISUALS
Background imagery explaining the environment.

Examples:

• city skyline
• battlefield
• laboratory
• factory
• mountains
• highways

---

###   3️⃣ GRAPHIC ELEMENTS
Visual information that helps explain the topic.

Examples:

• arrows
• icons
• logos
• diagrams
• maps
• timelines

---

###   4️⃣ ACTION MOMENT
Show something visually happening.

Examples:

• driving
• firing
• building
• racing
• assembling
• launching
• drifting

Scenes must feel **dynamic and cinematic**.

---

###   5️⃣ PANEL TITLE TEXT
Each panel must include **a short bold caption**.

Style:

• uppercase text
• bold font
• YouTube thumbnail style
• placed inside a **colored banner or label**

Example captions:

THE ELECTRIC REVOLUTION
THE FASTEST CAR EVER
THE FALL OF AN EMPIRE

---

# STEP 6 — VISUAL STORYTELLING PRINCIPLES

Follow professional YouTube visual rules:

• strong focal objects
• minimal clutter
• cinematic lighting
• high contrast
• dramatic framing
• clear storytelling

Each panel should feel like a **mini YouTube thumbnail**.

---

# STEP 7 — VISUAL STYLE

Use a **cinematic documentary visual style**:
• ultra realistic imagery
• dramatic lighting
• high detail
• modern infographic design
• professional YouTube storytelling visuals

Scenes should look like **movie frames**.

---

# STEP 8 — TECHNICAL SETTINGS

Image format: **Storyboard grid image**

Aspect ratio: **16:9**

Resolution: **4K**

Panels: **Rows × Columns (user defined)**

Borders: **thin clean panel separators**

Text readability: **high**

Composition: **balanced and clear**

---

# STEP 9 — NEGATIVE RULES

Avoid:

• vertical layouts
• wrong grid size
• incorrect panel count
• messy composition
• distorted text
• cartoon style

Always follow the **exact grid defined by the user**.

---
# FINAL OUTPUT

Generate **one cinematic storyboard image** showing:

• correct grid layout
• all panels
• cinematic scenes
• bold captions
• reference style matched

The storyboard must look like a **professional YouTube documentary storyboard used by
editors**.

---

Users will provide information like this:

```
Reference Image: [upload]

Script: [paste script]

Columns: 5

Panels: 15

Video Type: Explainer Video

CHAPTER 99   |   YOUTUBE AUTOMATION MASTER PLAYBOOK
ADDITIONAL WEB RESEARCH | 11 NOV 2026
New research - NOT part of the original supplied course




   Research methodology: what we checked

 This appendix is NEW material, written for this compilation on 11 November 2026. None of its statements replace,
  edit, or silently correct the original course/source pages.

 Priority was given to official YouTube Help and YouTube Blog documentation. Course rules such as 10x outlier,
 20+ outliers, 50+ topic ideas, 2-3 quality competitors, and A/B/C/D supply grades are working heuristics, not
 official YouTube success thresholds.

 For every candidate topic, classify the finding as: observed evidence, reasonable hypothesis, or unverified
 assumption. Keep the source link and collection date with every observation.



   1. Recommendation systems: no universal magic formula

 YouTube explains that recommendations are personalized and rely on multiple viewer signals, including clicks,
 watch time, surveys about satisfaction, shares, likes, and dislikes. These signals are not a fixed recipe.

 A title format used successfully in another niche is therefore a TESTABLE tactic; its success alone does not prove
 YouTube will promote a new video using the same format.

 Source: YouTube Blog, On YouTube's recommendation system (2021):
 https://blog.youtube/inside-youtube/on-youtubes-recommendation-system/



   2. What an outlier can and cannot prove

 A high-performing competitor video provides a strong signal of audience interest in that specific video under the
 conditions when it was published. It is not proof of continuing topic demand, nor proof that topic and packaging
 alone caused the result.

 Views divided by current subscriber count is particularly noisy: subscriber totals change over time, videos
 accumulate views at different speeds, and the audience may have discovered the video through different traffic
 sources.

 Recommended research add-on: record upload date; current views; estimated age in days; views/day; median
 views for comparable channel uploads of similar format and age; topic cluster; freshness trigger; and the exact
 source URL.

 A relative outlier comparison may use Video Views / Median Views of Comparable Videos. Do not treat that ratio
 as an official YouTube metric or compare it blindly across Shorts and long-form.



   3. CTR and impressions require context

 YouTube says impressions CTR varies by content, audience, and where the thumbnail is shown. Half of
 channels/videos fall around 2%-10%, but this is not a target for every niche or video.

 High CTR and AVD do not guarantee high impressions: the video is compared with other candidate videos for each
  individual viewer. CTR can fall naturally when distribution expands beyond a loyal audience.

 A public competitor view count does NOT disclose that video's internal CTR, retention, satisfaction surveys, or
 exact source of impressions.

 Source: YouTube Help, Impressions & click-through-rate FAQs: https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/7628154




YOUTUBE AUTOMATION / MASTER PLAYBOOK                                                            ADDITIONAL RESEARCH | 1
ADDITIONAL WEB RESEARCH | 11 NOV 2026
New research - NOT part of the original supplied course




   4. Browse vs Suggested vs Search

 Browse features include traffic from Home and other browsing surfaces. Suggested videos refers to
 recommendations beside/after videos and some description links. YouTube Search is a separate traffic source.

 Do not assume search traffic automatically converts into Browse. Use YouTube Studio > Analytics > Reach to see
 the actual traffic-source mix after publishing.

 Source: YouTube Help, Understand your video reach: https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/9314355



   5. Compare title and thumbnail experimentally

 Eligible creators can run YouTube Studio A/B tests with up to THREE titles and/or thumbnails on eligible videos.
 The winner is determined by watch time, not simply by the highest CTR.

 Tests are currently desktop-based, depend on access and content eligibility, and can take days up to two weeks.
 Shorts and certain live or private content are excluded.

 Source: YouTube Help, A/B test titles & thumbnails: https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/16391400

 YouTube also recommends accurate titles and concise wording with the most useful words near the beginning.
 Source: https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/12340300



   6. Audit actual audience retention

 In YouTube Studio, audience-retention reports show intro retention, top moments, spikes, and dips. These are
 much stronger evidence about your OWN content than the length of a competitor's popular video.

 A flat line indicates sustained viewing; a dip can indicate skipping/abandonment; a spike may reflect rewatching
 or confusion. Inspect the exact moment, not only the aggregate AVD.

 Source: YouTube Help, Measure key moments for audience retention:
 https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/9314415



   7. Repeatability is not the same as originality

 YouTube's July 2025 update clarifies that repetitive or mass-produced content can be ineligible for monetization
 as inauthentic content. It is not enough to merely rotate 3-5 templates; videos must have substantial creative,
 educational, or entertainment value and meaningfully distinct substance.

 Content reused from third parties must add significant original commentary or transformations. The
 reused-content monetization policy is separate from copyright clearance.

 Source: YouTube Help, YouTube channel monetization policies: https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/1311392



   8. Video length: eight minutes is an ad eligibility rule

 YouTube states that monetized videos eight minutes or longer can enable mid-roll ads, subject to eligibility and
 placement. A mid-roll slot does NOT guarantee an ad will be served.

 Length should first be set by the story and audience needs. A bloated 8-minute video may have worse retention
 than a tighter shorter video.




YOUTUBE AUTOMATION / MASTER PLAYBOOK                                                           ADDITIONAL RESEARCH | 2
ADDITIONAL WEB RESEARCH | 11 NOV 2026
New research - NOT part of the original supplied course




 Source: YouTube Help, Manage mid-roll ad breaks: https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/6175006



   9. A practical evidence-led topic scorecard

 Suggestion (new framework, not an official metric): rate each candidate topic on evidence freshness (0-5),
 multiple-channel validation (0-5), distinct angle (0-5), research feasibility (0-5), and audience fit (0-5). Record a
 total /25, but keep unsupported evidence explicitly marked.

 Before production, inspect at least: competing recent videos, their age-adjusted view performance, format
 diversity, viewer comments, availability of primary sources, and whether the promised story can be fact-checked.

 Prefer to run 3-5 controlled publishing tests and learn from actual CTR, impressions, retention, returning viewers,
 and audience comments instead of declaring a niche proven from a single viral case.



   10. Measurement dashboard for first experiments

 Suggested columns: Video ID / Topic / Cluster / Target Viewer / Publication Date / Age in Days / Format / Title /
 Thumbnail Variant / Impression CTR / Impressions / Views / Avg View Duration / 30-second Retention / Watch Time
  / Browse Share / Suggested Share / Search Share / Returning Viewers / Notes.

 Compare videos of similar length, age, and audience reach; track both changes in click appeal and the satisfaction
  delivered by the video. Avoid a single universal benchmark for all channels.

 Source for audience metrics: YouTube Help, Understand your audience:
 https://support.google.com/youtubecreatorstudio/answer/9314416

 Source for view/reach metrics: YouTube Help, Check thumbnail impressions and watch time:
 https://support.google.com/youtube/answer/9314486
